### [1] [TimeCapsule: Generative Hallucination as a Method for Historical Sensemaking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24750)
*Hayk Grigorian,Hamed Yaghoobian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces TimeCapsule, a 1.2B-parameter language model trained only on 1800–1875 Victorian texts to act as a temporally isolated generator of historically grounded language and interpretations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLMs are trained on large contemporary datasets and therefore embed present-day concepts and knowledge, making them poor at authentically modeling or narrating past worldviews. Researchers in humanities and historical studies need models that reflect period-specific language, concepts, and ontologies without contamination from later ideas or terminology. This work aims to create such a temporally bounded model and to explore how its limitations can be useful for interpreting historical thought.

Method: Train a 1.2B-parameter LLaMA-style causal language model exclusively on Victorian-era texts from 1800–1875, forming an “epistemologically isolated” archive. Evaluate it quantitatively using perplexity on held-out Victorian prose, comparing to a GPT-2 baseline and to larger, modern causal models that are not temporally constrained. Qualitatively, examine its generative behavior—especially its analogical explanations of unfamiliar modern concepts—and run a hermeneutic probe with two humanities scholars who classify excerpts as human- or machine-generated to study perceived authenticity.

Result: TimeCapsule lowers perplexity on Victorian prose by 45.4% relative to a GPT-2 baseline, indicating much better modeling of that historical language. While larger contemporary models still show lower raw perplexity due to broader training, they fail to maintain temporal isolation and thus mix in anachronistic knowledge. TimeCapsule can creatively analogize modern objects using Victorian conceptual resources (e.g., calling a computer a “hypertrophied lung”), and in a classification task, humanities scholars incorrectly labeled about 40% of authentic Victorian passages as machine-generated, revealing difficulties in judging textual authenticity in this context.

Conclusion: Training an LLM strictly on period-specific data can create a temporally isolated model that better reflects the language and conceptual world of a historical era, even if it is less powerful in absolute terms than larger contemporary models. This isolation leads to productive forms of “structural ignorance”: when confronted with future concepts, the model’s hallucinations become insightful probes into the ontologies and metaphors of the past. The work also highlights a crisis of authenticity in distinguishing real historical texts from machine outputs, raising methodological questions for digital humanities and historical interpretation.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are temporally overexposed: trained on vast contemporary corpora, they encode present-day concepts that make them unreliable narrators of the past. We present TimeCapsule, a 1.2B-parameter LLaMA-style causal model trained exclusively on Victorian texts (1800-1875) as an epistemologically isolated generative archive. Quantitative evaluation shows a 45.4% perplexity reduction over a GPT-2 baseline on held-out Victorian prose, while larger contemporary causal models achieve lower raw perplexity through broader pretraining but lack temporal isolation. TimeCapsule exhibits computational sensemaking, generating historically plausible analogical explanations for unfamiliar modern concepts (e.g., describing a computer as a "hypertrophied lung"). A qualitative hermeneutic probe with two humanities scholars revealed a crisis of authenticity, as both misclassified approximately 40% of genuine Victorian excerpts as machine-produced. We argue that structural ignorance of the future transforms hallucinations into interpretive probes of nineteenth-century ontologies.

</details>


### [2] [Measuring and Improving Behavioral Consistency in Large Language Models through Fact-Heuristic-Emotion State Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24765)
*Gi-Hun Lee,Joong Yull Park*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Cognitive Kernel Model (CKM), a prompting framework that enforces explicit separation of facts, heuristics, and emotions in LLM decision-making, and shows it significantly improves behavioral consistency (but not correctness) across many models and scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often give inconsistent answers to the same decision problem, and can even reverse their own prior decisions when these are fed back as context. This instability is problematic for using LLMs in decision-support roles, yet we lack lightweight, model-agnostic methods to measure and reduce such variability without retraining or fine-tuning model weights. The authors aim to define and quantify behavioral consistency and explore whether prompt-level structure can make decisions more stable.

Method: They introduce the Cognitive Kernel Model (CKM), a structured prompting and state-tracking layer that requires the model to classify input information into three epistemic roles: Fact (verifiable), Heuristic (assumptions/inferences), and Emotion (value or priority signals). CKM maintains a state S_t = {F_t, H_t, E_t} updated over turns, then uses that state to generate decisions. They evaluate CKM on a large Korean-language decision-making benchmark involving ambiguity, ethics, resource allocation, and error handling, testing 26 LLMs from four vendors over 37,403 instances. The study includes four main experiments, a 4-arm ablation to test structural components, a 5-arm sham-restriction ablation to control for format effects, and a temperature probe to assess robustness under increased sampling stochasticity. Effect sizes are reported via random-effects Hedges’ g.

Result: CKM substantially reduces output variability across repeated runs, with a large effect size (g=1.09) over 31 model pairs. Maintaining explicit state across turns cuts decision-flip rates by 82% in newer models (g=1.52). Experiments show that gains are not due merely to JSON or formatting, as a value-only recomputation still yields strong effects (g=2.24). Under fixed anchor states, intrinsic randomness is minimal, but CKM’s advantage grows as sampling temperature increases (g=2.87 at 0.7), indicating improved robustness under stochastic decoding. Sham ablations suggest roughly 45% of the improvement comes from structural scaffolding and 55% from Fact/Heuristic/Emotion content, with CKM uniquely both increasing response consistency and reducing decision flips among all arms tested. CKM does not improve factual or logical correctness of reasoning.

Conclusion: Behavioral consistency in LLMs—how stably they make decisions given the same problem—is a measurable property that varies by model and can be improved without modifying model weights. Forcing a model to explicitly separate facts, assumptions, and evaluative considerations before answering yields significantly more stable decisions, fewer decision reversals, and greater robustness to sampling noise, though it does not enhance correctness. CKM thus represents a practical, model-agnostic state-enforcement and prompting strategy for applications where decision stability matters more than raw reasoning accuracy.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can give different answers to the same decision problem across runs, and reverse a decision when their own prior answer returns as context. We ask whether this instability can be measured and partially reduced without changing model weights.
  We test the Cognitive Kernel Model (CKM), a prompt-level state-enforcement layer. Before deciding, the model must separate its input into three epistemic roles: Fact (given or verifiable), Heuristic (inferred or assumed), and Emotion (evaluative or priority signal). CKM adds no capability; it forces the model to track what kind of information it uses before acting. Formally it maintains a structured state S_t = {F_t, H_t, E_t} updated by a transition function.
  We evaluate CKM on Korean-language decision scenarios (ambiguity, ethical conflict, resource allocation, error handling) across 26 LLMs from four vendors and 37,403 observations, via four core experiments, a 4-arm ablation, a 5-arm sham-restriction ablation, and a temperature probe. Findings:
  (1) CKM reduces repeated-output variability (random-effects Hedges' g=1.09, 95% CI [0.83, 1.35], 31 model pairs);
  (2) state persistence cuts the decision-flip rate by 82% in newer models (g=1.52);
  (3) the effect is not JSON formatting alone (value-only recomputation, g=2.24);
  (4) intrinsic randomness under fixed anchor states is negligible;
  (5) the advantage grows under sampling stochasticity (g=2.87 at temperature 0.7);
  (6) a sham ablation attributes about 45% of the gain to structural scaffolding and 55% to Fact/Heuristic/Emotion content, and CKM is the only arm that both raises consistency and reduces flipping.
  CKM does not improve reasoning correctness. The narrower result: behavioral consistency is measurable, varies across models, and is partially improvable by forcing models to separate facts, assumptions, and evaluative signals before deciding.

</details>


### [3] [Neuromorphic Diffusion Language Models: Addressing Compute and Memory Bottlenecks via Sparsity and Block Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24841)
*Dengyu Wu,Clement Ruah,Jiechen Chen,Bipin Rajendran,Osvaldo Simeone*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes neuromorphic masked diffusion language models (N-MDLMs) that combine block diffusion with spike-based neuromorphic computation to improve throughput and energy efficiency over autoregressive LLMs, even on compute-bound platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLMs are inefficient at inference because each token generation requires reading all model parameters, causing low operational intensity and high energy use. Masked diffusion language models help by generating multiple tokens per parameter access, but their benefits are mostly limited to memory-bound regimes and do not extend well to compute-bound platforms with large on-chip memory. There is a need to further boost inference efficiency across modern hardware architectures.

Method: The authors introduce neuromorphic MDLMs (N-MDLMs), which integrate block diffusion generation with spike-based neuromorphic computation. Block diffusion allows generating multiple tokens per parameter access, improving token throughput. Neuromorphic spiking induces activation sparsity, allowing the system to skip inactive channels and thereby reducing effective parameter traffic and computations. They also construct a token-level, roofline-inspired performance model to analyze how block-parallel generation and spike sparsity jointly affect decoding efficiency across hardware regimes.

Result: On machine translation benchmarks, N-MDLMs show significant gains in both energy efficiency and throughput compared to baselines. Crucially, these gains appear even on compute-bound platforms where standard MDLMs do not provide advantages over conventional autoregressive LLMs, demonstrating that spike-induced sparsity successfully extends the efficiency benefits of diffusion-style decoding beyond memory-bound settings.

Conclusion: N-MDLMs, by combining block diffusion with spike-based neuromorphic sparsity, deliver more efficient language model inference than autoregressive LLMs and conventional MDLMs. The proposed roofline-style model explains how token-level block parallelism and spiking-induced sparsity interact to improve decoding efficiency in both memory-bound and compute-bound environments. This establishes N-MDLMs as a promising direction for scalable, energy-efficient deployment of large language models on modern hardware with substantial on-chip memory.

Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) large language models (LLMs) are inherently inefficient at inference time because each generated token requires accessing the full set of model parameters, leading to low operational intensity and high energy consumption. Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) partially address this limitation for memory-bound settings by allowing multiple tokens to be generated per parameter access. In order to further enhance inference efficiency on modern platforms with extensive in-chip memory, this work proposes neuromorphic MDLMs (N-MDLMs), which integrate block diffusion with spike-based neuromorphic computation to jointly improve throughput and energy efficiency. While block diffusion increases token throughput by producing multiple tokens per parameter access, spike-induced sparsity reduces effective parameter traffic and computations by skipping inactive channels. To analyze the synergistic effect of sparsity and diffusion, we develop a token-level roofline-inspired model that captures the combined impact of block-parallel generation and spike sparsity on decoding efficiency. Experimental results on translation tasks show that, thanks to spike-induced sparsity, N-MDLMs achieve substantial improvements in energy efficiency and throughput even in compute-bound platforms for which MDLMs would fail to improve over AR-LLMs.

</details>


### [4] [Research Report on Noise-Shaped One-Bit Coefficients in Discrete Polynomial Fourier Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24868)
*Shengquan Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes approximation errors when using noise-shaped one-bit (Sigma-Delta) quantization in normalized discrete polynomial Fourier extensions, proving sharp convergence rates under various smoothness and structural assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how accurately one-bit, noise-shaped (Sigma-Delta) quantization can approximate continuous signals when used in discrete polynomial Fourier extensions, and to rigorously quantify how the quantization structure and signal regularity affect convergence rates and stability.

Method: Model the first- and higher-order Sigma-Delta quantization error as discrete derivatives of a bounded state sequence, then apply discrete summation by parts and finite-record identities (with endpoint traces) to derive variation-based error estimates. Develop explicit bounds depending on phase functions such as a parabolic phase, and use tools including exact L2 orthogonality relations, fourth-moment formulas, local kernel estimates, and oscillatory integral/transfer bounds. Extend the framework to higher-order quantizers, polynomial phases, multidimensional parameter sets, large observation windows, and correlated state models, often using compatibility or boundary-correction conditions to retain high-order decay.

Result: For first-order Sigma-Delta quantization, the error can be written as a discrete derivative of a bounded state, yielding variation estimates and an O(N^{-1}) approximation rate on compact parameter sets; for the parabolic phase, this rate is quantified through an explicit functional J(x,t) and shown to be sharp. For rth-order noise-shaped errors, one obtains O(N^{-r}) decay for sufficiently smooth weights and O(N^{-(r-1+α)}) for C^{r-1,α} weights, given endpoint compatibility or suitable boundary corrections. Additional identities and estimates (L2 orthogonality, fourth-moment, kernel, and oscillatory transfer bounds) are established, and the theory is generalized to more complex phases, higher dimensions, expanding regions, and correlated states.

Conclusion: Noise-shaped one-bit Sigma-Delta quantization, when analyzed via discrete polynomial Fourier extensions, admits precise and often optimal convergence rates that depend on the quantizer order, signal smoothness, and boundary compatibility conditions. The framework developed in the paper provides sharp, extensible error bounds and structural identities that apply beyond simple phases and dimensions, demonstrating that carefully structured one-bit quantization can achieve high-order accuracy in a broad range of settings.

Abstract: This report studies noise-shaped one-bit coefficients in normalized discrete polynomial Fourier extension. For first-order Sigma-Delta quantization, the error is written as $e_k=u_k-q_k=Δv_k$ with a uniformly bounded state. Discrete summation by parts then yields variation estimates for complex weights and an $O(N^{-1})$ approximation rate on compact parameter sets. For the parabolic phase $φ_{x,t}(ξ)=xξ+tξ^2$, the bound is expressed through $J(x,t)=\int_0^1 |x+2tξ|dξ$, and the uniform $N^{-1}$ rate is shown to be sharp over the admissible input class. Higher-order finite-record identities are derived with all endpoint traces retained. Under endpoint compatibility, or after explicit boundary correction, an $r$th-order noise-shaped error $e=Δ^r v$ gives $O(N^{-r})$ decay for sufficiently smooth weights and $O(N^{-(r-1+α)})$ decay for $C^{r-1,α}$ weights. Exact $L^2$ orthogonality identities, fourth-moment formulas, local kernel estimates, and oscillatory transfer bounds are also established. Extensions to polynomial phases, multidimensional parameter families, growing observation regions, and correlated state models are included.

</details>


### [5] [CogArena: A Multimethod Evaluation of Cognitive Ability Structure in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24999)
*Dengzhe Hou,Lingyu Jiang,Fangzhou Lin,Kazunori D Yamada*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces CogArena, a 13-paradigm benchmark to test whether LLM “cognitive abilities” form stable, multidimensional profiles; it finds only a modest general factor and weak, fragile evidence for five distinct abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers often summarize large language models’ performance using profiles of supposed cognitive abilities (e.g., memory, reasoning, planning), implicitly assuming that these are real, stable dimensions that generalize across tasks and models. There is a need for a principled framework to test when such dimensional labels are justified, rather than over-interpreting noisy or task-specific score patterns.

Method: The authors build CogArena, a procedurally generated benchmark consisting of 13 paradigms organized into five theory-driven groupings of cognitive abilities. They evaluate 55 open-weight LLMs to study covariance among paradigms and to identify common performance factors. They then run a “frozen,” fully crossed experiment on 12 models from six families, applying targeted prompting scaffolds intended to selectively enhance specific groupings. They assess whether these scaffolds produce selective benefits, survive multiple-comparison correction, and improve prediction for held-out model families. A post-hoc replication using alternate task wordings is also conducted.

Result: Across 55 models, nearly all task correlations are positive, and a single common factor explains about half of the performance variance, suggesting a dominant general-performance axis. The expected advantage for tasks within the same theoretical grouping is small, depends on scoring choices, and is inconsistent across model families. In the frozen, fully crossed scaffold experiment, targeted prompts yield only a small advantage on matched groupings; no specific scaffold effect remains significant after correcting for multiple tests, and the selectivity of scaffolds does not improve prediction accuracy on held-out model families. The preregistered confirmation criterion is not met. A post-hoc replication with alternative wordings yields an even smaller positive effect and again fails to meet the criterion.

Conclusion: The evidence does not support strong, stable five-dimensional cognitive profiles for current LLMs. Instead, performance is dominated by a general factor, with only weak, fragile hints of theory-aligned dimensional structure. The authors advocate a “boundary conclusion”: while theory-aligned prompting produces a modest tendency for models to do relatively better on matched tasks, the data do not justify attaching firm five-dimensional cognitive labels to model scores. CogArena is proposed as a rigorous workflow that integrates behavioral patterns, covariance analysis, targeted interventions, and out-of-family generalization tests before assigning cognitive interpretations to LLM performance dimensions.

Abstract: LLM cognitive scores are increasingly summarized as per-ability profiles whose dimensions should converge across tasks, respond selectively to matched interventions, and generalize beyond the models used to define them. We introduce CogArena, a procedurally generated 13-paradigm benchmark built around a multimethod framework for determining when cognitive-task scores warrant dimensional labels across five theory-motivated groupings. Across 55 open-weight models, nearly all paradigm correlations are positive and a common axis explains about half the variance. The within-grouping advantage is small, scoring-sensitive, and uncertain across model families. In a separately frozen, fully crossed study across 12 models from six families, targeted scaffolds show a small matched-grouping advantage, but no scaffold-specific contrast survives multiplicity correction and selectivity does not improve held-out-family prediction. The frozen confirmation criterion fails. A post-hoc alternate-wording replication produces a smaller positive estimate and again fails. Together, these results support a boundary conclusion. Theory-aligned prompting produces a small in-battery diagonal tendency, but the present evidence does not establish stable five-dimensional profiles. CogArena provides a workflow joining behavioral signatures, covariance, matched interventions, and out-of-family prediction before cognitive labels are attached to model scores.

</details>


### [6] [DS@GT ARC at CheckThat! 2026: LLM-Based Trace Ranking and Grouped Reward Modeling for Multilingual Numerical Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25069)
*Sagnik Sinha,Shreyas Shrestha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents and compares two systems for automatically verifying numerical claims using reasoning traces from large language models in English and Arabic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual fact-checking of numerical claims is difficult and slow because it requires both understanding natural language and doing quantitative reasoning; the authors want automated methods that can assess the correctness of LLM-generated reasoning traces and final verdicts for such claims.

Method: They participate in CLEF 2026 CheckThat! Task 2 and design two main approaches: (1) an LLM-based verifier fine-tuned with LoRA to perform binary classification on each reasoning trace, using Best-of-N selection to pick the final verdict, with an additional experiment on adaptive sub-claim decomposition; (2) a lightweight TF-IDF reward model with custom numeric and temporal overlap features, which scores traces and aggregates them by verdict groups. For Arabic, they test both a multilingual model and AraBERT.

Result: The LLM-based verifier generally outperforms the lightweight reward model on most evaluation metrics, especially Recall@5, but the reward model is better on the Conflicting class. Sub-claim decomposition does not yield gains and appears to introduce noise. For Arabic data, AraBERT performs better than the general multilingual model on most metrics.

Conclusion: LLM-based verification with fine-tuning is more effective overall than a simple reward model for ranking reasoning traces and predicting verdicts on numerical claims, though lighter models can be competitive for specific categories like Conflicting. Decomposing claims into sub-claims is not necessarily beneficial, and language-specific models such as AraBERT are advantageous for Arabic numerical claim verification.

Abstract: Automated verification of numerical claims is a challenging problem, as it requires both language understanding and quantitative reasoning. This paper describes our system for CLEF 2026 CheckThat! Task 2, which focuses on ranking reasoning traces generated by large language models (LLMs) and predicting a final verdict for numerical claims in English and Arabic. We explore two approaches. The first approach fine-tunes an LLM-based verifier using LoRA to score each reasoning trace independently as a binary classification problem, and selects the final verdict using Best-of-N selection. We further experiment with adaptive sub-claim decomposition to break complex claims into simpler parts before verification. The second approach uses a lightweight TF-IDF reward model with handcrafted numeric and temporal overlap features to score traces, and aggregates scores by verdict group to determine the final prediction. For Arabic, we compare a general multilingual model against AraBERT, a language-specific model pretrained on Arabic text. Our results show that the LLM-based approach outperforms the lightweight reward model on most metrics, particularly Recall@5, while the reward-based approach shows stronger performance on the Conflicting class. Sub-claim decomposition did not improve performance, suggesting that claim splitting introduces noise rather than aiding reasoning. For Arabic, AraBERT outperforms the multilingual baseline across most metrics.

</details>


### [7] [Evaluating Communicative Belief Updates in Large Language Models via Implicature Recognition and Cancellation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25094)
*Cesare Spinoso-Di Piano,Verna Dankers,Marius Mosbach,Jackie Chi Kit Cheung*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether large language models understand unspoken beliefs conveyed by implicatures and how those beliefs are updated when implicatures are cancelled, finding that LLMs lag behind humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Successful communication with LLMs depends on their ability to track users’ unspoken beliefs and how these beliefs change. Implicatures—meanings implied but not explicitly stated—and their cancellation are central pragmatic phenomena in human language, yet it is unclear whether LLMs handle them as humans do. There is also a lack of dedicated datasets to rigorously evaluate this ability.

Method: The authors construct the first expert-annotated dataset focused on implicature cancellation, called [DatasetName]. They crowdsource human judgements about what implicatures are conveyed by utterances and whether proposed follow‑up statements successfully cancel or modify these implicatures. They then evaluate LLMs on recognizing implicatures, tracking belief updates under cancellation, and compare their performance to human judgements. They also run control experiments to test whether LLM performance relies on prior beliefs and to analyze how different types and forms of belief updates affect success rates.

Result: LLMs perform worse than humans at understanding belief updates triggered by implicature cancellation, particularly in more naturalistic contexts. Control analyses indicate that some apparent LLM successes may come from exploiting prior world knowledge or stereotypical assumptions rather than genuinely tracking the pragmatic belief update. Failures are not uniform; they vary with the type and linguistic form of the belief update.

Conclusion: Current LLMs do not yet reach human-level competence in interpreting unspoken beliefs and tracking their updates via implicature cancellation. The new dataset and analyses highlight specific weaknesses, suggesting that improving pragmatic reasoning and belief tracking is necessary for more human-like language understanding in future LLMs.

Abstract: Human language is driven by unspoken beliefs and belief updates, making these critical to model for successful communication between large language models (LLMs) and their users. In this paper, we evaluate the ability of LLMs to recognize unspoken beliefs made through implicatures and to understand their updates through implicature cancellation: the pragmatic phenomenon whereby an utterance's implied meaning is weakened or negated. We create the first expert-annotated implicature cancellation dataset, [DatasetName], crowdsourced for human judgements of implicatures and their corresponding cancellations. We find that LLM belief update understanding lags behind that of humans, especially in more naturally-occurring scenarios. Additional control experiments suggest that successes in LLM belief updates may stem in part from a reliance on prior beliefs, and that failures in belief updates may depend on their type and on their form. Overall, our study suggests that current LLMs have not yet reached human-level understanding of unspoken beliefs and belief updates. Code and data are available at https://github.com/cesare-spinoso/ImplicatureX.

</details>


### [8] [Deep Label-Wise Attentive Temporal Convolutional Networks Improve Medical Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25129)
*Muhammed Yavuz Nuzumlalı,Alexander Fabbri,Irene Li,Dragomir Radev*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a deep learning model for automatic medical coding that uses temporal convolution and label-wise attention, achieving large gains in F1 and especially recall over prior work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical coding is hard because it requires aggregating and selectively attending to information spread across long clinical notes, and each diagnosis/procedure code may depend on different parts of the text. Existing models struggle to capture long-range dependencies and code-specific evidence, leading to suboptimal accuracy and particularly low recall, which is dangerous in clinical decision support where missing codes is costly.

Method: They frame medical coding as multi-label text classification and design a deep neural network with two key components: (1) a multi-layer temporal convolutional network (TCN) to build a global representation of very long clinical documents by modeling long-range dependencies; and (2) a label-wise (label-specific) attention mechanism that, for each potential code, learns to focus on different relevant portions of the document, effectively creating a tailored view of the same note for every label.

Result: Compared to the previous state-of-the-art medical coding model, their approach substantially improves performance, achieving a 9% increase in F1 score and a 28% increase in recall, indicating it is much better at correctly identifying relevant diagnosis and procedure codes that should be assigned to a hospitalization.

Conclusion: A combination of deep temporal convolutions for long-document modeling and label-wise attention for code-specific evidence extraction is highly effective for automatic medical coding, offering markedly improved recall, which is especially valuable for clinical decision support applications where failing to assign important codes can have serious consequences.

Abstract: Medical coding is the task of assigning a set of diagnosis and procedure codes for a hospitalization using recorded notes. It requires aggregating information from different parts of the text and focus to different sections for each individual code, making it a very difficult problem even for professional human coders. We model the task as a multi-label text classification problem. To overcome the mentioned difficulties, we propose a deep neural model consisting of a multi-layer temporal convolution network (TCN) followed by label-wise attention. While multi-layer TCN helps extract a global document representation with the ability to learn relations over very long sequences, label-specific attention mechanism allows the model to focus on different aspects of the same document for each individual label. Our method achieves significantly better F-1 scores (9% increase) compared to the previous state-of-the-art model, with a remarkable increase in recall score (28% increase), which we believe is the more important metric for a clinical decision support setting.

</details>


### [9] [TabRank: Chain-of-Thought Distillation for Table Re-Rankers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25182)
*Adarsh Singh,Kushal Raj Bhandari,Jianxi Gao,Soham Dan,Vivek Gupta*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TabRank is a reasoning-based reranking framework for table retrieval that trains compact models using chain-of-thought traces from a stronger teacher to substantially improve table retrieval accuracy, including on multi-table and out-of-distribution settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-stage retrieval systems for tables depend on rerankers, yet current rerankers (sparse/dense or even standard neural ones) have limited explicit reasoning ability, especially compared to recent Large Reasoning Models with chain-of-thought, and there is a gap in methods and datasets for reasoning-focused reranking in tabular retrieval and multi-table QA.

Method: The authors construct a dataset of 6,728 chain-of-thought reasoning traces for tabular reranking on the Natural Questions Tables corpus, generated by a strong teacher model. They then train a compact student reranker with two strategies: (1) explicit CoT distillation where the student learns from the teacher’s reasoning traces, and (2) prompting the student with the teacher’s reasoning during reranking. They evaluate the resulting TabRank framework under several out-of-distribution and multi-table settings across diverse domains.

Result: TabRank consistently outperforms the base reranking model across multiple table retrieval datasets, achieving large relative gains in Acc@10: +30.5% on HybridQA, +15.2% on SQA, +52.9% on TabFact, and +13.1% on TATQA subsets of the Multi-Table QA Benchmark, and shows strong generalization to multi-table reasoning scenarios beyond its training distribution.

Conclusion: Reasoning-focused rerankers trained with chain-of-thought supervision—via distillation or conditioning on teacher traces—substantially improve table retrieval quality and generalize well to challenging multi-table and out-of-distribution QA settings, demonstrating that explicit reasoning is highly beneficial for tabular retrieval systems.

Abstract: The ability to retrieve relevant tables for answering questions is a key task for structured information retrieval. Multi-stage retrieval systems rely heavily on rerankers to refine candidate lists produced by efficient first-stage retrievers. As a result, neural rerankers and LLM-based reranking methods have become increasingly important due to their superior capacity for semantic understanding and reasoning compared to conventional sparse or dense retrieval models. Recently, Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) equipped with explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning have shown strong improvements in ranking quality in unstructured passage retrieval. In this work, we present TabRank, a framework for training reasoning rerankers for Tabular Retrieval. We first present a comprehensive dataset of 6728 reasoning traces for tabular reranking on the Natural Questions Tables dataset. We then explore two variants of training a compact reasoning model on these reasoning traces: explicit CoT distillation and conditioning the student reranker on the teacher's reasoning trace within the prompt. We stress-test TabRank on several out-of-distribution generalization settings on diverse domains and multi-table scenarios. Our approach significantly improves performance across a variety of table retrieval datasets, increasing Acc@10 by 30.5% on HybridQA, 15.2% on SQA, 52.9% on TabFact, and 13.1% on TATQA subsets of the Multi-Table QA Benchmark compared to the base model. Notably, TabRank generalizes effectively to multi-table reasoning. Our code, data and models are available at https://github.com/AdarshSingh7647/TabRanker

</details>


### [10] [A scaling law of contextual persistence in human language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25184)
*Elan Barenholtz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper discovers a new scaling law in human language describing how the influence of earlier context on a word’s predictability decays with distance, roughly following a 1/d pattern.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While many statistical laws of language are known for word frequencies and pairwise co-occurrence, there is no well-established quantitative law describing how the *arrangement* of words in a sequence contributes to meaning over long distances. The authors aim to isolate and measure how much ordered context at different distances improves prediction of upcoming words, and to test whether this follows a systematic, universal pattern across languages and modalities.

Method: The authors use large language models as probabilistic probes on diverse corpora. For a target word, they compare its perplexity given real preceding context up to distance d with the perplexity when the same contextual words are scrambled, so that only unordered lexical content remains. The difference defines a contextual persistence function P(d), which quantifies the unique effect of word order at distance d on predictability. They estimate how P(d) decays with distance across ten corpora in six language families and both written and spoken data. They run controls with scrambled and synthetic text and with domain-native models on genomic and protein sequences.

Result: Across all natural language corpora, the contextual persistence function P(d) decayed approximately as a power law 1/d with exponent α ≈ 1 (mean α = 1.04) and high goodness of fit (median r^2 = 0.96). This pattern did not appear in scrambled or synthetic text, and it was absent in genomic and protein sequences analyzed with appropriate models. The law was robust across independent language-model probes and data types.

Conclusion: Human language exhibits a robust scaling law of contextual persistence: the contribution of ordered context to word predictability decays roughly as 1/d with distance. An exponent near 1 implies that contextual influence is spread almost uniformly over logarithmic timescales, suggesting that language is organized so that meaningful dependencies are neither too short- nor too long-ranged, but instead scale in a specific, quantitatively simple way. This adds a new fundamental statistical regularity to our understanding of linguistic structure beyond word frequency and pairwise co-occurrence laws.

Abstract: Human language exhibits lawful structure at the level of words (frequency, vocabulary growth) and word pairs (co-occurrence across distance). Here we show that the arrangement of words in sequence -- a central determinant of meaning -- obeys a comparable law. Using large language models as probabilistic probes, we measured the reduction in target perplexity conferred by prior context at distance d beyond that of the same words scrambled; this difference, the contextual persistence function P(d), isolates the influence of arrangement. Across ten corpora spanning six language families and written and spoken modalities, P(d) decayed approximately as 1/d ($P(d) \propto d^{-α}$, mean $α= 1.04$; median $r^2 = 0.96$). The effect vanished in scrambled and synthetic controls, replicated across independent probes, and did not appear in genomic or protein sequences under domain-native models. An exponent near 1 distributes contextual influence approximately uniformly across logarithmic timescales. The results establish a scaling law of contextual persistence in human language.

</details>


### [11] [MyoCardBench: A Real-World Data Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Clinically Authentic Cardiovascular Care Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25186)
*Xiao Li,Mouxiao Bian,Zhaodi Wu,Sijie Ren,Juechen Chen,Lu Lu,Jingru Ding,Yun Zhong,Jie Xu,Yixiu Liang,Junbo Ge*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MyoCardBench is a large, real-world, multi-task benchmark to evaluate medical LLMs across the full cardiovascular care workflow, revealing performance levels and gaps in clinically important tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical LLM benchmarks mainly test exam-style knowledge or narrow, isolated tasks, which do not capture the longitudinal, multimodal, and safety-critical nature of real cardiovascular care. There is a need for a benchmark grounded in authentic clinical data and workflows to better measure how LLMs perform in realistic cardiology use cases and to guide safe deployment and improvement.

Method: The authors constructed MyoCardBench with 2,263 items aggregated from 13 task-specific datasets derived from de-identified cardiovascular records and exam data, spanning the cardiovascular care continuum. Sixteen cardiologists created annotations and reference answers, with cross-review by two senior cardiologists to ensure quality and consensus. Seven LLMs were tested in standardized zero-shot settings, generating 15,841 responses. Open-ended tasks were evaluated along two axes—key-point coverage and holistic clinical quality—while a dedicated CardioEthics task was evaluated by accuracy.

Result: Among the evaluated models, GPT-5.4 achieved the best performance with a macro-average score of 62.55 and an item-weighted mean of 62.19, followed by Gemini 3.1 Pro and Qwen 3.6 27B. GPT-5.4 led across all three evaluated dimensions. Performance varied sharply across task types: CardioAuxReport showed the highest scores (86.38), whereas CardioECGRead and CardioEthics had the lowest (17.25 and 17.34). Large discrepancies between holistic clinical quality and key-point coverage were observed especially in CardioComm, CardioEmergRescue, and CardioTreatPlan, indicating that models may hit checklists without producing fully adequate clinical responses in complex scenarios.

Conclusion: MyoCardBench establishes a large-scale, realistic, and multidimensional benchmark for evaluating LLMs in cardiology across the complete care continuum. It highlights current model strengths and weaknesses, including critical gaps in ECG interpretation, ethical reasoning, and complex communication or treatment planning. The benchmark offers a structured way to identify clinically important omissions and sets priorities for improving and safely deploying LLMs in cardiovascular practice.

Abstract: Background: Most medical large language model (LLM) benchmarks focus on examination knowledge or isolated tasks and may not reflect the longitudinal, multimodal, and safety-critical workflow of cardiovascular care. Objective: To develop MyoCardBench, a real-world benchmark spanning the cardiovascular care continuum, and assess LLM performance across clinical dimensions and specialist tasks. Methods: MyoCardBench includes 2,263 items from 13 task-specific datasets derived from de-identified cardiovascular records and examination data. Sixteen cardiology physicians conducted annotation and reference construction, followed by cross-review from two senior cardiologists. Seven LLMs generated 15,841 outputs under standardized zero-shot settings. Open-ended tasks were evaluated using key-point coverage and holistic clinical quality, while CardioEthics was scored by accuracy. Results: GPT-5.4 achieved the highest macro-average (62.55) and item-weighted mean (62.19), followed by Gemini 3.1 Pro (59.95) and Qwen 3.6 27B (59.72). GPT-5.4 ranked first in all three dimensions. CardioAuxReport performed best (86.38), whereas CardioECGRead (17.25) and CardioEthics (17.34) were lowest. The largest gaps between holistic clinical quality and key-point coverage occurred in CardioComm (52.71), CardioEmergRescue (52.05), and CardioTreatPlan (48.80). Conclusions: To our knowledge, MyoCardBench is the largest real-world, multi-task benchmark for LLM evaluation across the cardiovascular care continuum and offers the broadest coverage of clinically authentic cardiology scenarios reported to date. It provides a rigorous framework for identifying model strengths, clinically important omissions, and priorities for future development.

</details>


### [12] [A Cross-lingual Comparison of Human and Classification Model Entrainment Behavior in Code-switched Speech Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25202)
*Debasmita Bhattacharya,Siying Ding,Alayna Nguyen,Julia Hirschberg*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how people and models adapt to each other’s style (entrainment) in spoken code-switched conversations across several language pairs, and evaluates whether classification models focus on the same cues humans use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational entrainment—how speakers align in word choice, prosody, and style—is well understood in single-language or written settings, but little is known about how it works in spoken code-switching between languages. With multilingual users and code-switched speech becoming common in real-world applications, it is important to understand whether computational models capture the same entrainment patterns humans exhibit, in order to build more natural conversational agents.

Method: The authors perform a cross-lingual empirical analysis of entrainment in spoken Mandarin-English, Hindi-English, and Spanish-English dialogues. They examine lexical entrainment, acoustic-prosodic entrainment, and code-switching style entrainment. Then they train both classical machine learning classifiers and Transformer-based models to detect entrainment. Using feature importance and ablation studies, they compare which features the models rely on against the features most indicative of human entrainment behavior.

Result: They find that lexical entrainment patterns are largely consistent across the different language pairs. However, entrainment in acoustic-prosodic features and in code-switching style varies with context and language pair. Classification models can detect entrainment reasonably well overall, but they tend to prioritize features that are not the ones most salient for human entrainment, as inferred from the empirical analysis.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that human entrainment in code-switched spoken dialogue shows both generalizable and context-specific aspects, and that current classifiers, while effective, are not aligned with human-salient cues. It introduces a human-grounded framework for evaluating model decision-making in multilingual stylistic settings and highlights open challenges for building conversational agents that can produce natural, human-like code-switched speech.

Abstract: Conversational entrainment is well-studied in monolingual and written contexts, but remains underexplored in spoken code-switching (CSW). We present a novel cross-lingual analysis of entrainment in Mandarin-English, Hindi-English, and Spanish-English dialogue and show that, while lexical entrainment generalizes across language pairs, entrainment over acoustic-prosodic and CSW style aspects exhibits context-specific variation. We build on these findings by asking whether classification models capture these human behavioral patterns. Applying feature importance and ablation analyses, we find that classical and Transformer-based classifiers detect entrainment reasonably well but consistently prioritize features other than those most salient to human entraining behavior. Our approach introduces a human-grounded framework for evaluating model decision-making in multilingual stylistic contexts, and suggests future challenges for developing conversational agents capable of producing naturalistic code-switched speech.

</details>


### [13] [Interpretable Column Annotation with LLM-Symbolized Decision Process Materialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25228)
*Mengqi Wang,Jianwei Wang,Qing Liu,Xiwei Xu,Zhenchang Xing,Michael Bain,Liming Zhu,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SymCA is an LLM-powered, interpretable framework for column annotation in tables that builds a semantic label skeleton and evolves local interpretable classifiers, significantly improving Micro-F1 and Macro-F1 over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing column annotation methods rely on end-to-end neural mappings from columns to labels. These models tend to be black boxes, with low interpretability and adaptability, and they fail to fully exploit the semantic structure and relationships within the label space, which harms accuracy and robustness.

Method: SymCA reformulates column annotation as a symbolic global-to-local decision process. First, a global skeleton induction component uses LLMs to generate candidate tree-structured semantic skeletons (hypernym-style label hierarchies) and then applies Minimum Bayes Risk-based consensus to select a robust skeleton that accounts for generation variance. Second, a local substrate evolution component treats each internal node of the skeleton as an executable predictive substrate: for each node, it trains an interpretable random forest classifier based on a current operator (feature) set, uses an LLM to propose node-specific operator modifications, and applies an exploration-exploitation strategy to evolve and focus on the most promising substrates over multiple rounds.

Result: In experiments, SymCA achieves higher accuracy, robustness, and interpretability than existing column annotation baselines, with reported average gains of 6.42 percentage points in Micro-F1 and 11.03 percentage points in Macro-F1 metrics.

Conclusion: By combining LLM-generated semantic label structures with interpretable, evolvable local classifiers, SymCA delivers an accurate, robust, and transparent solution for column annotation, demonstrating that explicitly modeling label semantics and using a symbolic decision process can outperform purely neural end-to-end approaches.

Abstract: Column annotation (CA), including column type annotation (CTA) and column property annotation (CPA), aims to identify the meanings of table columns and the semantic relationships among them. Recent CA methods usually use various neural models to learn column representations and directly map them to label categories, thereby (1) sacrificing model interpretability and adaptivity, and (2) overlooking rich label semantics and ultimately limiting accuracy. To address these limitations, we propose SymCA, an LLM-empowered interpretable CA framework that materializes column annotation as a global-to-local symbolic decision process. SymCA consists of two components: (1) global skeleton induction, which constructs a semantic skeleton over the label space, and (2) local substrate evolution, which evolves predictive substrates within the skeleton. Specifically, to exploit label semantics while preserving an interpretable decision process, the global skeleton induction module leverages LLMs to generate candidate hypernym-inspired tree-structured semantic skeletons and employs a Minimum Bayes Risk (MBR)-based consensus strategy to select a robust skeleton against generation variance. Since different internal nodes require different evidence to distinguish among their child nodes, the local substrate evolution module materializes each internal node as an executable and evolvable predictive substrate. Over multiple evolution rounds, each substrate trains an interpretable random forest classifier with the current operator set, leverages the LLM to propose node-specific operator modifications, and uses an exploration-exploitation strategy to prioritize promising substrates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SymCA is accurate, robust, and interpretable, outperforming the strongest baselines by an average of 6.42% in Micro-F1 and 11.03% in Macro-F1.

</details>


### [14] [VisualPatchWorld: Code World Models as Latent Structured Representations for Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25236)
*Jiaxin Bai,Jiaxuan Xiong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes VisualPatchWorld (VPW), a framework that learns world models as executable code, combining the scalability of neural dynamics models with the interpretability and editability of physics engines for planning and control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models either use neural predictors, which learn dynamics implicitly in vector spaces but are hard to interpret or edit, or hand-built physics engines, which are explicit and interpretable but time‑consuming and hard to scale. There is a need for world models that are automatically constructed from data yet remain inspectable, editable, and directly usable for planning and simulation.

Method: VPW represents world dynamics as code. It operates in two stages: (1) it uses short, active probing interactions with the environment to select an appropriate qualitative dynamical form (i.e., a code template describing the structure of dynamics), and (2) it fits the free parameters of the selected form using recorded state–action trajectories by minimizing multi‑step prediction error. The resulting programmatic models can be rolled out like simulators, inspected in source form, connected to image-based scene graphs for live state, and plugged into a model-predictive control loop.

Result: Across benchmarks against prior code-based world models, VPW achieves 69.0% mean planning success and improves over the strongest code baseline by 23.5 percentage points. The gains are especially large in tasks where correctly identifying the qualitative dynamics is critical. When used with a shared planner, VPW’s learned models achieve performance close to a ground-truth physics engine on navigation and grasp-intensive tasks, while still lagging on contact-rich pushing. Supplementing planning by verifying a shortlist of promising plans in the ground-truth engine largely eliminates this remaining gap.

Conclusion: Automatically inducing world models as executable code from data is feasible and effective for planning. VPW offers a practical middle ground between black-box neural dynamics and manually engineered physics engines, delivering interpretable, inspectable, and planner-ready world models that approach the performance of ground-truth simulators on several control tasks. The work points toward scalable, code-based world models that can support perception, simulation, and decision-making in complex environments.

Abstract: Different research lines use the term world model in different ways, yet they share a common aim: to capture how the world evolves under action in a form that supports perception, simulation, and planning. Two prominent realizations are neural predictors that learn dynamics in continuous vector spaces, and hand-built physics engines that expose explicit state and physical laws. Neural predictors scale from data but leave the form of the dynamics implicit; physics engines are inspectable and editable but difficult to construct at scale. We introduce VisualPatchWorld (VPW), which represents world dynamics as code. VPW first selects a qualitative dynamical form with short active probes, then fits that form's free parameters from recorded state-action traces by minimizing multi-step prediction error. The resulting programs can be rolled forward like a simulator, inspected in source form, and used inside model-predictive control; image-derived scene graphs can supply the live state at replan time. Across comparisons with prior code-based world models, VPW attains 69.0% mean planning success and exceeds the strongest code baseline by 23.5 points. The largest gains arise when choosing the correct qualitative dynamics is essential. Under the same planner, the induced models approach ground-truth engine success on navigation and grasp-rich control; a residual gap remains for contact-rich pushing, and checking a shortlist of promising plans in the engine closes most of that gap. These results establish a practical route toward automatically constructed code world models that are useful for planning. Code is available at https://github.com/HKBU-KnowComp/VisualPatchWorld/.

</details>


### [15] [Where Steering Signals Come From: Activation Source Selection in Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25270)
*Jiaran Ye,Lingxu Ran,Zijun Yao,Chenpeng Wang,Yong Jiang,Lei Hou,Juanzi Li,Liangming Pan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how the choice of activation source—where and how hidden states are collected—affects activation steering in language models, finding that steering works best when using execution-boundary states and introducing a technique called tail subtraction to improve signal quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While activation steering can control language model behavior by modifying hidden states, existing work pays little attention to how the steering vectors are sourced. The authors aim to systematically study how different choices of source context and readout policies influence steering performance and to understand what properties of hidden states make them effective for control.

Method: They define activation source selection as the pairing of a source context with an activation readout policy, and then hold the downstream steering intervention fixed while varying only this source across experiments. Using three instruction-tuned models and four families of steering tasks, they measure how different source choices affect success, distinguishing between pre-realization (execution-boundary) and post-realization states. They also develop and evaluate a new procedure, tail subtraction, which subtracts shared prompt/continuation components from boundary states to isolate cleaner steering directions.

Result: They find that steering performance is highly sensitive to the choice of activation source: even with identical downstream interventions, different source activations yield substantially different success rates. Effective steering is not fully predicted by whether the target behavior appears in the source text; instead, the most informative signals arise from execution-boundary states where the model is about to generate the target behavior. Answer-based sources work when their activations align with such boundary directions. Tail subtraction further improves the stability and clarity of these steering signals by removing confounding shared semantics.

Conclusion: The study concludes that activation steering efficacy crucially depends on representations of impending model behavior captured in execution-boundary states, rather than on surface-level occurrence of the target behavior in text. By highlighting the importance of activation source selection and introducing tail subtraction to refine boundary-based signals, the paper reframes how steering vectors should be constructed and suggests more principled ways to control language models via their internal activations.

Abstract: Activation steering controls language models by adding vectors or features to hidden states at inference time, but the upstream source of these steering signals is often treated as a secondary detail. We study this source choice as activation source selection: the combination of source context and activation readout policy used to collect the hidden states from which a steering signal is built. Holding the downstream intervention fixed, we show across three instruction-tuned models and four steering task families that changing only the source activations substantially changes steering success. We further find that effective steering is not explained simply by whether the desired behavior appears in the source text. Instead, strong signals come from execution-boundary states, where the model is about to produce or continue the target behavior. This pre-/post-realization distinction explains why answer-based sources sometimes work: their useful component aligns with execution-boundary directions rather than target appearance alone. Building on this view, we introduce tail subtraction, which removes shared prompt and continuation semantics from boundary states and yields cleaner, more stable steering signals. Overall, our results suggest that steering depends on representations of what the model is about to do, not merely on what has already appeared.

</details>


### [16] [CoSA: Accelerating Long-Context Inference via Proxy-Kernel Co-Designed Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25291)
*Yufei Xue,Lin Niu,Hong Liu,Siran Liu,Hanyong Shao,Wei Liu,Guanghua Yu,Jianchen Zhu,Jun Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CoSA is a training-free, proxy–kernel co-designed sparse attention method that improves long-context LLM inference efficiency while preserving accuracy, especially under tight compute budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-attention has quadratic complexity in sequence length, making long-context inference extremely expensive. Block-sparse attention with a proxy that predicts which blocks to keep is a common workaround, but when compute or memory budgets are tight, the proxy misses important attention blocks and the attention kernel must rigidly follow this imperfect sparse mask, causing significant accuracy loss. There is a need for a method that remains accurate and efficient even under very tight budgets.

Method: The paper introduces CoSA, a two-stage, training-free sparse attention framework based on co-design of the proxy and the kernel. Stage 1 uses a Kernel-Aware Proxy (KAP) that, under a moderate budget, selects attention blocks and outputs not just a binary mask but an ordered mask that encodes the sequence in which key–value (KV) pages should be visited in the kernel. Stage 2 uses an Ordered-Skipping Kernel (OSK) that executes attention following this ordering but, during runtime, leverages online softmax statistics to decide which additional blocks can be skipped under a stricter budget, effectively pruning more computation without retraining. The method works with existing LLM backbones and long-context settings, and is applied during inference only.

Result: Across several mainstream LLM architectures and long-context benchmarks, CoSA delivers higher accuracy than existing sparse attention schemes when operating at equal or lower compute budgets. It significantly accelerates attention computation, achieving a 4.93× speedup for the attention module and a 2.53× reduction in end-to-end Time-to-First-Token for 128K context length, while keeping performance degradation negligible compared to dense attention.

Conclusion: By jointly designing the proxy and attention kernel and using an ordered mask plus runtime softmax statistics, CoSA enables more aggressive sparsification of attention under tight resource budgets without substantial accuracy loss. This training-free approach makes long-context inference substantially faster and more practical for large language models while preserving model quality.

Abstract: The quadratic cost of self-attention makes long-context inference prohibitively expensive, and proxy-based block-sparse attention has become a practical remedy. Existing methods typically rely on a proxy to predict a binary sparse mask and a kernel to consume this mask and perform sparse attention computation. Such an approach is effective under moderate budgets. However, as the budget tightens, the estimated proxy inevitably drops some salient blocks, while the kernel can only apply the sparse mask mechanically, leading to an evident drop in model accuracy. We propose CoSA, a two-stage training-free Sparse Attention under proxy-kernel CO-design, which couples a Kernel-Aware Proxy (KAP) with an Ordered-Skipping Kernel (OSK). In the first stage, the KAP selects blocks under a moderate budget and produces an ordered mask that prescribes the order in which KV pages are visited in the kernel inner loop. In the second stage, the OSK applies this mask and skips more blocks under a tightened budget given online-softmax statistics. Across mainstream LLM backbones and long-context benchmarks, CoSA attains higher accuracy at lower budgets. Impressively, CoSA achieves a 4.93$\times$ attention speedup and reduces end-to-end Time-to-First-Token by 2.53$\times$ under a context length of 128K with negligible performance degradation.

</details>


### [17] [Toward a systematic method for identifying language areas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25305)
*Hiram Ring*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a simple geographical clustering method to systematically define linguistic macroareas and local language areas, aiming to better control for contact and inheritance in typological research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In linguistic typology, researchers need to distinguish universal language properties from those arising due to contact or inheritance. Existing macroareas, used as controls for this purpose, are mostly expert-defined and continent-based, lacking a systematic and reproducible method for identifying areas of potential language contact. This creates a gap in typological methodology and risks arbitrary or theory-laden area definitions.

Method: The paper introduces a simple geographical clustering technique that can be applied to any region of the world. Using geographic locations of languages, it groups them into areas that reflect potential contact zones. This method is flexible with respect to scale, allowing both global macroareas and smaller, local groupings to be identified algorithmically rather than by expert judgment alone.

Result: Applying the clustering method yields a set of worldwide macroareas that broadly correspond to those already used in typology, validating the approach. In addition, the method identifies finer-grained local clusters in a well-known sprachbund, showing that it can capture meaningful contact areas at different scales.

Conclusion: Systematic, geography-based clustering can replace or complement expert-based definitions of macroareas in linguistic typology. The proposed method offers a reproducible way to define both global macroareas and local language areas, supporting more rigorous control for areal effects and inheritance when investigating universal vs. local properties of language.

Abstract: Macroareas are geographical areas used in typological research for grouping variables of interest. In linguistic typology, languages in a given macroarea are considered to have potential for contact, in contrast to those outside the area, where contact is less likely. Along with language family membership, macroareas are used as controls for models in linguistic typology, in an attempt to address the problem of autocorrelation - the observation that historical developments or typological patterns may be due to contact between neighboring languages and/or inheritance from a common ancestral language. Macroareas are therefore a central aspect of research that seeks to separate universal properties of language from local (or language-specific) properties. Existing macroareas largely depend on expert determinations of what constitutes a geographical area of potential contact, and to date have mainly aligned with continents or landmasses (Hammarström and Donohue 2014; Nichols, Witzlack-Makarevich, and Bickel 2013). While there are various historical and theoretical reasons for these groupings, there as of yet has been no systematic approach to identifying such areas for a given region. This paper attempts to address such a gap and move beyond macroarea to identification of language areas of relatively arbitrary size, presenting a simple geographical clustering method for identifying groupings over any area. The method produces a set of worldwide macroareas that largely align with existing groupings, as well as local groupings for a well-known sprachbund.

</details>


### [18] [CAST: Game Solvers as Turn-Level Teachers for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25308)
*Yu Wang,Yi-Kai Zhang,Wentao Shi,Ziang Ye,Yuchun Miao,Yueqing Sun,Qi Gu,Xunliang Cai,Lan-Zhe Guo,Han-Jia Ye,Fuli Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces CAST, a method that uses game solver value changes as dense turn-level rewards to better train LLMs for long-horizon games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for long-horizon games suffers from sparse final rewards, making it hard to attribute success or failure to specific decisions. Existing approaches to generate denser intermediate signals are either expensive or inaccurate, limiting their practical usefulness for training generalist decision-making LLMs.

Method: The authors propose CAST (Credit Assignment from Solver Teachers). CAST leverages a game solver’s state value estimates: by tracking how the solver’s value changes after each action, it computes a "solver advantage" that indicates whether a move improves the state. These scalar advantages are then injected as turn-level reward signals into RLVR. Under a soft-optimal solver assumption, they show that maximizing solver advantage is equivalent to on-policy distillation from the solver, but only requires scalar value outputs instead of full logits.

Result: In experiments on long-horizon puzzle and interaction tasks—Sokoban, Minesweeper, and Rush Hour—CAST outperforms all trained baselines across both in-domain and out-of-distribution (unseen difficulty) settings. Additionally, CAST achieves the best average zero-shot performance on more complex environments, ALFWorld and WebShop, indicating strong generalization of the learned decision policies.

Conclusion: Using solver-derived value changes as dense training signals effectively addresses turn-level credit assignment in RLVR for long-horizon tasks. CAST simplifies teacher usage by only requiring scalar values while still matching an on-policy distillation objective, and empirically yields superior and more generalizable performance across several games and interactive benchmarks.

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) to act in long-horizon games is a promising step toward generalist decision-making, yet reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) relies on sparse final rewards that reveal little about which decisions determine success. Denser process signals could supply this missing turn-level credit, but existing sources are hard to keep both cheap and accurate. We observe that changes in a game solver's state value reveal whether an action advances the state toward success. Building on this insight, we propose CAST (Credit Assignment from Solver Teachers), which converts these value changes into solver advantages and injects them into RLVR as turn-level signals. We further show that, under a soft-optimal solver assumption, maximizing the solver advantage is equivalent to on-policy distillation from the solver, requiring only scalar values rather than teacher logits. Across Sokoban, Minesweeper, and Rush Hour, CAST outperforms all trained baselines on every game under both in-domain and unseen-difficulty evaluation and achieves the highest average zero-shot performance on ALFWorld and WebShop. Our code is available at https://github.com/Wloner0809/CAST.

</details>


### [19] [Every Time I Hire a Linguist, Inference Costs Go Down: On Linguistic Rules as Effective Prompt Compressors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25335)
*Jianfei Ma,Zhaoxin Feng,Emmanuele Chersoni,Si Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether purely rule-based, linguistically motivated prompt compression can match LM-scoring-based methods, using evolutionary search over linguistic rules and showing comparable performance under light-to-moderate compression but degradation under heavy compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt compression reduces inference cost for LLMs, but most methods rely on expensive LM forward passes to score token importance. It is unclear whether such nuanced, model-based token scoring is really necessary, especially given that linguistics offers well-studied cues for information content that can be implemented as simple rules. The authors want to see if we can get effective, cheaper compression using only deterministic linguistic rules, eliminating LM scoring at compression time.

Method: They design a rule-based compression framework grounded in lexical, syntactic, semantic, and discourse features. Using offline evolutionary search, they explore combinations of these rules to find effective compressors. The resulting systems perform compression using only CPU-side rule application with no LM forward passes during deployment. They evaluate with a dual-path protocol that measures both direct use of compressed prompts (Direct path) and performance when the original text is reconstructed from compressed prompts (Reconstruction path), across multiple datasets such as short passages, multi-document reasoning, and dialogue-memory QA.

Result: The evolved linguistic compressors reach performance comparable to recent advanced LM-based prompt compression methods on several benchmarks. Their performance is particularly strong at light-to-moderate compression ratios, but degrades as compression becomes more aggressive. The Direct and Reconstruction evaluation paths show different behavior, indicating different sensitivities to compression. Evolutionary analysis indicates that the most effective compressors combine cues from multiple linguistic levels, and that as compression gets stronger, successful rule sets shift from fine-grained token pruning toward coarser sentence-level extraction.

Conclusion: Purely linguistic, rule-based prompt compressors—designed via evolutionary search—can serve as competitive alternatives to LM-scoring-based compression under light-to-moderate compression levels, while being cheaper at deployment because they avoid LM forward passes. However, their effectiveness declines with very aggressive compression, and the analysis suggests that good compression strategies integrate multiple linguistic signals and adapt from token-level pruning to sentence-level extraction as compression demands increase.

Abstract: Prompt compression shortens LLM input to reduce inference cost, yet existing methods score token importance through LM forward passes. It remains questionable whether such nuanced, costly token selection is necessary. Compression requires identifying informative content, a problem that linguistic research has long addressed through cues that can be operationalized as deterministic rules. We therefore ask: can \textbf{linguistic rules alone} serve as effective prompt compressors, without LM-based scoring at compression time?
  To address this, we conduct offline evolutionary search over lexical, syntactic, semantic, and discourse seeds to find competitive rule combinations. The resulting linguistic compressor requires no LM forward pass at deployment and uses only CPU-side processing for compression. We evaluate it with a dual-path protocol to balance compression quality and reconstruction fidelity.
  Across short passages, multi-document reasoning, and dialogue-memory QA datasets, evolved compressors achieve performance similar to that of recent advanced prompt-compression strategies. Performance is strongest under light-to-moderate compression and degrades as compression becomes more aggressive, while the Direct and Reconstruction paths exhibit distinct patterns. Evolutionary analysis reveals that effective compression fuses signals across linguistic levels and, as the compression ratio increases, rules shift from token pruning to sentence extraction.

</details>


### [20] [Temporal-Distance JEPA: Plan-Aware Representation Learning for Latent World Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25337)
*Jiaxin Bai,Jiaxuan Xiong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TD-JEPA, a method that augments JEPA world models with a learned temporal-distance cost from offline trajectories to improve planning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing JEPA-based planners use latent Euclidean distance as a proxy for progress cost, which is incidental to representation learning and not explicitly derived from temporal structure in offline logs. This leads to a mismatch between short-horizon JEPA training and the multi-step planning objective, creating a train–plan gap.

Method: TD-JEPA keeps the standard JEPA encoder–predictor backbone but adds a temporal-distance head trained on reward-free trajectories. It treats the order of states along the same trajectory as positive supervision for directed temporal progress, uses cross-trajectory pairs as heuristic negatives, and adds a rollout-consistency loss so the temporal predictions are consistent over the planner’s multi-step horizon. The learned temporal-distance signal is then used both as a planning cost and as an additional representation-learning objective.

Result: On locked (fixed-checkpoint) evaluation, using the mined temporal cost directly improves success on Two-Room from 97.4% (LeWM) to 100.0%. When the same temporally trained model is used with standard Euclidean planning, performance improves substantially on OGB-Cube (by 14.2 points over LeWM) and also on Push-T. Across all tested environments and compared to LeWM and the concurrent RC-aux baseline, TD-JEPA matches or exceeds their performance. Ablation studies indicate that each of the three components—directed temporal head, cross-trajectory negatives, and rollout-consistency—has a positive impact.

Conclusion: TD-JEPA reduces the mismatch between JEPA training and planning by explicitly learning temporal progress structure from offline, reward-free logs and aligning the form of the learned cost with how it is used at planning time. This co-designed temporal-distance objective improves both direct cost-based planning and latent Euclidean planning across multiple tasks, narrowing the train–plan gap for JEPA-based world-model planners.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn world models by predicting in representation space rather than reconstructing pixels, making them a natural backbone for latent model predictive control from offline demonstration logs. JEPA-style training optimizes short-horizon latent prediction, whereas planning requires a multi-step ranking of imagined futures by goal progress. Prior JEPA planners often inherit that ranking from embedding geometry, typically latent Euclidean distance, which arises as a byproduct of representation learning rather than as a progress cost mined from the logs. We propose temporal-distance JEPA (TD-JEPA), which retains the LeWM encoder--predictor backbone and mines a directed temporal cost from reward-free trajectories: same-trajectory step order supplies positive targets, cross-trajectory pairs act as heuristic negatives, and a rollout-consistency term matches the planner horizon. The mined supervision serves two roles: as the deployed planning cost when progress is topological, and as a representation signal that improves Euclidean planning when contact geometry dominates. Under locked evaluation, deploying the mined cost raises Two-Room success to 100.0% versus LeWM's 97.4%, while shared Euclidean planning on the same temporally trained checkpoint raises OGB-Cube by 14.2 points over LeWM and improves Push-T. Against LeWM and the concurrent RC-aux baseline under locked evaluation, TD-JEPA matches or exceeds both methods on every environment. Ablations show that the directed head, cross-trajectory negatives, and rollout consistency each contribute.
