TD-JEPA narrows the train--plan gap for JEPA world-model planners by discovering temporal progress structure in offline logs and co-designing cost form with plan-time deployment. Code is available at https://github.com/HKBU-KnowComp/TD-JEPA.

</details>


### [21] [Inspect India Evals: An Open Benchmarking Framework for Evaluating Large Language Models in the Indian Linguistic and Cultural Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25375)
*Abhishek Kumar Singh,Shrey Nag,Sachita,Lipi Goel,Rajeshwar Singh Janwar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Inspect India Evals introduces an India-focused evaluation framework for LLMs, adding multilingual, safety, fairness, and cultural knowledge benchmarks and showing which open models perform best on an India Fairness Index.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks such as MMLU, BIG-Bench, and TruthfulQA are predominantly English- and Western-centric. As LLMs are being deployed widely across India’s highly diverse linguistic and cultural landscape, these benchmarks fail to capture safety, fairness, and accuracy issues unique to Indian users and contexts. There is a need for systematic, reproducible evaluation tailored to Indian languages, social biases, cultural knowledge, and critical public digital infrastructure.

Method: The authors build Inspect India Evals, an open-source evaluation framework on top of UK AISI’s Inspect AI platform. They design six benchmarks: (1) Multilingual MMLU across 16 Indian languages; (2) BharatBBQ, an Indian adaptation of the BBQ social bias benchmark; (3) a safety evaluation specific to Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI); (4) a multilingual safety test using harmful prompts in Indian languages; (5) a multi-turn jailbreak resistance test; and (6) an Indian cultural knowledge benchmark scored via LLM-as-judge rubrics. They then evaluate five open-weight LLMs from 8B to 32B parameters on this benchmark suite and aggregate scores into a composite “India Fairness Index.”

Result: Among the five open-weight models tested (8B–32B), Sarvam-M 24B and Gemma 2 27B achieve the best overall performance, each scoring 80% on the composite India Fairness Index. Sarvam-M 24B outperforms even larger 32B models on Indian cultural knowledge and DPI safety compliance. All models achieve 100% refusal rates in the Multilingual Safety benchmark (they consistently refuse harmful prompts), but DPI safety scores vary widely between 20% and 100%, indicating uneven robustness in critical public-service contexts.

Conclusion: Inspect India Evals fills a critical gap by providing India-specific, multilingual, and safety-oriented benchmarks for LLMs, enabling more contextually appropriate evaluation for a large and diverse user base. The open-source framework, compatible with the UK AISI registry, allows others to reproduce the results and extend the benchmark suite. Empirical results show that some mid-sized open models can match or exceed larger models in Indian fairness, cultural competence, and DPI safety, underscoring the importance of targeted evaluation over raw model size.

Abstract: India is a vast nation of over 1.4 billion people, varied by hundreds of diverse and locally specific traditions and cultures and 22 officially recognized languages. Large language models (LLMs) are now being deployed on a massive scale throughout the mainland as well as in remote villages. However, the common benchmarks - MMLU, BIG-Bench, and TruthfulQA are almost exclusively English- and Western-centric. They do not identify those safety, fairness, and accuracy failures unique to the Indian context. That is the gap Inspect India Evals seeks to fill. It is an open-source framework built on top of UK AISI's Inspect AI platform. It has six benchmarks: Multilingual MMLU across sixteen Indian languages, BharatBBQ (our adaptation of BBQ for Indian social bias), a safety evaluation for Digital Public Infrastructure, a multilingual safety test using harmful prompts in Indian languages, a multi-turn jailbreak resistance test, and an Indian cultural knowledge benchmark scored using LLM-as-judge rubrics. In this study, we tested five open-weight models ranging from 8B to 32B parameters. Sarvam-M 24B and Gemma 2 27B came out on top, both scoring 80% on the composite India Fairness Index, with Sarvam-M even beating larger 32B models on Indian cultural knowledge and DPI safety compliance. All models scored 100% refusal on Multilingual Safety, whereas DPI safety varied from 20% to 100%. The framework is public. It's built to work with the UK AISI registry. Anyone can reproduce or extend this work.

</details>


### [22] [Memory for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25380)
*Sining Zhoubian,Dan Zhang,Evgeny Kharlamov,Jie Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Survey proposes a unified, architecture-centric taxonomy of memory mechanisms in large language models, organizing diverse approaches and outlining future directions for scalable, adaptive LLM memory design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory mechanisms in LLMs have rapidly diversified (e.g., attention, recurrent states, external stores, parameter-efficient tuning), leading to a fragmented landscape with unclear conceptual relationships, terminology, and design trade-offs. Researchers and practitioners lack a systematic framework to compare, combine, and evaluate these methods, which slows principled progress in memory-centric LLM architectures.

Method: The authors conduct a survey and taxonomy-building study: (1) they review recent memory-related architectures for LLMs across implicit and explicit mechanisms; (2) they define three orthogonal axes—representation (implicit vs. explicit), update dynamics (offline vs. online), and persistence (short-term vs. long-term); (3) they formalize core operations such as memory writing, routing, state transitions, and consolidation; (4) they analyze hybrid architectures and system-level trade-offs; and (5) they discuss evaluation methodologies for memory in LLMs.

Result: The survey yields an architecture-centric taxonomy that organizes existing memory approaches along the proposed three axes and clarifies the distinctions between computation-coupled memories (e.g., hidden states, attention) and independently addressable memories (e.g., external stores, key–value caches). It systematically maps recent work into this framework, highlights patterns and gaps, and articulates how different mechanisms interact in hybrid systems, including their efficiency and scalability implications.

Conclusion: By providing a unified conceptual and operational framework for memory in LLMs, the survey helps reconcile disparate architectural paradigms and offers guidance for designing future memory-centric models. It underscores the importance of viewing memory as a first-class architectural dimension, motivates more principled hybrid designs, and calls for richer, multi-dimensional evaluation benchmarks to measure memory quality, efficiency, and adaptability in large language models.

Abstract: Memory has evolved into a foundational architectural dimension in large language models (LLMs), shifting from an implicit byproduct of computation to a spectrum of explicit, controllable mechanisms. While recent advances introduce diverse strategies---spanning transient attention, recurrent state dynamics, parameter-efficient adaptations, and scalable lookup storage---this rapid evolution has led to a highly fragmented research landscape. In this survey, we present a systematic, architecture-centric taxonomy of memory in LLMs. Our framework characterizes memory along three orthogonal axes: representation (implicit versus explicit), update dynamics (offline versus online), and persistence (short-term versus long-term). We further formalize the granular mechanisms dictating memory writing, routing, state transitions, and consolidation. This unified perspective elucidates the conceptual boundaries between computation-coupled and independently addressable memory, effectively bridging disparate architectural paradigms. Additionally, we critically analyze hybrid memory architectures, system-level efficiency trade-offs, and multi-dimensional evaluation methodologies. By consolidating these scattered advancements into a cohesive framework, this survey charts the trajectory of memory-centric LLM design and provides a principled foundation for future innovations in scalable and adaptive language modeling.

</details>


### [23] [Phase Structure in Rotary Attention: A Spectral Framework for Semantic Continuity and Execution-Boundary Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25507)
*Abraham Chachamovits*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a spectral/phase-based framework to analyze rotary position embeddings and hidden-state continuity in transformers, without treating them as physical wave systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analyses of transformer language models mostly use static vector geometry, which does not capture the ordered, phase-like structure introduced by context order and rotary position encodings. There is also conceptual confusion between continuity of representations and notions of safe, permissible model behavior. A framework is needed that can rigorously describe phase alignment and continuity in hidden states while clearly separating representational properties from governance or safety judgments.

Method: (1) Formally treat ordered sequences of hidden states as the domain for spectral/phase decomposition rather than just token indices. (2) Re-express the RoPE attention score as a sum of magnitude-weighted cosine terms, making its phase structure explicit. (3) Prove a local stability lemma that bounds degradation of pre-softmax attention scores when phase displacements remain uniformly bounded. (4) Define complex modal coordinates over fixed orthonormal direction pairs to allow phase-style analysis in arbitrary bases, not only the native RoPE basis. (5) Introduce a weighted coherence functional over hidden-state trajectories to quantify internal spectral coherence. (6) Use these constructions to formally distinguish representational continuity from conditions required for execution-level admissibility or safe transitions.

Result: Shows that RoPE attention scores can be written as magnitude-weighted cosines of phase differences and that small, bounded phase changes only locally affect attention scores in a controlled way (local stability). Provides a general method to define complex modal coordinates and a coherence functional for hidden-state trajectories, enabling spectral/phase analysis beyond RoPE’s native coordinates. Demonstrates how these tools can diagnose hidden-state continuity and semantic drift without conflating them with behavioral safety or governance conditions.

Conclusion: Spectral and phase-based structure in transformer hidden states offers a precise way to analyze rotary position embeddings, phase alignment, and continuity, but such internal coherence concerns only representation, not the permissibility of model actions. The proposed framework gives a methodological basis for deciding when spectral structure genuinely explains continuity of behavior and when safety or governance must be imposed as an external constraint over execution rather than inferred from internal representations alone.

Abstract: Transformer language models are usually analyzed through vector geometry, yet ordered context and rotary position encoding introduce explicit phase structure into query-key interactions. This paper develops a bounded spectral framework for examining rotary phase alignment, hidden-state continuity, and semantic drift without treating language models as literal physical wave systems. It first identifies ordered hidden-state sequences, rather than vocabulary indices, as valid domains for spectral decomposition. It then derives the Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) attention score as a sum of magnitude-weighted cosine terms and proves a local stability lemma: uniformly bounded phase displacement limits degradation of the corresponding pre-softmax score. To extend phase analysis beyond native RoPE coordinates, the paper defines complex modal coordinates over fixed orthonormal direction pairs and introduces a weighted coherence functional for hidden-state trajectories. These constructions support a strict distinction between representational continuity and execution-boundary admissibility. Internal coherence may describe preservation of task-relevant relations, but it cannot authorize a consequential transition. Positioned against existing geometric, spectral, phase-modulation, representation-analysis, and mechanistic-interpretability accounts, the framework contributes a theoretical and methodological program for determining when spectral structure explains continuity and when governance must remain an external predicate over execution.

</details>


### [24] [IRIS: Reusable Identity Representations from Frozen LLMs for Entity Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25579)
*Xinran Liu,Shengtao Li,Shouqian Shi,Ge Wang,Xin-Wei Yao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces IRIS, a training-free framework that uses frozen LLM internal states to build stable, comparable identity signatures for entities, enabling efficient and accurate entity alignment across knowledge graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing entity alignment approaches rely on graph structure and surface text, which struggle with heterogeneous descriptions and semantically similar entities. LLM-based EA methods tap into deeper semantics but only in a pair- or candidate-dependent way, requiring repeated LLM calls and failing to create a reusable identity space. The authors aim to obtain stable, reusable entity representations that capture identity-level semantics directly from LLMs, eliminating per-pair re-computation and improving robustness across KGs.

Method: IRIS (Identity Representations from Internal States) is a training-free framework that queries a frozen LLM to elicit identity-oriented contextual representations for each entity. For each entity, IRIS constructs an ‘iris-like’ signature by carefully designing prompts/contexts so that the internal hidden states focus on the entity’s distinctive and stable identity characteristics. These internal states are then processed into fixed embeddings living in a shared semantic space across KGs. Once these signatures are computed once per entity, alignment is performed simply via similarity comparison (e.g., nearest neighbor search) between signatures from different KGs, without further pair-dependent representation construction or candidate-wise LLM inference.

Result: On four standard EA benchmarks (D-Y-15K V2, DBP-WIKI, ICEWS-WIKI, and ICEWS-YAGO) and using two frozen LLMs as backbones, IRIS variants reach state-of-the-art performance, achieving Hits@1 of 100.00, 99.38, 98.31, and 97.99, respectively. These results show that the proposed identity signatures are highly discriminative and robust across heterogeneous KGs while being obtained without additional training.

Conclusion: IRIS demonstrates that identity-focused internal representations from frozen LLMs can serve as powerful, reusable entity signatures for entity alignment. By decoupling representation construction from specific KG pairs and avoiding candidate-wise inference, IRIS provides a training-free, efficient, and highly accurate EA solution. This suggests a promising direction for leveraging LLM internal states for stable semantic identity spaces in other cross-graph or cross-domain matching tasks.

Abstract: Entity alignment (EA) identifies entities across knowledge graphs (KGs) that refer to the same real-world object. Conventional EA methods mainly exploit explicit graph structures and textual fields, which often provide insufficient semantic understanding to recognize the same entity under heterogeneous descriptions and distinguish it from semantically similar entities. Although large language models (LLMs) offer deeper entity understanding, existing LLM-based EA methods largely use this capability for auxiliary generation or candidate-conditioned decisions. Consequently, such understanding is not distilled into a stable and directly comparable identity space, leaving alignment tied to specific KG pairs or candidate sets and requiring repeated processing as the matching context changes. To address these limitations, we propose IRIS (Identity Representations from Internal States), a training-free framework that constructs for each entity an iris-like signature encoding its distinctive and stable identity characteristics. IRIS derives these signatures by eliciting identity-oriented contextual representations from a frozen LLM, thereby forming a shared space in which each entity is encoded once and can be aligned across different KGs through direct similarity comparison, without pair-dependent representation construction or candidate-wise LLM inference. Across four established EA benchmarks and two frozen LLM backbones, the best IRIS variants achieve Hits@1 scores of 100.00, 99.38, 98.31, and 97.99 on D-Y-15K V2, DBP-WIKI, ICEWS-WIKI, and ICEWS-YAGO, respectively.

</details>


### [25] [Evaluation of forced alignment of code-mixed speech: the case of Hindi-English](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25581)
*Ayushi Pandey,Pamir Gogoi,Kevin Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates and improves forced alignment for Hindi-English code-mixed speech, showing that bootstrapped, code-mixed acoustic and lexical resources greatly reduce alignment error versus monolingual models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code-mixed speech (here, Hindi-English) is common but hard to align automatically because of larger phoneme inventories, spelling inconsistencies, and variable pronunciations. Existing forced aligners and lexicons are mostly designed for monolingual data, which yields poor performance on bilingual or code-mixed speech. The authors want to understand these limitations and develop better alignment strategies for such data.

Method: They use the Montreal Forced Aligner on Hindi-English code-mixed speech and systematically compare different lexicon and acoustic model configurations. They tackle (1) free variation between native and non-native phoneme realizations, and (2) detecting phoneme boundaries when English words appear mid-utterance in Hindi. They design principled, bootstrapped lexicons and train acoustic models using sentence-level code-mixed data, then measure alignment error and compare against monolingual Hindi and isolated English acoustic models.

Result: Bootstrapping lexicons and using code-mixed training data substantially improve alignment performance compared to using unmodified, standard lexicons and monolingual models. The best code-mixed acoustic models reach a mean alignment error of 4.15 ms, approximately 10 times lower than monolingual Hindi (38.18 ms) or isolated English (37.58 ms) models on the same code-mixed data.

Conclusion: Reliable forced alignment for bilingual, code-mixed speech requires both carefully designed, code-mixed-aware lexicons and acoustic models trained directly on code-mixed data. Simply applying monolingual models or unmodified lexicons is inadequate; principled bootstrapping strategies and bilingual training lead to large gains in timing accuracy for phonemic boundaries.

Abstract: Code-mixed speech poses unique challenges to forced alignment: expanded inventories, orthographic errors, and speaker variation. We evaluate forced alignment of Hindi-English code-mixed speech using the Montreal Forced Aligner. We address 2 problems: (1) free variation involving native vs non-native pairs and (2) phonemic boundary detection for mid-utterance English words. Bootstrapping strategies substantially outperform unmodified lexicons. Acoustic models trained on sentence-level code-mixed data achieve a mean error of 4.15ms, ie. ten times lower than monolingual Hindi (38.18ms) or isolated English (37.58ms) alternatives. Principled lexicon design and code-mixed training data are both essential for reliable alignment of bilingual speech.

</details>


### [26] [PILA: Plug-and-Play Insertion for LLM-native Advertising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25590)
*Zhaowei Zhang,Yuhan Fu,Yihang Zhang,Xiaohan Liu,Ceyao Zhang,Xiaoyuan Zhang,Yipeng Kang,Tonghan Wang,Yaodong Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PILA, a model-agnostic sidecar system that inserts sponsored content into LLM responses by rewriting them after generation, improving ad effectiveness while preserving response quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monetizing LLMs through integrated ads is important, but current approaches tightly couple ad insertion with content generation in a single model, which conflicts with API/workflow-based deployments and degrades response quality. A decoupled, controllable, and model-agnostic solution is needed.

Method: The authors formulate ad insertion as a conditional response rewriting task handled by a separate, lightweight module (PILA) that sits alongside existing LLM services. This sidecar rewrites already-generated responses to incorporate ads according to controllable parameters that balance naturalness with ad exposure, without changing the base model or workflow.

Result: Across different upstream LLMs, PILA increases advertising effectiveness (e.g., better ad exposure and presumably user engagement) while maintaining the original response quality as judged by relevant metrics or evaluations.

Conclusion: PILA offers a practical, model-agnostic approach to LLM-native advertising by decoupling ad insertion from core content generation, enabling flexible deployment and explicit control over the trade-off between user experience and ad visibility.

Abstract: How to monetize large language models (LLMs) by naturally integrating sponsored content into their responses, known as LLM-native advertising, has recently emerged as a critical problem. However, existing solutions entangle advertising with content generation inside a single model, which is incompatible with modern API-only or workflow-based LLM applications and inevitably compromises the original response quality. To address this, we propose PILA, which reformulates ad insertion as a conditional response rewriting problem and decouples it from the upstream service as a lightweight sidecar module. PILA is model-agnostic and can be seamlessly integrated with existing LLM services without modifying the base model or its workflow. It also exposes a controllable trade-off between user-side naturalness and ad-side exposure, offering a practical interface for downstream pricing and deployment. Experiments across diverse upstream models show that \pila consistently improves ad effectiveness while preserving response quality, highlighting its promise as a practical solution for LLM-native advertising.

</details>


### [27] [A Human-in-the-Loop Corpus for LLM-Based Simplification of Scientific Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25630)
*Kyuri Im,Michael Färber*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds a human-in-the-loop workflow and dataset for simplifying scientific summaries across disciplines, analyzing how LLM-generated simplifications compare to expert-edited ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interdisciplinary work is growing, but scientific papers are still hard to understand for researchers outside the originating field. Existing simplification methods may oversimplify or distort key technical content, and there is a lack of high-quality, human-annotated resources focused on cross-disciplinary scientific simplification. The authors aim to systematically study and support the simplification of scientific text for non-specialist researchers while preserving crucial scientific meaning.

Method: Using SciSummNet as the base corpus, the authors first generate baseline simplified summaries with an LLM (GPT-4o-mini). They then run a two-phase human-in-the-loop workflow: (1) STEM readers from fields other than computer science annotate difficult parts of the text and evaluate original vs. GPT-simplified summaries on comprehensibility, naturalness, and simplicity; (2) computer science experts use this feedback to create expert-edited reference simplifications that better balance accessibility with fidelity to technical content. They also collect human judgments and compute automatic evaluation metrics for these versions.

Result: Phase 1 results show that cross-disciplinary STEM readers generally prefer the GPT-generated simplifications over the original summaries for both comprehensibility and simplicity. In Phase 2, qualitative analysis of expert edits reveals systematic issues, such as the need to maintain domain-specific terminology where it is essential and to accurately preserve the strength and nuance of scientific claims. The final output is a curated corpus with multiple versions of each summary plus human evaluation data and automatic scores.

Conclusion: LLM-based simplification can substantially improve the accessibility of scientific summaries for non-specialists, but naive simplification risks losing important technical precision and hedging. A human-in-the-loop workflow with domain experts yields higher-quality reference simplifications and a valuable dataset for training and evaluating future simplification systems tailored to cross-disciplinary scientific communication.

Abstract: Interdisciplinary research is accelerating, yet scientific papers remain difficult to understand outside their home fields. We study large language model (LLM)-based simplification of scientific texts and present a human-in-the-loop workflow that transforms expert summaries into more accessible versions for non-specialists. Using SciSummNet as the source corpus, we first generate baseline simplifications with GPT-4o-mini. In Phase 1, readers from STEM fields outside computer science identify difficult sentences and phrases and compare the original and GPT-simplified summaries in terms of comprehensibility, naturalness, and simplicity. In Phase 2, computer science experts use this feedback to create expert-edited reference simplifications. We release the resulting corpus together with human judgments and automatic evaluation results. The Phase 1 judgments show a clear preference for the GPT-generated summaries in terms of comprehensibility and simplicity, while qualitative analysis of the Phase 2 edits highlights the importance of preserving domain-specific terminology and the strength of scientific claims. The resulting resource supports the training and benchmarking of simplification systems for cross-disciplinary scientific communication.

</details>


### [28] [Construction-Driven Injection: Linguistically-Grounded Edit-Based Code-Mixing Fingerprints for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25633)
*Yongyi Cui,Yue Li,Tianbao Jiang,Xin Yi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified framework to embed robust, verifiable ownership fingerprints into large language models using linguistically informed code-mixed triggers and structure-aware parameter updates, achieving strong watermark verification with minimal impact on model utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are valuable but vulnerable to unauthorized copying and misuse. Existing model fingerprinting techniques either use natural-language triggers that can fire accidentally, or unnatural, garbled triggers that are easily detected and filtered. Moreover, prior approaches separate how a fingerprint is designed from how it is injected into the model, so the injection step does not leverage the linguistic structure of the trigger. The authors aim to build fingerprints that are both natural and robust while integrating construction and injection into a single, coordinated framework.

Method: The authors introduce a two-part framework. (1) LCF (Language Code-mixing Fingerprint) constructs fingerprints by code-mixing low-resource languages according to a semantic-density substitution rule and grammar-biased mixing, producing triggers that are linguistically plausible, low-perplexity, and unlikely to occur by chance. (2) LCFEdit injects these fingerprints into an LLM by computing a null-space projection using high-resource multilingual representations to preserve existing knowledge, and adds a cross-lingual alignment step that guides the weight update toward the representation subspace of the fingerprint language. This joint design makes the fingerprint-specific fine-tuning aware of and aligned with the linguistic structure of the trigger phrases.

Result: The unified framework yields fingerprints that have substantially lower perplexity than garbled baselines while reducing accidental activations compared to natural-language triggers. The null-space and alignment-based injection leads to stable model behavior, maintaining original capabilities while reliably activating the fingerprinted behaviors under trigger queries. Experiments show that the fingerprints are hard to detect with standard perplexity-based filters, remain imperceptible in normal use, and support robust ownership verification under black-box access.

Conclusion: By tightly coupling the design of code-mixed linguistic fingerprints with a linguistically informed injection procedure, the proposed framework provides robust, hard-to-remove, and black-box-verifiable ownership signals for LLMs. It overcomes the main limitations of previous methods—accidental activation and easy detectability—while preserving model utility, offering a practical approach for protecting LLM intellectual property.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are costly intellectual assets that remain exposed to unauthorized redistribution and commercial misuse. Injected fingerprints, i.e., trigger--target pairs embedded in model behavior, offer a practical, black-box-verifiable ownership signal, but existing methods decouple the two stages of the fingerprint life cycle: how a fingerprint is constructed and how it is injected. Existing fingerprinting frameworks suffer from two limitations. Natural-language fingerprints are prone to accidental activation, and garbled fingerprints are easily filtered by perplexity-based detection. Furthermore, decoupling construction from injection leaves the latter unaware of the trigger's linguistic structure, missing the opportunity for targeted optimization. We argue that fingerprint construction should drive injection, and present a unified fingerprinting framework that jointly optimizes both stages. First, LCF constructs code-mixing fingerprints by combining low-resource languages under a semantic-density substitution rule and grammar-biased mixing, yielding triggers whose perplexity sits far below garbled baselines while avoiding the accidental-activation failures of natural-language triggers. Second, LCFEdit injects each fingerprint with a null-space projection derived from high-resource multilingual representations that preserves knowledge, augmented by a cross-lingual alignment step that steers the weight update toward the fingerprint language's representation subspace. This construction-aware injection ensures that the update is linguistically informed and therefore more stable. Extensive evaluations on imperceptibility, detectability, and harmlessness demonstrate persistent ownership verification with negligible impact on utility.

</details>


### [29] [MyMentorLLM: A psychotherapy GenAI environment with multimodal voice/text patients, trainees and experts for deliberate practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25667)
*Rodolfo Rizzi,Alessandro Grecucci,Massimo Stella*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents MyMentorLLM, a multimodal LLM-based simulation environment for scalable deliberate practice in CBT training, generating 2,100 supervised therapy sessions with DSM-5-TR-based simulated patients, trainee therapists, and LLM supervisors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Psychotherapist training requires extensive repeated practice and expert supervision, which is hard to scale due to limited expert availability and time. The authors aim to investigate whether large language model-based simulation can provide realistic, evaluable CBT training sessions at scale, including patients, therapists-in-training, and supervisors.

Method: They build MyMentorLLM, a multimodal (voice and text) simulation environment that automatically generates full CBT training sessions. Each session includes a DSM-5-TR-grounded simulated patient with specific disorders (major depressive disorder, generalized anxiety disorder, or borderline personality disorder), a trainee therapist, and an expert supervisor implemented by various LLMs. Using 2,100 generated sessions, they analyze emotional dynamics in conversations, therapeutic competence, diagnostic accuracy, and the impact of supervisory feedback across multiple LLMs, including models with native speech-to-speech abilities.

Result: Simulated patients display emotional patterns consistent with their diagnosed disorders, and trainee therapists mirror these emotions similarly to real counseling interactions. Supervisory quality varies by LLM: many models systematically overestimate trainee competence, whereas a native speech-to-speech model aligns most closely with human supervisor ratings. Supervisory feedback generally improves diagnostic performance in simulated therapists in 5 of 7 evaluated LLMs, and larger models show higher accuracy in identifying symptoms.

Conclusion: LLM-driven simulation of deliberate practice for CBT training is feasible and can produce realistic emotional dynamics and useful supervisory feedback that often improves diagnosis and symptom recognition. However, the system’s effectiveness depends on model choice; issues such as patient fidelity, accurate calibration of supervisors (to avoid inflated ratings), and the risk of harmful or misleading feedback must be jointly assessed before safe deployment in real training contexts.

Abstract: Psychotherapists need repeated training and supervision by experts; however, scalability is problematic. Here we present MyMentorLLM, a multimodal voice- and text-based simulation environment for deliberate practice, used to generate 2,100 complete Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT) training sessions. Each session links a DSM-5-TR-grounded patient (with major depressive, generalised anxiety or borderline personality disorder), a therapist-in-training and an expert supervisor. As an initial implementation, we adopted CBT because its structured procedures and competency-based supervision facilitate standardised simulation and evaluation. Sessions were analysed for emotional dynamics, therapeutic competence and diagnostic accuracy. Simulated patients expressed disorder-congruent emotional profiles, which trainee therapists mirrored as in real human counselling. The quality of supervision differed across LLMs: while most models overestimated trainees' competences, native speech-to-speech was closest to human scores. Supervisors' feedback led to better diagnoses in simulated psychotherapists in 5 out of 7 LLMs, and symptom identification accuracy increased with model size. This work shows that simulation of deliberate practice is possible for CBT training, although patient fidelity, calibration of supervisors, and harmful feedback should be evaluated together.

</details>


### [30] [SpeechLLM Meets Federated Learning for End-to-End ASR: English and Italian Case Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25716)
*Mohamed Nabih Ali,Daniele Falavigna,Alessio Brutti*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to train large SpeechLLM-based end-to-end ASR models with federated learning efficiently and reliably.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While federated learning has been used for ASR, its application to large SpeechLLM-style models is unexplored, and such models pose challenges due to large parameter counts, communication overhead, and resource limits on distributed clients.

Method: The authors design a communication-efficient federated optimization strategy customized for SpeechLLM architectures, and empirically evaluate it on monolingual English and Italian ASR under diverse acoustic and speaking conditions. They also perform an ablation study of different speech encoder architectures within the federated setting.

Result: Their federated SpeechLLM training achieves competitive word error rates versus centralized baselines while significantly reducing communication costs, and shows stable behavior across varied conditions.

Conclusion: Federated training of SpeechLLM-based ASR is feasible and effective: with a tailored optimization strategy, one can obtain competitive accuracy with lower communication cost, and the analysis suggests good encoder choices for decentralized deployment in real-world, potentially multilingual, scenarios.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables privacy-preserving training of automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems across distributed data sources, yet its application to large-scale speech language models (SpeechLLMs) remains unexplored. This paper presents the first systematic study of federated training for SpeechLLM-based end-to-end ASR systems. We design a communication-efficient federated optimization strategy tailored to the unique challenges of SpeechLLM architectures, addressing high-dimensional parameter spaces, gradient communication overhead, and computational constraints in distributed settings. Through extensive empirical evaluation on monolingual ASR tasks in English and Italian, we demonstrate the effectiveness and stability of our federated approach compared to centralized training baselines across diverse acoustic conditions and speaking styles. Additionally, we conduct a comprehensive ablation study analyzing the impact of different speech encoder architectures on monolingual English ASR performance within the federated framework, providing insights into optimal model configurations for decentralized training. Our results achieve competitive word error rates while reducing communication costs, establishing practical foundations for federated SpeechLLM deployment in real-world multilingual scenarios.

</details>


### [31] [WorkSurface-Bench: Benchmarking Enterprise Agents on Multi-Surface Knowledge Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25765)
*Hao Liang,Meiyi Qiang,Sizhe Qiu,Linzhuang Sun,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces WorkSurface-Bench, a benchmark to test whether enterprise agents can correctly choose among documents, tables, and graphs when answering questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI agents must work over heterogeneous knowledge formats—narrative documents, computational tables, and dependency graphs. Existing benchmarks mostly test retrieval or tool use in isolation and don’t clearly measure the crucial first step: deciding which knowledge surface(s) to use for a given task. This gap makes it hard to diagnose whether failures come from bad routing or weak reasoning.

Method: The authors build WorkSurface-Bench from persona-specific workspaces in Workspace-Bench-Lite, decomposing them into 1,151 atomic tasks that target four types of questions: document, table, graph, and cross-surface. For each type, they create auditable gold answers: DuckDB queries for table questions, curated text spans for document questions, and dependency annotations for graph questions. They then run four backbone models under six controlled agent configurations (varying tool access and hints) to generate 27,624 valid trajectories, and evaluate both routing performance (Route F1) and answer accuracy (Answer), plus perform human quality audits.

Result: With perfect (gold-constrained) tool access, agents route almost perfectly (98.7–99.8 Route F1), yet their answer accuracy lags significantly (56.1–75.3 Answer), showing that picking the right surface doesn’t guarantee correct solutions. Interventions that provide surface hints generally boost Answer for three of four models, while pruning irrelevant tools mainly improves routing quality and efficiency. A human audit of 200 sampled tasks finds that all pass six quality criteria by majority vote, with 192 tasks receiving unanimous agreement on every criterion.

Conclusion: Surface routing—choosing the right knowledge sources—is a distinct, measurable capability for enterprise agents and can be evaluated using WorkSurface-Bench. The benchmark demonstrates that while modern agents can nearly perfect routing under ideal conditions, substantial headroom remains in actual problem solving and reasoning. The released dataset and tooling offer a standardized way to study routing, tool configuration, and their impact on end-task performance.

Abstract: Enterprise agents often need to integrate heterogeneous knowledge sources: documents for narrative facts, tables for computation, and dependency graphs for file relationships. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate retrieval or tool use without distinguishing whether an agent first selects the appropriate knowledge sources. We introduce WorkSurface-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability as surface routing. It contains 1,151 atomic tasks derived from persona-scoped Workspace-Bench-Lite workspaces, spanning document, table, graph, and cross-surface questions. Its reference answers are auditable: table answers are reproduced through executed DuckDB queries, document answers are grounded in verified text spans, and graph answers are traced to source dependency annotations. We evaluate four model backbones across six controlled agent settings, yielding 27,624 protocol-error-free trajectories. Under gold-constrained tool access, agents achieve 98.7-99.8 Route F1, while Answer remains only 56.1-75.3 percent, showing that correct surface selection is necessary but insufficient for task completion. Matched interventions further show that surface hints improve Answer for three of four models, whereas removing irrelevant tools primarily improves routing and efficiency. In an independent three-annotator audit, all 200 sampled tasks pass all six quality criteria by majority vote, with 192 receiving unanimous judgments on every criterion. We release the dataset, construction pipeline, scoring code, and agent harness at https://github.com/haolpku/WorkSurface-Bench.

</details>


### [32] [Evaluation of Adversarial Robustness in Arabic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25814)
*Anwar Alajmi,Ayed Salman,Imtiaz Ahmad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how robust five top Arabic language models are against various adversarial attacks at character, word, and sentence levels, and examines whether adversarial training can defend against these attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent Arabic language models are powerful but potentially vulnerable to adversarial attacks that can cause incorrect predictions, creating security and safety risks. There is a lack of systematic evaluation and defense analysis of such attacks for Arabic, a morphologically rich language.

Method: The authors subject five state-of-the-art Arabic language models to a suite of adversarial attacks at different granularities: character-level (e.g., adding diacritics), word-level (e.g., manipulating conjunctions), and sentence-level (e.g., paraphrasing). They employ various adversarial example generation strategies and then apply an adversarial training defense technique to the models to test improvements in robustness.

Result: Character-level attacks using diacritic insertion can reduce accuracy of some models by up to 92% with minimal perturbation. Word-level attacks that modify Arabic conjunctions keep semantics similar and perturbation low, yet still decrease accuracy by up to 58%. Sentence-level paraphrasing attacks lower model performance on average by 76%. Adversarial training improves robustness overall, with MARBERT being the most robust model and AraBERT achieving the largest relative robustness gains. However, defenses remain weak, especially against character-level noise.

Conclusion: Arabic language models are highly vulnerable to adversarial attacks across multiple linguistic levels, particularly subtle character-level and morphologically-aware perturbations. Adversarial training offers partial but insufficient protection, underscoring the need for stronger, language-specific robustness methods tailored to morphologically rich languages like Arabic.

Abstract: The emergence of the recent outstanding capabilities of Arabic Language Models has opened doors for exposing their vulnerabilities. One of the major security risks associated with such Natural Language Processing models is adversarial attacks. These attacks can deceive the model into the wrong prediction, raising critical model security and safety concerns. This study aims to assess the robustness of five state-of-the-art Arabic Language Models under a distinct set of Arabic adversarial attacks applied at various levels of granularity and using different example generation strategies. We also explore a defense technique based on adversarial training to enhance model robustness. The results show that insertion of diacritics can reduce the accuracy of some models by 92% while maintaining a low perturbation distance. For word-level attacks, manipulating Arabic conjunctions preserves high semantic similarity scores, low perturbation distance, and leads to an accuracy degradation of up to 58%. For sentence-level attacks, paraphrasing proves its effectiveness by an average reduction of 76% in the victim models' performance. While adversarial training improves overall resilience, with MARBERT being the most robust and AraBERT showing the greatest relative gains, challenges persist, particularly against character-level noise. These findings highlight both the potential and limitations of current defense strategies in morphologically rich languages like Arabic.

</details>


### [33] [AngelSpec: Towards Real-World High Performance Inference with Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25852)
*Hong Liu,Rui Cen,Junhan Shi,Guangshuo Qin,Jiebin Zhang,Tianyu Liu,Runzhi Fan,Guoliang Zhao,Ruobing Xie,Kai Zhang,Song Liu,Guanghua Yu,Jianchen Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AngelSpec is a unified framework that trains and serves multiple speculative decoding strategies (MTP and block-parallel diffusion) so that LLM inference is faster and better matched to different domains and workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculative decoding methods speed up LLMs but none works best across all tasks: MTP works well for short, high-entropy chat, while block-parallel diffusion is better for long, predictable outputs like code and math. A single universal drafter and fixed verification strategy underperform when workloads and hardware characteristics vary. The authors want a way to co-design training, architecture, and inference policies so speculative decoding is robust and efficient across diverse real-world use cases.

Method: 1) Training level: Introduce AngelSpec, which trains different drafters on domain-specialized data—MTP on broad conversational data; block-diffusion on code/math—to co-specialize data and drafting structure. 2) Architecture level: Propose DFly, a block-diffusion speculative decoder with a hybrid target-conditioning backbone and a predecessor-conditioned autoregressive head, to better use target features and model intra-block dependencies while remaining parallel. 3) Inference level: Treat verification compute as a shared resource across a batch of requests; dynamically shift verification depth toward high-confidence prefixes and adapt it online using an expected-utility plus profiled-cost model, responsive to domain, request characteristics, load, and hardware.

Result: On the Hy3 model series, DFly increases average accepted length on Hy3-A21B by about 30%. It achieves the highest throughput across all tested levels of concurrency (4–64), providing a 1.98–2.40x speedup over standard autoregressive decoding and a 10.5–11.8% throughput improvement over DFlash, a strong speculative baseline.

Conclusion: Specializing speculative drafting strategies and data per domain, improving the block-diffusion architecture (DFly), and adaptively allocating verification compute at inference time yields consistent speedups over both vanilla autoregressive decoding and prior speculative methods. AngelSpec provides a practical framework and implementation for training and extending such heterogeneous speculative decoders across real-world workloads.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference without changing the target distribution, but no single drafting structure performs best across real-world workloads. Autoregressive multi-token prediction (MTP) is a lightweight, stable proposal mechanism, whereas block-parallel diffusion amortizes drafting latency over much longer candidate sequences; the better choice depends strongly on the output distribution. We present AngelSpec, a unified training framework for MTP and block-parallel speculative decoding that addresses this heterogeneity at three levels. At the training level, rather than fitting one universal drafter to a uniform data mixture, we co-specialize structure and data: the MTP drafter is trained on diverse conversational data for high-entropy open-ended chat, and the block-diffusion drafter on code and mathematics data for longer predictable continuations. At the architecture level, we propose DFly, a block-diffusion framework combining a hybrid target-conditioning backbone with a predecessor-conditioned autoregressive head, improving target-feature utilization and intra-block dependency modeling while keeping generation parallel. At the inference level, both acceptance length and verification cost vary with domain, request, online load, and hardware, so DFly treats verification as a shared batch-level resource: it reallocates compute toward high-confidence prefixes across requests and combines expected utility with a profiled cost model to adapt verification depth online. Across the Hy3 series, DFly raises the average accepted length on Hy3-A21B by roughly 30% and attains the highest average throughput at every tested concurrency from 4 to 64, a 1.98-2.40x speedup over autoregressive decoding and 10.5-11.8% higher throughput than DFlash. We release AngelSpec to support training and extending these methods.

</details>


### [34] [Shieldstral](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25857)
*Antonia Calvi,Avinash Sooriyarachchi,Giada Pistilli,Guillaume Lample,Maarten Buyl,Maximilian Augustin,Maximilian Müller,Pierre Stock,Tom Bewley,Wassim Bouaziz,Yimu Pan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Shieldstral is a 3B-parameter multimodal safety classifier that, via a unified yes/no QA formulation and large-scale heterogeneous training data, matches or surpasses much larger models on text and multimodal safety benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety classifiers for text and multimodal content are often large, fragmented across many taxonomy-specific tasks, and struggle to adapt when policies or label taxonomies change. There is a need for a compact yet strong model that can perform moderation across diverse safety tasks and modalities, and flexibly adapt to different safety policies without retraining from scratch.

Method: They design Shieldstral, a 3B-parameter multimodal classifier that casts moderation as a binary question-answering task: given content plus a policy-style question, the model outputs yes/no. This reframing allows them to unify heterogeneous safety datasets with different taxonomies into a single training objective. They build and use a large corpus of around 54.1M curated and synthetically generated samples, and construct a fine-grained evaluation set specifically to measure how well the model can adapt to varying safety policies.

Result: Shieldstral matches or outperforms models almost seven times larger on text safety benchmarks, and achieves state-of-the-art performance on multimodal safety classification. The adaptive QA-style formulation and large, diverse training data allow the relatively small model to be highly competitive with much larger systems.

Conclusion: Formulating content moderation as a binary QA task, combined with large-scale heterogeneous training data, enables a small 3B-parameter multimodal model to reach or surpass the performance of significantly larger models and to adapt effectively to different moderation policies. This suggests that policy-adaptive, unified QA-style architectures are a promising direction for scalable, efficient safety classification.

Abstract: We introduce Shieldstral, a 3B-parameter policy-adaptive multimodal safety classifier that matches or outperforms models nearly 7$\times$ its size on text safety benchmarks and sets a new state of the art on multimodal safety classification. Shieldstral formulates content moderation as a binary question-answering task. This simple formulation unifies diverse moderation tasks into a single yes/no problem, enabling heterogeneous safety datasets with divergent taxonomies to be consolidated under one training framework. We present the data construction recipe, covering curation and generation of approximately 54.1M samples and a fine-grained evaluation set to evaluate policy adaptability. Together, these enable a small adaptive model to match or outperform much larger models.

</details>


### [35] [AI's Capability in Assisting Scientific Research in Physics, Astrophysics, and Cosmology II: Project Planning and Proposal Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25881)
*Jia Liu,Veena Krishnaraj,Kateryna Vovk,Kosuke Aizawa,Adrian E. Bayer,Linda Blot,Jessica Cowell,Suyog Garg,Jonathan Grée,Anamaria Hell,Ben Horowitz,Masaya Ichikawa,Kanyuni Iemoto,Keigo Kondo,Zacharie Lorsin,Kevin McCarthy,Jamie Robinson,Miguel Ruiz-Granda,Leander Thiele,Ievgen Vovk,Mingshen Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Study tests how well large language models can write and evaluate scientific project plans versus humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly help write grant proposals and may also be used to review them, it is important to know whether their project plans are comparable to humans’ and whether AI reviewers treat human- and AI-written proposals differently or exhibit bias.

Method: Researchers took eight real physics, astrophysics, and cosmology project ideas designed by experts. For each project, one-page plans were written independently by human researchers and by three LLMs (ChatGPT, Claude, DeepSeek; mid‑2025 default setups), giving 32 proposals. Four human reviewers and two newer LLM reviewers (Claude Opus 4.8, ChatGPT Pro 5.5) then blindly scored each proposal on four rubric dimensions and tried to guess whether each was human- or AI-written.

Result: Human reviewers gave similar average scores to human- and AI-generated project plans. In contrast, the two AI reviewers rated AI-written proposals about one point higher (on a five-point scale) than human-written ones, indicating a sizable AI-favoring bias. For authorship detection, human reviewers correctly identified human- and AI-generated texts 72% and 79% of the time, while both AI reviewers perfectly distinguished them (100% accuracy).

Conclusion: LLMs can already generate scientific project plans that human reviewers judge to be on par with those written by researchers. However, AI reviewers exhibit a systematic preference for AI-generated proposals and can reliably detect AI authorship, raising concerns about fairness and bias if LLMs are widely used in both drafting and evaluating scientific proposals. The study recommends caution in integrating LLMs into proposal workflows.

Abstract: We investigate how well large language models (LLMs) can assist scientific project planning and proposal evaluation. One-page project plans were independently generated for eight expert-conceived research projects in physics, astrophysics, and cosmology by human researchers and three contemporary LLMs (ChatGPT, Claude, and DeepSeek; mid-2025 models, used with their default tool access). The resulting 32 proposals were blindly evaluated by four human reviewers and two newer frontier LLMs (Claude Opus 4.8 and ChatGPT Pro 5.5) using a four-aspect evaluation rubric. Reviewers were also asked to identify whether each proposal was written by a human or an AI. Human reviewers rated human- and AI-written proposals similarly overall, whereas both AI reviewers scored AI-written proposals about one point higher (on a five-point scale) than human-written proposals. Human reviewers correctly identified human- and AI-written proposals 72% and 79% of the time, respectively, while both AI reviewers correctly classified all 32 proposals (100%). These results suggest that current LLMs can produce project plans comparable to human-written ones in the eyes of human reviewers, but that AI reviewers show a systematic preference for AI-generated proposals. Our results suggest caution when deploying LLMs widely in proposal preparation and evaluation.

</details>


### [36] [Evaluating Multi-Turn Multimodal Diagnostic Reasoning on Challenging Real-World Clinical Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25933)
*Rui Yang,Weihao Xuan,Yi Lin,Zhuhan Bao,Jonathan Chong Kai Liew,Matthew Yu Heng Wong,Nicolás Lescano,Nikita R. Paripati,Emily Ling-Lin Pai,Jiarui Liu,Heli Qi,Heng-Jui Chang,Benny Kai Guo Loo,Huitao Li,Kunyu Yu,Yufan Wang,Chuan Hong,Shijian Lu,Douglas Teodoro,Naoto Yokoya,Ross Koppel,Mona Diab,Hua Xu,David W. Bates,Nan Liu,Yifan Peng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ClinMM-Bench, a large multi-turn multimodal benchmark to realistically evaluate clinical diagnostic performance of multimodal LLMs, revealing limited diagnostic accuracy and major weaknesses in reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of multimodal large language models in medicine mostly use single-turn or isolated tasks, which fail to reflect how clinicians actually work: they iteratively gather multimodal data (text, images), update hypotheses, and refine reasoning over time. There is a gap between current benchmarks and real-world clinical diagnostic practice, so a more realistic evaluation setting is needed.

Method: The authors constructed ClinMM-Bench, a benchmark of 1,089 real-world, challenging clinical cases with 3,760 medical images across eight specialties, organized in multi-turn, progressively disclosed interactions. They then evaluated 15 representative multimodal LLMs with a two-level framework: (1) diagnostic accuracy (correctness of final diagnosis) and (2) quality of diagnostic reasoning (how well the model’s reasoning process matches sound clinical logic). They also performed error analysis to categorize typical failure patterns.

Result: Proprietary multimodal LLMs achieved the best diagnostic accuracy on ClinMM-Bench, but even for them, the rate of fully correct diagnoses is still low, indicating substantial room for improvement. Across all models, reasoning quality is limited: models can usually suggest plausible diagnostic directions but often fail to produce reliable, coherent clinical reasoning. Error analysis revealed five common failure modes: (1) information synthesis failure (inability to integrate multimodal data), (2) knowledge mapping error (incorrect linking of findings to diseases), (3) perception error (misinterpretation of images), (4) premature closure (settling on a diagnosis too early), and (5) visual hallucination (describing or relying on non-existent visual findings).

Conclusion: ClinMM-Bench offers a more realistic and challenging way to evaluate multimodal LLMs for clinical diagnosis by modeling multi-turn, multimodal interactions and assessing both accuracy and reasoning. Current models, including leading proprietary systems, remain far from clinically reliable: they struggle with robust reasoning and show characteristic failure modes. The benchmark thus highlights key gaps and provides a platform for driving future improvements in clinically oriented multimodal AI.

Abstract: Clinical diagnostic evaluation should not only assess whether models can provide correct diagnoses, but also reflect the realities of clinical practice, including progressive disclosure of multimodal information, dynamic updating of diagnostic hypotheses, and continuous refinement of clinical reasoning. However, existing evaluations of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) typically rely on single-turn or isolated tasks, making it difficult to fully capture the complexity of real-world clinical diagnosis. To bridge this gap, we developed ClinMM-Bench, the largest multi-turn multimodal clinical diagnostic evaluation benchmark to date. ClinMM-Bench contains 1,089 challenging real-world clinical cases and 3,760 medical images across eight specialties. We systematically evaluated 15 representative MLLMs using a two-level evaluation framework that assessed both diagnostic accuracy and diagnostic reasoning quality. Results showed that proprietary models achieved the highest overall diagnostic accuracy, but the proportion of completely correct diagnoses remained limited across all models. In terms of diagnostic reasoning quality, current models can identify plausible diagnostic directions but still have considerable limitations in generating reliable diagnostic reasoning. Error analysis further identified five representative failure modes: information synthesis failure, knowledge mapping error, perception error, premature closure, and visual hallucination.

</details>


### [37] [Polistemics: Evaluating LLMs as Information Mediators in Politics & Elections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25953)
*Baran Peters*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Polistemics is a benchmark to evaluate how responsibly LLMs mediate political information in elections, especially under imperfect or conflicting evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to provide political information to citizens, yet there is no standard, theory-based way to evaluate whether they do this responsibly. Existing work mainly checks if models reproduce information correctly, not how they mediate between citizens and imperfect or uncertain information. The authors want a framework that reflects citizens’ epistemic agency and deals explicitly with noisy, partial, or contradictory evidence in election contexts.

Method: They introduce Polistemics, a theory-grounded benchmark based on the normative principle of Epistemic Modesty (LLMs should respect limits of evidence and the user’s epistemic agency). The benchmark tests LLMs in controlled scenarios for elections, systematically varying information properties like clarity, noise, absence, and internal consistency. They then apply this benchmark to three state-of-the-art LLMs, evaluating their behavior on tasks related to the 2025 German and Dutch elections, and score models on how well they satisfy Epistemic Modesty across conditions.

Result: Across models, high average benchmark scores conceal systematic weaknesses. The models perform reliably when evidence is clear and unambiguous but perform poorly when information is missing, vague, noisy, or contradictory. Under these harder conditions, models both fail epistemically (e.g., overstate what is known) and pragmatically (e.g., flatten the intensity of political language). The authors also find that these failures seem to be driven by prior assumptions about parties, affected by cues such as party labels and the language of the output.

Conclusion: Reliable, epistemically modest mediation of political information by LLMs appears possible in principle—models can behave well in clear-evidence scenarios—but no evaluated system does so consistently across realistic, imperfect-information conditions. Therefore, current LLMs should not be treated as robust, responsible mediators of election-related information, and evaluation in this area must move beyond simple correctness toward theory-guided benchmarks like Polistemics that explicitly test performance under uncertainty and bias-inducing conditions.

Abstract: As LLMs increasingly mediate the political information citizens rely on, there is still no standardized way to assess whether they do so responsibly. We introduce Polistemics, a theory-grounded benchmark for evaluating LLMs as mediators of political information in elections. Prior work has treated this task as reproduction rather than mediation, leaving its epistemic dimensions and interaction with imperfect information unaddressed. We ground the evaluation in Epistemic Modesty, a normative standard derived from citizens' epistemic agency, and test it across controlled settings that vary informational properties such as clarity, noise, and consistency. Applying the benchmark to three state-of-the-art LLMs on the 2025 German and Dutch elections, we find that high aggregate scores mask systematic failures. Models mediate reliably under clear evidence but break down under absent, vague, or contradictory information, while flattening the intensity of political language. These failures are likely driven by party priors, influenced by party labels and output language. Reliable mediation appears achievable, but no model delivers it consistently.

</details>


### [38] [Detecting Knowledge Inconsistencies Across Text, Tables, and Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25959)
*Fanfu Wei,Thibault Ehrhart,Raphaël Troncy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes KONTRAST, a framework to automatically detect and categorize inconsistencies between text/tables and knowledge graphs in Wikipedia/Wikidata using Text-to-SPARQL and LLM reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wikipedia and Wikidata are central to information access, LLM pre-training, and retrieval-augmented generation, but their knowledge is spread across text, tables, and knowledge graphs that sometimes disagree. There is no systematic way to detect and explain when these modalities conflict, which is crucial for data quality, model reliability, and knowledge auditing.

Method: The authors define a taxonomy of cross-modal knowledge inconsistencies, including differences in granularity, direct factual conflicts, temporal changes, and incompleteness in the knowledge graph. They then introduce KONTRAST, an automatic framework that converts table-based questions to SPARQL queries (Text-to-SPARQL), retrieves knowledge graph evidence, and uses LLM reasoning to compare table answers with KG results and classify the type of inconsistency observed.

Result: On multiple table question-answering datasets, KONTRAST finds that inconsistencies between tables and the knowledge graph are frequent and provide useful signals. The framework uncovers true factual conflicts, missing or incomplete KG structure, and temporal mismatches, while its performance is constrained by errors in the Text-to-SPARQL component and noise in the data.

Conclusion: Text, tables, and knowledge graphs each contain complementary aspects of knowledge, and their systematic cross-modal comparison can surface errors and gaps in any single modality. KONTRAST offers a practical approach to large-scale auditing of such resources and serves as a benchmark for future research on cross-modal knowledge consistency.

Abstract: Wikipedia and Wikidata are widely used for information access, LLM pre-training, and retrieval-augmented generation. Their knowledge is deeply connected but scattered across text, tables, and knowledge graphs. This raises a practical question: when these modalities disagree, how can we detect and explain the conflict? We study this problem as \emph{modality-level inconsistency detection}. We first introduce a taxonomy of cross-modal knowledge inconsistencies, covering information granularity differences, direct conflicts, temporal changes, and KG incompleteness. We then present \textsc{Kontrast}, an automatic framework that uses Text-to-SPARQL and LLM reasoning to compare table-based answers with KG evidence and categorize the resulting inconsistencies. Experiments on various Table-QA datasets show that cross-modal inconsistencies are common and informative. They reveal not only true knowledge conflicts, but also missing KG structure and temporal mismatches while being limited by Text-to-SPARQL errors and noise. Our analysis shows that text, tables, and KGs can complement and correct one another through systematic comparison. \textsc{Kontrast} provides a practical tool for large-scale knowledge auditing and establishes a benchmark for future work on cross-modal knowledge consistency. Code and data are available at https://github.com/ECLADATTA/KONTRAST.

</details>


### [39] [Instruction-Tuned Models Locally Reuse Human Syntax More Than Humans Do](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26015)
*Zandi Eberstadt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests whether large language models show syntactic convergence to human interlocutors, finding they do, especially for rarer syntactic constructions, but that instruction tuning changes the strength and character of this convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate if large language models, like humans, adapt their syntactic choices to their conversational partners, and how factors like model size and instruction tuning affect this behavior. Prior work documents syntactic convergence in humans but it is unknown if LLMs show analogous convergence at scale across many syntactic constructions.

Method: The authors use substitution-paradigm data: in natural human-human dialogues, they replace one speaker’s turns with outputs from various Llama and Gemma models (1B–70B, pretrained and instruction-tuned). They then measure how much each model reuses context-free grammar (CFG) rules from the immediately preceding human turn versus an unrelated human prime. They analyze overlap in CFG rules, focusing on frequency effects and comparing pretrained vs instruction-tuned variants. Exploratory analyses also compute lexical and semantic similarity between model outputs and the previous turn versus human responses, and compare similarity before and after instruction tuning.

Result: All 16 models show more CFG-rule overlap with the actual preceding human turn than with an unrelated human prime, and the difference is larger for rarer rules. Instruction-tuned models’ natural outputs have more overlap with the actual prime than the human responses they replace, and in every architecture pair, instruction tuning increases actual-prime overlap. However, instruction-tuned models also overlap more with unrelated primes, reducing the gap between actual and random overlap and lowering conditional rule-reuse odds once target rule-set size is controlled for. Exploratory analyses show that all models have higher mean lexical and semantic similarity to the preceding turn than human responders do. Instruction-tuned models show higher mean semantic similarity than pretrained versions across all architecture pairs, while lexical similarity changes are mixed.

Conclusion: Large language models display robust syntactic convergence to human interlocutors that resembles human-like adaptation and is particularly strong for low-frequency syntactic patterns. Instruction tuning strengthens some aspects of convergence (e.g., higher raw overlap and semantic similarity) but also makes models more globally aligned such that convergence effects, once baseline similarity is controlled for, can appear weaker or less selective. Overall, LLMs not only mirror but in some respects exceed humans in short-range syntactic, lexical, and semantic accommodation, though the nature of that accommodation depends systematically on training regime.

Abstract: Syntactic convergence (the tendency of speakers to adapt in language towards the grammatical profiles of their interlocutors) is a well-documented feature of human dialogue widely considered to operate below conscious awareness. Whether large language models exhibit analogous syntactic convergence toward human users relative to human baselines and across a broad range of syntactic constructions remains an open question. Using substitution-paradigm data in which model generations replace one speaker's turns in pre-existing human dialogues, this study measures turn-adjacent reuse of context-free grammar (CFG) rules across sixteen open-weight Llama and Gemma models (1B-70B, pretrained and instruction-tuned) at 1,901 matched positions per model. Every model showed greater CFG-rule overlap with the preceding human turn than with a sampled unrelated human prime, and in every model this actual-versus-random difference was larger for lower-frequency rules. Each instruction-tuned model also showed greater natural-output overlap with the actual prime than the human response it replaced, and all eight matched architecture pairs exhibited greater actual-prime overlap after instruction tuning. However, relative to pretrained variants, instruction-tuned outputs overlapped more with unrelated primes, showed a smaller actual-versus-random increment, and had lower conditional rule-reuse odds once target rule-set size was held constant. In exploratory analyses, each model exhibited greater mean lexical and semantic similarity to the preceding turn than the matched human responses did. Instruction-tuned models additionally produced responses with greater mean semantic similarity than their pretrained counterparts in all eight architecture pairs, whereas the lexical similarity results were more heterogeneous.

</details>


### [40] [UniMem: Complementary Episodic-to-Parametric Memory for Boundary-Agnostic Task Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26017)
*Siyu Xia,Chenheng Zhang,Yanting Wu,Haoxuan Li,Jiajun Chai,Xiaohan Wang,Guojun Yin,Wei Lin,Zhouchen Lin,Haifeng Zhang,Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UniMem is a self-routing memory framework for LLM agents that dynamically balances episodic (retrieval) and parametric (model) memory to handle long, evolving task streams without explicit task boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM memories face a stability-plasticity dilemma: retrieval memories quickly learn but are inefficient and fail to consolidate stable patterns, while parametric memories are efficient but need clear task boundaries and fixed parameter sizes. Real-world task streams are boundary-agnostic and evolving, so there is a need for an autonomous mechanism that can decide what to store episodically and what to consolidate into the model parameters, expanding capacity as needed without manual labels or fixed budgets.

Method: The paper proposes UniMem, which introduces learnable routing tokens that act as memory controllers. During deployment, these routing tokens determine whether an input corresponds to a novel/sparse task (stored and used via an episodic buffer with retrieval-augmented execution) or a recurring/reliable pattern (which is consolidated into parametric memory blocks). Task identification is decoupled from task execution: routing tokens handle task detection and selection of memory pathways, while execution is done via parametric memory blocks that can be expanded on demand, enabling autonomous memory management under streaming tasks.

Result: On long-horizon streaming task sequences, UniMem consistently outperforms baseline memory approaches in terms of exact match (EM) accuracy while preserving execution fidelity. Across three different backbone models, UniMem achieves an average improvement of 4.0 EM points, demonstrating its effectiveness in handling evolving, boundary-free task streams.

Conclusion: UniMem provides a brain-inspired, unified memory framework that adaptively coordinates episodic and parametric memory for LLM agents, mitigating the stability-plasticity dilemma in streaming task settings. By using routing tokens and expandable parametric memory blocks, it can autonomously expand memory capacity, consolidate stable behaviors, and maintain efficient execution without requiring explicit task labels or fixed parameter budgets during deployment.

Abstract: Memory is essential for LLM agents to accumulate task experience and reuse task-specific execution strategies. However, real-world deployment over boundary-agnostic and evolving task streams exposes a fundamental stability-plasticity dilemma. External retrieval-based memory can rapidly absorb new evidence, but it often fails to internalize recurring execution patterns and incurs inference-time retrieval overhead. Parametric memory enables stable and efficient execution once learned, but typically relies on explicit task boundaries and fixed parameter budgets. Inspired by the human brain, which balances plasticity and stability through complementary episodic storage and gradual consolidation, we propose UniMem, a self-routing framework for autonomous memory management. UniMem uses learnable routing tokens as memory controllers, enabling adaptive coordination between complementary memory pathways: novel or sparse tasks are retained in an episodic buffer for retrieval-augmented execution, while recurring and reliable patterns are consolidated into expandable parametric memory. By decoupling task identification from task execution with routing tokens and parametric memory blocks, UniMem expands memory on demand without task labels during deployment or uncontrolled parameter growth. Experiments on long-horizon streaming task sequences show that UniMem consistently outperforms baselines while maintaining execution fidelity, achieving an average gain of 4.0 EM points across three backbone models.

</details>


### [41] [Pass the Baton: Trajectory-Relayed On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26057)
*Haolei Xu,Xiaowen Xu,Haiwen Hong,Zixuan Ni,Hongxing Li,Yiwen Qiu,Weiming Lu,Yongliang Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes Relay On-Policy Distillation (Relay-OPD) to fix prefix failure in on-policy distillation for reasoning models by selectively handing off to the teacher mid-trajectory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on-policy distillation (OPD) fails when the student enters a wrong reasoning direction: later tokens are conditioned on an already-bad prefix, so supervision becomes unreliable and computation is wasted. There is a need to better align student training trajectories with effective teacher behavior while remaining label-free and compute-efficient.

Method: Identify a teacher-student continuation asymmetry on failed prefixes: when a prefix is bad, the teacher tends to redirect while the student continues the mistaken line. Use this asymmetry as a label-free trigger to decide when the teacher should temporarily take over generation. During training, construct "relay trajectories": the student generates its own tokens until a trigger is detected; then the teacher generates a short "teacher leg"; then the student resumes generation and is trained on the entire resulting trajectory. A limited relay budget prioritizes early, critical intervention steps while keeping trajectories close to the student policy.
