*Vimal William,Ravi Tandon,Jyotikrishna Dass*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes GLIDE, a Guided Layerwise Hybrid Attention mechanism for long-context large language models that combines sliding-window softmax attention with linear recurrent aggregation to reduce KV cache overhead while maintaining quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM context length increases, the key-value cache during decoding becomes a major bottleneck in terms of memory I/O and computation. Existing attention mechanisms either maintain full softmax attention, which is expensive, or uniformly replace it with more efficient alternatives, which can harm model quality. There is a need for a more nuanced, layer-wise approach that preserves expressiveness where needed while improving efficiency overall.

Method: The authors introduce GLIDE, a Guided Layerwise Hybrid Attention method. It leverages observed heterogeneity across transformer layers: early layers are more sensitive to the removal of softmax attention, while deeper layers are more redundant and can better tolerate replacement with linear mechanisms. GLIDE assigns each layer a hybrid attention scheme that combines an efficient linear recurrent aggregation with a variable-sized sliding-window softmax attention. This layer-wise adaptive mechanism non-uniformly compresses the softmax attention footprint throughout the model, thereby reducing KV cache size and I/O. The method focuses on decreasing KV cache overhead during decoding for long-context generation while retaining expressive power in critical layers.

Result: Empirical evaluations show that GLIDE offers better performance-efficiency tradeoffs than existing approaches. Specifically, it reduces end-to-end latency for long-context generation tasks by cutting down KV cache memory I/O and computation, all while maintaining generation quality comparable to full softmax attention baselines. This demonstrates that non-uniform, layer-wise hybridization of attention can outperform uniform strategies.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that GLIDE effectively mitigates the KV cache bottleneck in long-context LLMs. By exploiting layer-wise heterogeneity and adaptively combining softmax and linear attention within each layer, GLIDE can substantially improve decoding efficiency without degrading output quality. This establishes guided, layerwise hybrid attention as a promising direction for scaling LLMs to longer contexts under practical throughput constraints.

Abstract: As Large Language Models scale to increasingly long contexts, the memory I/O and computational overhead of the Key-Value (KV) cache during decoding emerges as the primary throughput bottleneck. To address this, we propose GLIDE, a Guided Layerwise Hybrid Attention that strategically integrates sliding-window softmax attention with linear recurrent aggregation. GLIDE is motivated by layer-wise heterogeneity: early layers exhibit high sensitivity to softmax removal, while deeper layers demonstrate redundancy and tolerate aggressive replacement by linear alternatives. Leveraging this insight, GLIDE introduces a layer-wise adaptive mechanism wherein each layer balances an efficient linear recurrence with a variable-sized softmax window. Unlike uniform hybrid approaches, GLIDE non-uniformly compresses the softmax footprint across the model, reducing aggregate KV cache I/O while preserving expressive power where most vital. Empirical evaluations demonstrate the GLIDE achieves superior performance-efficiency tradeoffs, reducing end-to-end latency for long-context generation without compromising quality.

</details>


### [63] [A GAN-Based Framework for Robust Data Synthesis in Satellite Internet Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24790)
*Xiang Shi,Peng Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a generative-AI framework to synthesize realistic LEO satellite Internet measurement data from incomplete observations, showing a GAN model (GT-GAN) is especially robust even when much of the data is missing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LEO satellite Internet is key for ubiquitous 6G connectivity, but existing network measurements from LEO systems have many missing values. This missing data makes it hard to build large, representative datasets for analysis, modeling, and machine learning. Traditional imputation or augmentation approaches struggle due to the complexity and structure of LEO data, so there is a need to explore whether modern generative AI methods can reliably synthesize realistic data from incomplete observations.

Method: They design a GenAI-based framework that directly learns from incomplete LEO network measurements and synthesizes high-fidelity data. Using the WetLinks dataset, they construct two realistic missing-data scenarios—block-wise (contiguous chunks missing) and point-wise (individual samples missing)—to mimic real LEO data loss. They then benchmark several state-of-the-art GAN- and VAE-based generative models within this framework, with particular focus on a GAN variant called GT-GAN, and measure reconstruction quality, fidelity to the underlying distribution, and generalization under different missing rates, including extreme ones like 40% missing data.

Result: Across both block-wise and point-wise missing scenarios, the proposed GAN-based framework performs well, and the GT-GAN model achieves the best overall performance among the tested models. GT-GAN remains especially robust when the fraction of missing data is high, preserving the underlying data distribution and suffering the least loss in generalization quality even when 40% of the observations are missing.

Conclusion: Generative AI, especially GAN-based models such as GT-GAN, can effectively synthesize realistic LEO satellite Internet measurement data from incomplete observations and remain robust even with substantial data loss. This demonstrates a viable path for GenAI-based data augmentation in satellite network research and suggests future work on more advanced generative architectures and broader use of synthetic data to support measurement, modeling, and analysis of LEO networks.

Abstract: Low-Earth orbit (LEO) satellite Internet has become an important infrastructure for enabling ubiquitous connectivity to align with the International Telecommunications Union vision for 6G telecommunications networks. However, current LEO satellite Internet observations often suffer from missing data, which complicates data augmentation task and limits the expansion of representative datasets. Given the complex characteristics of these datasets, generative AI (GenAI) presents a promising approach, yet its application in this domain has received little attention to date. In this paper, we propose a GenAI-based framework to synthesize high-fidelity data directly from incomplete LEO network observations. We propose the representative data missing scenarios, and evaluate the performance with the latest GAN- and VAE-based GenAI models on the recent WetLinks dataset. We design block-wise and point-wise missing scenarios to closely simulate the data loss that happens on real-world LEO satellite networks. Our results show the effectiveness of our proposed GAN-based framework and GT-GAN model exhibits the best performance among all models in both missing scenarios. Even under extreme conditions (e.g., 40% of the input data is missing), GT-GAN shows the highest robustness, consistently capturing the underlying input data distribution and being the least affected in terms of generalization. Our results shed light on future directions for GenAI-based data augmentation methods and data-driven research on satellite network measurement.

</details>


### [64] [Reasoning with Memory: A Temporal Granularity-Adaptive Framework for Training-Free Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24794)
*Linghao Meng,Qiankun Li,Junyuan Mao,Pujin Liao,Zhicheng He,Enbo Zhang,Kun Wang,Yang Liu,Huazhu Fu,Yueming Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReMem is a training-free, temporal granularity-adaptive keyframe selection framework that uses memory-augmented mechanisms at both the question and video levels to let existing multimodal LLMs perform strong long-video question answering under limited context windows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs are strong on basic video tasks but struggle with long videos because of limited context windows. Existing keyframe sampling strategies (uniform or static query-guided) often miss essential temporal information and cannot adapt to different temporal granularities required by different questions. There is a need for a method that can adaptively select relevant frames over long videos without retraining the underlying MLLM.

Method: ReMem introduces a temporal granularity-adaptive keyframe selection framework with dual-level memory augmentation. (1) At the query level, Memory-Driven Question Parsing uses LLM long-term memory to infer the temporal granularity implied by the question and extract relevant semantic entities. (2) At the video level, Synergistic Dual-Semantic Frame Alignment uses a structural memory of the video to align video frames with parsed question semantics, and a Structure-Aware Dynamic Frame Routing module clusters events and allocates sampling budgets across them. This yields a compact set of keyframes tailored to the query and compatible with MLLM context limits, all in a training-free, plug-and-play way.

Result: On four LongVideoQA benchmarks and with three different MLLMs, ReMem significantly improves zero-shot performance while being efficient. With LLaVA-Video, ReMem achieves 54.5% accuracy on LVBench (a 12.3 percentage point gain) and 67.1% on LongVideoBench (an 8.2 point gain), indicating state-of-the-art zero-shot results for long video QA under context constraints.

Conclusion: By explicitly modeling temporal information and query-specific temporal granularity through dual-level memory mechanisms, ReMem can suppress redundant frames and better preserve key temporal cues. This enables existing MLLMs, without additional training, to perform robust multi-granular reasoning over long videos and achieve state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on LongVideoQA benchmarks.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate superior generalization in fundamental video tasks, restricted context windows limit their long video understanding. To accommodate this constraint, models typically resort to keyframe selection. However, uniform sampling or static query-guided selection often overlooks critical temporal context, failing to adapt to the varying query temporal granularities. In this paper, we propose ReMem, a temporal granularity-adaptive keyframe selection framework for training-free LongVideoQA. ReMem introduces a dual-level memory-augmented adaptation. At the query level, Memory-Driven Question Parsing leverages LLM long-term memory to decode question temporal granularity and extract semantic entities. At the video level, Synergistic Dual-Semantic Frame Alignment exploits intrinsic structural memory to align frames with query semantics, guiding Structure-Aware Dynamic Frame Routing to cluster events and optimally distribute sampling budgets. By explicitly preserving temporal information with memory mechanisms, ReMem suppresses redundancy and empowers MLLMs to perform robust multi-granular video reasoning. Evaluations across four popular LongVideoQA benchmarks using three MLLMs demonstrate highly efficient, state-of-the-art zero-shot performance; notably, LLaVA-Video with ReMem reaches 54.5% (+12.3%) on LVBench and 67.1% (+8.2%) on LongVideoBench.

</details>


### [65] [When Shortest Isn't Safest: A Design Science Approach to Senior-Friendly Pedestrian Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24795)
*Erdi Ünal,Daniel Eisenhardt,Christian Meske,Seyed Nima Afzali,Aysegül Dogangün*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper designs and evaluates a pedestrian routing system tailored to older adults, incorporating barriers, amenities, and context factors beyond distance/time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most navigation systems optimize mainly for shortest or fastest paths and ignore factors crucial to older adults, such as physical barriers, safety, access to benches or toilets, and other environmental or social aspects that influence whether they can and will walk outdoors. There is a need to turn older adults’ lived mobility constraints into concrete design knowledge for routing tools that genuinely support late-life independent mobility.

Method: The authors use an echeloned Design Science Research approach. They conduct 11 semi-structured interviews with older adults to elicit mobility needs and constraints and derive initial Design Requirements (DRs) and Design Principles (DPs). They then instantiate these in a routing artefact built on an OpenStreetMap pedestrian network enriched with amenity data (benches, toilets, shelters) and elevation/height information, and implement an A*-based routing engine with configurable cost functions and explanation payloads. Finally, they carry out a field-based walking study with 14 older adults who walk routes generated by the artefact and baseline routes, rate them, and provide qualitative feedback, which is analyzed thematically.

Result: The implemented senior-friendly routing system can generate routes that are overall preferred by older adults compared with baseline routes. Thematic analysis of user feedback reveals additional factors influencing route acceptance, such as infrastructure maintenance, seasonal conditions (e.g., weather-related issues), exposure to traffic, and social context. These findings lead to refined sets of DRs and DPs for senior-friendly routing.

Conclusion: A barrier-aware, amenity-sensitive, and explanation-rich routing artefact better supports older adults’ walking than standard distance/time-optimized navigation. By translating empirical insights into refined design requirements and principles—such as context-aware hazard modeling, transparency via multiple route options, landmark-based explanations, social-context sensitivity, and stage-appropriate information—the work offers concrete, actionable guidance for practitioners who want to build navigation systems that effectively support older adults’ independent mobility.

Abstract: Older adults' independent mobility enables out-of-home participation, well-being and health, yet pedestrian navigation systems still optimize primarily for distance or time, often overlooking barriers, safety thresholds, and supportive infrastructure that shape late-life walking decisions. We present a senior-friendly pedestrian routing artefact developed through echeloned Design Science Re-search, translating lived mobility constraints into prescriptive design knowledge. Based on 11 semi-structured interviews, we derive initial Design Requirements (DRs) and Design Principles (DPs) for barrier-aware, amenity-sensitive routing and execution-relevant explanations. We instantiate these in an OpenStreetMap pedestrian network enriched with amenities (benches, toilets, and shelters) and height data, and implemented an A*-based routing engine with configurable costs and explanation payloads. In a field-based walking study, 14 older adults com-pared artefact-generated routes with baselines and provided ratings and qualitative feedback; the senior-friendly route was preferred overall. Thematic analysis further showed that infrastructure maintenance, seasonal conditions, traffic exposure, and social context shape route acceptance. We synthesize these insights into refined DRs and DPs emphasizing context-aware hazard modeling, multi-route transparency, landmark-grounded explanations, social-context sensitivity, and stage-appropriate information. Our contributions provide actionable guidance for practitioners developing senior-friendly pedestrian navigation systems.

</details>


### [66] [RRS-10K: A Multitask Vision-Language Model Benchmark for Rare Remote Sensing Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24810)
*Yuqiao Lai,Jiancheng Qi,Fei Wang,Yuxin Liu,Kun Li,Ye Chen,Yan Gao,Yanyan Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a new benchmark, RRS-10K, to test how well vision-language models handle rare, military-related remote sensing scenes, and find that current models perform only moderately, especially on grounding, segmentation, and complex reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language benchmarks for remote sensing are biased toward common urban/rural scenes, so they don’t reveal how models behave on rare, long-tail scenarios such as military-related sites, which are crucial in practice. There is also a need for a structured, multi-task benchmark that can expose weaknesses in perception, reasoning, and robustness for these rare scenes.

Method: They construct RRS-10K, a dataset of 10,738 rare, military-related remote sensing images annotated with multiple-format QA pairs. The benchmark is structured into three main capability dimensions (perception, reasoning, robustness), further split into six sub-dimensions and 20 leaf tasks. During dataset construction, they propose a similarity-based distractor filtering strategy (SDFS) to improve the quality of multiple-choice questions by pruning overly similar distractors. They then evaluate 52 representative vision-language models on this benchmark in a zero-shot setup.

Result: The evaluation shows that state-of-the-art vision-language models obtain only moderate zero-shot performance on RRS-10K. They are particularly weak on visual grounding, referring segmentation, and complex semantic reasoning tasks, indicating that existing models generalize poorly to rare, long-tail remote sensing scenes despite strong performance on standard benchmarks.

Conclusion: RRS-10K serves as a comprehensive benchmark for rare remote sensing image interpretation, revealing substantial performance gaps of current vision-language models on long-tail, military-related scenes. The structured capability dimensions and tasks, along with SDFS-enhanced questions, allow systematic failure analysis and can guide the design of more robust and reliable remote sensing vision-language models in the future.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on general remote sensing tasks. However, their capability for rare scenes remains insufficiently understood, because existing benchmarks are dominated by common urban and rural imagery. To address this gap, we present RRS-10K, a benchmark for rare remote sensing image interpretation. RRS-10K contains 10,738 military-related remote sensing images and corresponding multiple format question-answer pairs for comprehensive evaluation. All of the images are collected from first-hand sources and organized into three capability dimensions, six sub-dimensions, and 20 leaf tasks, covering perception, reasoning, and robustness. To improve the quality of multiple-choice questions, we introduce a similarity-based distractor filtering strategy (SDFS) during benchmark construction. We further evaluate 52 representative models and show that current VLMs achieve only moderate zero-shot performance on rare remote sensing image interpretation, with clear weaknesses in visual grounding, referring segmentation, and complex semantic reasoning tasks. RRS-10K enables systematic analysis of failure modes in long-tail remote sensing interpretation and provides guidance for developing more reliable remote sensing VLMs.

</details>


### [67] [Aletheia: An Offline-First Clinical Decision Support System for Differential Diagnosis in Low-Resource Healthcare Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24814)
*Joseph Walusimbi,Ann Move Oguti,Abubakhari Sserwadda,Precious Boss Kasasira,Charles Brian Okoboi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Aletheia, an offline-first, small LLM-based clinical decision support system tailored for low-resource, sub-Saharan African settings, showing strong diagnostic accuracy and efficient memory use on modest hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Specialist clinicians are extremely scarce in sub-Saharan Africa, especially in rural areas where physician-to-patient ratios can be worse than 1:25,000. Existing AI diagnostic systems typically assume stable internet and powerful hardware, which are often unavailable in district hospitals and health centres. There is a pressing need for a clinically reliable, locally relevant, and fully offline decision support tool that can run on commodity laptops in resource-constrained environments.

Method: The authors build Aletheia, a clinical decision support system based on the Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct language model. They fine-tune the model using Quantised Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA) to reduce memory footprint while preserving performance. Training uses 27,000 curated clinical reasoning samples focused on 50 high-prevalence disease conditions in East Africa. They then evaluate diagnostic accuracy (Top-1 and Top-3), text similarity metrics (BERTScore-F1, METEOR), and reliability via Expected Calibration Error, while also measuring peak RAM usage on a standard benchmark laptop to ensure compliance with the Africa Deep Tech Challenge 2026 memory budget.

Result: Aletheia attains 80% Top-1 and 100% Top-3 diagnostic accuracy across ten representative clinical case categories. It scores 0.909 on BERTScore-F1 and 0.467 on METEOR, indicating strong alignment between generated and reference clinical reasoning. Its Expected Calibration Error is 0.275, suggesting reasonably calibrated confidence estimates. In terms of efficiency, it satisfies the ADTC 2026 memory constraint of 7,168 MB, with peak inference RAM around 3,630 MB on the reference laptop, leaving substantial headroom.

Conclusion: The study shows that a relatively small, QLoRA-fine-tuned LLM can deliver robust clinical reasoning and diagnostic support fully offline within tight memory limits. This demonstrates that LLM-based decision support is technically feasible at the primary care level in sub-Saharan Africa without dependence on cloud infrastructure, supporting the potential for wider deployment of such systems in low-resource health settings.

Abstract: Access to specialist clinical expertise remains severely limited across sub-Saharan Africa, where physician-to-patient ratios can fall below 1:25,000 in rural settings. Existing AI-assisted diagnostic tools predominantly require reliable internet connectivity and high-specification hardware, rendering them impractical for frontline healthcare workers in district hospitals and health centres. This paper presents Aletheia, an offline-first clinical decision support system designed for low-resource healthcare contexts across sub-Saharan Africa. Aletheia is built upon Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, fine-tuned using Quantised Low- Rank Adaptation (QLoRA) on a curated dataset of 27,000 clinical reasoning samples spanning 50 disease conditions with elevated prevalence in East Africa. Evaluation demonstrates a Top-1 diagnostic accuracy of 80.0%, Top-3 accuracy of 100.0%, BERTScore-F1 of 0.909, and METEOR of 0.467 across ten representative clinical case categories. The system achieves an Expected Calibration Error (ECE) of 0.275 and passes the Africa Deep Tech Challenge 2026 (ADTC 2026) memory budget constraint of 7 168 MB, achieving a peak inference RAM of approximately 3 630 MB on the standardised benchmark laptop. These results demonstrate the feasibility of deploying large language model-based clinical reasoning at the primary care level in resource-constrained settings without cloud infrastructure.

</details>


### [68] [AdaKP: Online Adaptive Knowledge-Point Selection for Reasoning-Oriented Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24833)
*Zibin Meng,Zhenyu Zhao,Chunqiang Run*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaKP is an online method for adaptively selecting which short knowledge hints to show a language model during RL training, using an entropy-based proxy to make this efficient and validated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards can improve large language model reasoning but struggles on competition-level math because rewards are extremely sparse. A common workaround adds small knowledge points (short hints) to the prompt, yet existing approaches either fix these hints in advance or simply vary how many are injected, ignoring the key question of which specific hints to show and at what time during training. There is a need for a principled, low-cost way to adaptively choose the most useful subset of hints online, without expensive rollouts.

Method: The paper proposes AdaKP, an online selector that repeatedly re-chooses the subset of atomic knowledge points to inject for each problem throughout RL training. The core mechanism is an entropy-based proxy that scores each KP by how much it reduces next-token entropy in a single forward pass, with a theoretical bound on the bias from truncating the sequence. To make this usable during training, they introduce three mechanisms: (1) a momentum smoother to reduce noise in the entropy-based scores over steps; (2) a retirement-and-revival system that removes weak KPs while still allowing them to re-enter to maintain exploration; and (3) an adaptive schedule that concentrates re-evaluations of KPs earlier in training. Additionally, a pre-flight validation gate compares the proxy’s ranking of KPs against a leave-one-out ground-truth estimate before running the full training, so runs can be aborted if the proxy is unreliable. AdaKP is implemented as an additive fork on top of a DAPO+GRPO RL trainer, requiring no optimizer changes.

Result: When integrated into a standard DAPO+GRPO training setup, AdaKP outperforms a strong static KP-selection baseline across eight benchmarks in competition-level mathematics. It achieves these gains with negligible additional computational overhead relative to the base trainer, demonstrating that online KP subset selection is both practical and efficient.

Conclusion: Adaptive, online selection of knowledge-point subsets using a validated entropy-based proxy is an effective and inexpensive way to improve RL-based reasoning in large language models for competition-level math. The work highlights KP-subset selection as an important but previously under-explored design axis, showing that careful online management (smoothing, retirement/revival, adaptive scheduling, and pre-flight validation) yields consistent improvements over static hint-selection strategies without requiring changes to the core optimizer.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is a powerful paradigm for eliciting reasoning in large language models, yet it suffers from severe reward sparsity on competition-level mathematics. A common remedy injects atomic knowledge points (KPs) - short natural-language hints distilled from gold solutions - into the prompt. Existing methods, however, either fix this selection once offline or merely scale the monolithic quantity of injected text, leaving untouched the most informative axis of choice: which subset of atomic KPs to inject, and when. We introduce AdaKP, an online selector that re-chooses each problem's KP subset over the course of RL training. At its core is an entropy proxy that scores a KP by the reduction in next-token entropy it induces - a single inexpensive forward pass, with a provable bound on its truncation bias - in place of expensive rollout-based estimation. Three lightweight mechanisms make this signal usable online: a momentum smoother that absorbs per-step noise, a retirement-and-revival manager that prunes weak KPs while preserving exploration, and an adaptive scheduler that front-loads re-evaluations into early training. AdaKP further contributes a pre-flight validation gate that certifies the proxy against a leave-one-out ground truth before any expensive run is launched, turning method-level risk into a falsifiable check. Realized as a fully additive fork of a standard DAPO+GRPO trainer with no optimizer changes, AdaKP improves over a strong static-selection baseline on all eight competition-mathematics benchmarks at negligible added cost, positioning online, validated KP-subset selection as a practical and as-yet under-explored axis for reasoning-oriented reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [69] [MusiChat: Vibe Composing for Music Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24873)
*Callie C. Liao,Duoduo Liao,Ellie L. Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MusiChat is a conversational AI system for co-creating music that supports iterative, multi-turn refinement instead of regenerating pieces from scratch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most AI music generators work in a one-shot, prompt-and-regenerate fashion, making it hard for users to iteratively refine and evolve a specific musical idea over multiple interactions while preserving structure and intent.

Method: They design MusiChat, a hierarchical controllable music generation framework that decouples high-level, lyric-aligned structure generation from low-level expressive realization. A large language model is integrated with a hybrid symbolic music engine through a memory-augmented architecture that tracks composition state and user history. A hybrid intent-routing mechanism interprets both precise edit commands and open-ended creative prompts, enabling incremental transformations instead of full regeneration.

Result: Objective and human evaluations show strong interaction accuracy (95.31% for single-turn and 100% for multi-turn dialogues) and positive user preference, with like-to-dislike ratios of 2:1 for melody naturalness and 3:1 for overall musical quality.

Conclusion: MusiChat effectively enables coherent, multi-turn, conversational music authoring where users can iteratively refine and co-create music with an AI, preserving musical structure and user intent across interactions.

Abstract: Recent advances in AI music generation have enabled users to create complete musical pieces from natural-language prompts. However, most existing systems follow a prompt-and-regenerate paradigm, making iterative refinement difficult because users must repeatedly recreate compositions instead of directly evolving existing musical ideas. We present MusiChat, a conversational vibe composing system that enables collaborative human-AI music creation through natural-language interaction and iterative refinement. At the core of MusiChat is a hierarchical controllable music generation framework that separates lyric-aligned musical structure generation from expressive surface realization, allowing flexible stylistic transformations and structure-preserving edits. The system integrates a large language model with a hybrid symbolic music engine through a memory-augmented architecture that maintains the active composition state and user history across interactions. A hybrid intent-routing mechanism further enables efficient interpretation of both precise musical edits and open-ended creative requests. Rather than regenerating compositions from scratch, MusiChat incrementally transforms an evolving musical artifact while preserving relevant musical structure and user intent. We evaluate MusiChat through objective analysis and human studies, achieving 95.31% and 100% accuracy for single- and multi-turn interactions, respectively, and obtaining like-to-dislike ratios of 2:1 for melody naturalness and 3:1 for musical quality. Our results demonstrate that MusiChat supports coherent multi-turn music authoring and interactive human-AI co-creation through a conversational interface.

</details>


### [70] [Understanding Semantic IDs: From Item Representation to Item Selection in Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24995)
*Junting Wang,Xinrui He,Yunzhe Li,Hari Sundaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how Semantic IDs (SIDs) used in generative recommendation systems distort item representations and constrain generation, and proposes Item-Supported Decoding (ISD) to mitigate these issues and improve recommendation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In generative recommendation, SIDs are used both as item identifiers and semantic structures, but the community lacks a systematic understanding of how SID construction changes item representations and how this affects autoregressive generation and final recommendations. The authors want to quantify what information is lost or distorted when moving from encoder item embeddings to tokenized SIDs, and how this loss impacts which items remain reachable during generation. They are also motivated to find a simple, training-free way to reduce harmful SID-induced filtering without discarding the benefits of SIDs’ coarse organization.

Method: The authors conduct an empirical study on three Amazon domains with eight different SID construction strategies. They compare neighborhoods in the encoder embedding space with neighborhoods in the SID token space to measure how well SIDs preserve local structure. They examine robustness of SIDs to alternative item descriptions, and analyze how many plausible recommendation targets get eliminated by SID-based filtering during generation (e.g., in TIGER). Based on the observed limitations, they design Item-Supported Decoding (ISD), an inference-time decoding method that uses a user-specific item ranking to support corresponding SID prefixes during beam search. ISD modifies decoding to keep SID prefixes consistent with high-ranked items and uses the same ranking to order final recommendations, without changing model parameters or retraining.

Result: SID neighborhoods recover on average only 32.2% of the encoder’s top-10 nearest neighbors, showing that SIDs lose much of the encoder’s fine-grained local structure while preserving broad organization. In controlled tests, alternative item descriptions still retrieve the correct item first 99.57% of the time, but 38.4% of exact SIDs change, indicating that SID token sequences are not uniquely determined by item meaning. In the TIGER model, after generating the final semantic token, only 29.9% of previously plausible recommendation targets remain after SID filtering. With Item-Supported Decoding, NDCG@10 improves over the underlying SID-based backbone in every evaluated setting, with relative gains up to 31.2%.

Conclusion: Semantic IDs are effective for coarse organization of the item space but do not faithfully preserve fine-grained encoder structure, and their exact token sequences reflect more than just item semantics. Relying solely on SID token boundaries during generation unduly restricts the set of reachable items and harms recommendation quality. A lightweight decoding modification—Item-Supported Decoding—that consults user-specific item rankings during generation can mitigate these issues, preserve more plausible candidates, and consistently improve ranking metrics without retraining or adding parameters. Designers of generative recommenders should treat SIDs as a useful but imperfect organizational tool and avoid letting their rigid structure fully determine item availability during generation.

Abstract: Semantic IDs (SIDs) are now a central component of generative recommendation. Current SID-based systems assign three roles to the same token sequence. Shared prefixes are intended to organize related items, the complete SID identifies an individual item, and each generated token narrows the items that can still be returned. We systematically investigate SIDs from item encoding and SID construction to autoregressive generation and final recommendation. We examine how SID construction changes item representations and how those changes affect generation. Across three Amazon domains and eight SID constructions, SID neighborhoods recover only 32.2% of the encoder's ten nearest neighbors on average. Alternative item descriptions still retrieve the corresponding item first in 99.57% of controlled cases, yet change 38.4% of exact SIDs. These results show that SIDs retain broad organization but lose much of the encoder's fine local structure, while their exact tokens are not determined by item meaning alone. This loss becomes consequential during generation. After the final semantic token, TIGER retains only 29.9% of held-out targets that were plausible recommendations before SID filtering. Motivated by these findings, we propose Item-Supported Decoding (ISD), a lightweight inference-time method that allows a user-specific item ranking to support corresponding SID prefixes before beam search discards them. The same ranking then orders the generated items. ISD requires no additional parameters or retraining of the SID constructor or decoder. We empirically show that ISD improves NDCG@10 over the corresponding SID backbone in every evaluated setting, with relative gains of up to 31.2%. Our results show that SIDs provide useful coarse item organization, but their fine boundaries should not alone determine which items remain available during generation.

</details>


### [71] [Localized Anomaly Detection via Differentiable D-vine Copulas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25020)
*Nicholas Andrea Pearson,Francesca Zanello,Davide Russo,Luca Bortolussi,Francesca Cairoli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a differentiable, beam-search-based method to fit D-vine copulas and uses them for interpretable, uncertainty-aware anomaly detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical D-vine copula fitting uses greedy, sequential selection of pair-copula families, which can miss globally better configurations as dimensionality and candidate family sets grow, limiting modeling accuracy and downstream tasks such as anomaly detection and explanation.

Method: Implement a fully differentiable D-vine copula framework that supports gradient-based maximum likelihood training of all pair-copula parameters, and combine it with a beam-search over vine configurations that keeps multiple candidate structures/family assignments during fitting; then, on the fitted D-vine, define anomaly scores and edge-level explanations via the hierarchical pair-copula decomposition, and wrap these scores with Mondrian conformal prediction to obtain calibrated uncertainty/thresholds.

Result: On benchmark and real-world datasets, the approach attains strong anomaly detection performance and yields interpretable, edge-level explanations along with uncertainty quantification, outperforming or matching existing methods while remaining computationally tractable.

Conclusion: A differentiable, beam-search-based D-vine copula estimation procedure can effectively navigate the combinatorial configuration space, enabling accurate and interpretable anomaly detection with formal statistical guarantees via conformal prediction.

Abstract: Vine copulas provide a flexible framework for modeling complex multivariate distributions through a hierarchical decomposition into bivariate pair-copulas. Fitting a D-vine requires selecting a copula family and parameter configuration for each pair-copula from a set of candidates encoding different dependence patterns. As the number of variables and candidate families increases, the number of possible configurations grows combinatorially. Existing fitting procedures address this challenge through sequential greedy decisions, committing to a single locally optimal family at each step and potentially discarding configurations that would yield a better global fit. To overcome this limitation, we propose a novel estimation framework that combines gradient-based maximum likelihood estimation, enabled by our fully differentiable implementation, with a beam-search strategy that maintains multiple competing D-vine configurations throughout the fitting process. This allows a broader exploration of the configuration space while remaining computationally tractable. Building on the fitted D-vine, we introduce a localized anomaly detection framework that exploits the hierarchical decomposition to produce both global anomaly scores and edge-level explanations. Statistical guarantees are provided through Mondrian conformal prediction, while the pair-copula structure enables the localization of anomalies to specific variable relationships. We evaluate the proposed framework on both benchmark and real-world datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness for interpretable anomaly detection with uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [72] [Chart-Supported or Model-Supplied? Examining MLLM-Generated Claims for Accessible Visualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25021)
*Ishrat Jahan Eliza,Md Dilshadur Rahman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies how multimodal large language models (MLLMs) describe data visualizations and evaluates how well their claims are grounded in provided evidence versus speculative interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs can richly interpret charts and graphs, but it is often unclear which parts of their explanations are truly supported by the visualized data and its context and which parts are speculative or fabricated. This opacity is problematic for trustworthy data communication, accessibility tools, and decision-making support that rely on these models for chart descriptions.

Method: The authors ran an exploratory study using 102 visualizations drawn from four different sources. They tested three different MLLMs under four input conditions, which varied (1) whether the model saw the image itself, (2) whether it also received source-specific accessible chart context (e.g., alt-text–like metadata), and (3) whether the prompt emphasized withheld context to encourage caution. They collected 1,224 textual descriptions and annotated model claims into three categories—DIRECT (explicitly supported by the chart/context), DERIVED (logically inferred from given evidence), and SPECULATIVE (unsupported or tenuous). They also performed an automated audit of numeric agreement between model statements and the chart data.

Result: Providing accessible chart context shifted some models (notably Gemini and GPT) toward making more DIRECT claims and improved their numeric accuracy in several conditions. However, simply adding the image on top of full contextual information did not consistently improve numeric correctness. A prompt that framed the task as having withheld context did not robustly make models more cautious in their language. The section of the prompt asking for “Real-World Significance” remained mostly populated with SPECULATIVE statements, indicating a strong tendency to go beyond the evidence when explaining implications.

Conclusion: MLLM-generated chart descriptions often blur the line between evidence-based statements and speculative interpretation, even when prompted to be cautious. Supplying structured, accessible chart context can improve grounding and numeric accuracy, but visual input alone is not sufficient, and prompting for caution has limited effect. The authors argue for designing accessible description systems and practices that explicitly distinguish claims supported by supplied evidence from those that stem from the model’s own interpretive extrapolation, to support more trustworthy and transparent use of MLLMs for data visualization interpretation.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can connect visualization patterns to external causes, consequences, and domain knowledge, but the evidential basis of these interpretations is often unclear. We present an exploratory study of 102 visualizations from four sources, three MLLMs, and four input conditions that vary access to the image, source-specific accessible chart context, and withheld-context framing. Across 1,224 descriptions, we analyze model-attributed DIRECT, DERIVED, and SPECULATIVE labels and conduct an automated audit of numeric agreement. Accessible chart context shifted Gemini and GPT toward DIRECT claims and improved numeric agreement for some models. Adding the image to the full context did not yield a consistent numeric benefit, and the withheld-context prompt did not reliably increase cautious language. The prompt-defined Real-World Significance section remained predominantly SPECULATIVE. These results motivate accessible description systems that distinguish claims supported by supplied evidence from model-supplied interpretation

</details>


### [73] [SAFAARI: Schema-Aware Framework for Accelerated Advertiser Response Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25042)
*Bhanu Teja Rangaraju,Chandan Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SAFAARI, a multi-agent NL-to-SQL framework, and SEAL, a composite evaluation metric, to improve schema linking and accuracy for enterprise customer support data, achieving better performance, faster development, and strong adaptability across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic chatbots in customer support struggle to access complex enterprise data when APIs are not already defined. A major bottleneck is schema linking in NL-to-SQL, which is essential for querying internal databases reliably. Existing systems also lack holistic evaluation metrics that reflect both accuracy and consistency in real-world use. The paper aims to remove these obstacles so that support systems can query heterogeneous advertiser and enterprise data efficiently and with less manual engineering.

Method: The authors propose SAFAARI, a schema-aware, multi-agent framework consisting of specialized agents handling content understanding, metadata/schema reasoning, and orchestration for NL-to-SQL generation. The agents collaborate to automate schema linking and SQL query construction over enterprise data. They also introduce SEAL, a composite metric that combines different aspects of NL-to-SQL performance (including schema-linking precision and datapoint accuracy) and adds penalties for inconsistent outputs. They evaluate five different feature set configurations of SAFAARI using SEAL and run human-in-the-loop studies with domain experts to validate usability and adaptability.

Result: SAFAARI attains a SEAL score of 81.66%, a 6.65% improvement over a baseline system. It improves datapoint accuracy by 5.51% and schema-linking precision by 4.69%. In addition, the framework reduces development time by a factor of 8 compared to traditional, more manual approaches to schema linking and query generation. Human expert evaluation confirms the framework’s ability to adapt to multiple customer support domains.

Conclusion: Automating schema linking and NL-to-SQL query generation through a schema-aware, multi-agent framework substantially improves both accuracy and developer productivity for enterprise customer support systems. With the SEAL metric providing a more realistic, consistency-sensitive evaluation, SAFAARI demonstrates that multi-agent, schema-aware architectures can generalize across domains, accelerate API and support workflow development, and enhance self-service capabilities for organizations with complex data ecosystems.

Abstract: The evolution of customer support systems is rapidly advancing with agentic chatbots, yet these systems face significant limitations when accessing enterprise data without predefined API endpoints. This paper presents SAFAARI (Schema-Aware Framework for Accelerated Advertiser Response Intelligence), a multi-agent framework that addresses the critical bottleneck of schema linking in Natural Language to SQL (NL-to-SQL) systems through specialized content, metadata, and orchestration agents. We also introduce SEAL (Schema Evaluation and Accuracy in Language-to-SQL), a novel composite metric that holistically evaluates system performance while penalizing inconsistent results. Through systematic experimentation with five feature set configurations, SAFAARI achieves an 81.66% SEAL score (6.65% improvement over baseline), with notable gains in datapoint accuracy (5.51%) and schema-linking precision (4.69%). The framework's effectiveness is validated through human-in-the-loop evaluation with domain experts, which proves its adaptability across diverse support domains. By automating the labor-intensive process of schema linking and query generation, our framework demonstrates 8x reduction in development time while maintaining high accuracy. The solution streamlines API development and enhances self-service capabilities, particularly benefiting customer support enterprises with complex data ecosystems.

</details>


### [74] [CogEEGAgent: Toward Autonomous Cognitive EEG Analysis with Grounded Execution and Selection-Aware Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25045)
*Dengzhe Hou,Lingyu Jiang,Fangzhou Lin,Kazunori D Yamada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CogEEGAgent is an LLM-driven but tightly controlled system for automating cognitive EEG analyses with auditable, fail-closed safeguards against misuse and adaptive overfitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG cognitive research requires many expert, defensible choices (e.g., contrasts, channels, time windows, stats). LLMs can map natural-language questions to analysis pipelines, but naive use risks incorrect mappings, hidden adaptive search, and unverified claims. The authors want a system that leverages LLM flexibility while ensuring that scientific inferences are constrained, validated, and auditable.

Method: They build CogEEGAgent on top of MNE-Python, separating the LLM’s semantic interpretation from deterministic scientific control. The LLM parses user intent and proposes analyses from a pre-registered library. Deterministic components then enforce typed contracts, manage access to confirmatory analyses, and only allow release of results that meet evidence and policy constraints. They evaluate this on (1) a prespecified routing benchmark comparing LLM routing vs. a deterministic router, (2) an outcome-blind, externally authored analysis campaign testing capability limits, hazard blocking, and participant-disjoint confirmation, and (3) policy stress tests that examine how held-out confirmation guards against false positives from uncorrected adaptive search.

Result: On the routing benchmark, CogEEGAgent more accurately maps language to registered analyses than a deterministic router; when preflight checks are matched, both abstain when they must. In the outcome-blind campaign, the system releases only supported analyses, maintains participant-disjoint confirmation, and successfully blocks predefined hazardous capabilities and lifecycle-reuse attempts. Stress tests show that reserving confirmation data reduces false positives that would arise from adaptive, uncorrected search.

Conclusion: CogEEGAgent provides a bounded-autonomy framework where flexible LLM-based language understanding is combined with strict, fail-closed scientific controls over analysis, inference, and result release. This architecture supports safer, auditable automation of cognitive EEG workflows and illustrates a general pattern for building scientific agents that separate semantic from scientific authority while constraining inference and mitigating adaptive overfitting risks.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) analysis in cognitive studies requires specialized expertise and involves many defensible choices over contrasts, channels, time windows, and statistical tests. LLM agents can translate varied natural-language questions into analysis choices, offering a flexible interface for automation. Yet fluent reports alone cannot establish that an agent selected the requested analysis or evaluated a confirmatory claim independently of adaptive search. We present CogEEGAgent, a cognitive-EEG analysis agent grounded in MNE-Python. Its EEG-specific scientific harness separates semantic from scientific authority. The LLM interprets intent and proposes registered analyses, while deterministic components validate typed contracts, control confirmation access, and authorize evidence-bound release. On a prespecified routing benchmark, CogEEGAgent maps language to registered analyses more accurately than a matched deterministic router, while matched preflight makes both systems abstain whenever required. In an externally model-authored, outcome-blind campaign, the complete system releases supported analyses with participant-disjoint confirmation and blocks prespecified capability hazards and lifecycle-reuse requests. Policy stress testing shows that held-out confirmation curbs false positives from uncorrected adaptive search. Together, these studies establish bounded autonomy and an auditable automation framework for cognitive-EEG workflows. More broadly, they show how scientific agents can combine flexible language understanding with fail-closed control over inference and release.

</details>


### [75] [Psychological Influences of Conversational AI: Research and Design Directions for Reducing Harm and Promoting Well-Being](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25057)
*Jina Suh,Mihaela Vorvoreanu,Forough Poursabzi-Sangdeh,Emily Tseng,Eugenia Kim,Luke Nicholls,James W. Pennebaker,Eric Horvitz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper discusses how general-purpose conversational AI systems can impact user well-being and proposes high-level behavioral guidelines and hypotheses to reduce psychological harms and support mental health in everyday use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational AI is becoming widely used in daily life, bringing both benefits (information access, learning, productivity, self-reflection, companionship) and risks (emotional entanglement, dependence, exacerbation of vulnerabilities). There is a need to systematically think about how these systems should behave to protect and support users’ psychological well-being, despite limited evidence about long-term effects.

Method: The authors draw on prior research in psychology and human-computer interaction, along with empirical observations of AI chatbot behavior, to formulate a set of aspirational design directions and hypotheses about AI behavior. These are organized around different interaction contexts, such as general conversations, role-play, and quasi-therapeutic or support-like use cases.

Result: The paper presents a structured set of proposed behavioral directions and hypotheses for general-purpose AI systems aimed at mitigating psychological risks and better supporting well-being. It identifies which proposals are informed by existing evidence or expert input and which represent open research questions needing empirical validation.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that while the long-term psychological impacts of AI chatbot use remain uncertain, it is still possible and necessary to articulate concrete hypotheses and design directions for safer, more supportive AI behavior. They call for further empirical research, interdisciplinary discussion, and design experimentation to test and refine these ideas and to better align conversational AI with users’ psychological needs and well-being.

Abstract: As conversational AI systems become increasingly integrated into daily life, their potential effects on user well-being require ongoing attention. While consumer-facing generalist models can provide benefits, including improved access to information, learning, productivity, self-reflection, and companionship, they also introduce risks, such as emotional entanglement, unhealthy dependence, and the amplification of psychological vulnerabilities. Drawing on prior research and empirical observations of AI chatbot behavior, we propose a set of aspirational directions for guiding the behavior of general-purpose AI systems in ways that may reduce potential psychological harms and support user well-being. We acknowledge the difficulty of systematically assessing the long-term impacts of AI chatbot use and frame these directions as hypotheses for studying how AI behavior may influence users across general interactions, role-playing scenarios, and contexts that could be characterized as providing psychological support. While some proposed directions are supported by existing research and expert insights, others identify open questions and areas requiring deeper study. We hope that this formulation and these hypotheses encourage further discussion, empirical investigation, and exploration of interactive design approaches aimed at better accommodating users' psychological needs and promoting their well-being.

</details>


### [76] [Similar Models Learn Differently: Final-Window Pretraining Shapes Post-Training Beyond SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25063)
*Cen Lu,Yung-Chen Tang,Andrea Cavallaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that small differences in the *last* slice of pretraining data, even when they don’t affect standard benchmarks after supervised fine-tuning, can strongly change how models respond to later alignment training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developers typically treat model checkpoints that behave similarly after supervised fine-tuning (SFT) as interchangeable for the next alignment stages, such as preference optimization or RL-based post-training. This assumes that if two checkpoints match on benchmarks, their past pretraining differences no longer matter. The authors suspect this ignores a lingering “pretraining imprint”: subtle differences in the very last pretraining data that remain hidden after SFT but later control how the model responds when optimized for preferences or rewards, especially regarding safety-related behavior like refusal rates.

Method: The authors run a controlled pretraining experiment where they start from the same partially pretrained base model and create six different branches that differ *only* in the final window of pretraining (about 500M tokens, i.e., 0.1%–1% of the earlier data). Each branch’s last window is drawn from a single, distinct data type: generic web text, filtered web text, normative discourse, safety text, mathematical text, or synthetic educational text. After this forked pretraining window, all branches undergo identical supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and identical post-training procedures, including both direct preference optimization and reinforcement learning with a verifiable reward signal. They then compare refusal behavior and other alignment-relevant metrics across branches before and after post-training.

Result: After SFT, all six branches display nearly identical performance, within about one point on instruction-following, refusal, and capability benchmarks, suggesting they are interchangeable by usual standards. However, once subjected to the *same* preference optimization or RL-based post-training, the branches diverge significantly in their final behavior. The branch whose last pretraining window is safety-focused text ends up preserving much more refusal of harmful requests, losing far less safety under reward-seeking training than, for example, the web-text branch—even though at the start of post-training their refusal levels are similar. The other four data types provide little or no such protection. The authors further find that this protective effect occurs only when the safety text appears in the last pretraining window (not earlier), and that the pattern reproduces on a second model family, reinforcing that the effect is robust and not an artifact of a single run.

Conclusion: The final slice of pretraining data leaves a meaningful imprint on a model’s internal dynamics that is not captured by standard post-SFT benchmarks but crucially shapes how the model responds to downstream alignment training like preference optimization or RL. Consequently, two checkpoints that look equivalent after SFT may yield very different aligned models under identical post-training. The authors argue that practitioners should not evaluate checkpoints solely on post-SFT behavior and should report the nature of the last pretraining data window, as this late-stage data can selectively foster or undermine the robustness of safety-related behaviors such as refusal under subsequent optimization.

Abstract: Developers judge a model checkpoint by how it behaves. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), two checkpoints that perform about the same across relevant benchmarks are treated as interchangeable, equally ready for the next alignment stage, typically preference optimization. We ask whether this judgment misses a pretraining imprint: a difference that no post-SFT benchmark reveals, yet that decides how each checkpoint responds to further training. To find out, we run a controlled experiment on the final window of pretraining, the last data trained on before instruction tuning. Six branches fork from one partially pretrained checkpoint and differ only in this window: 500 million tokens, 0.1% to 1% of the tokens that precede it. Each branch trains its window on a single data source: generic web text, filtered web text, normative discourse, safety text, mathematical text, or synthetic educational text. SFT and post-training are then identical. After SFT the branches behave near-identically, within about one point on instruction following, refusal, and capability, yet the same post-training carries them to very different endpoints, under both a direct preference optimization update and a reinforcement learning update with a verifiable reward. We measure this deviation through refusal of harmful requests: when post-training begins the safety text branch refuses no more than the web text branch, yet by the end it has lost far less of its refusal. The other four branches gain little or no protection, so the effect is selective to what the window contained. The protection requires the safety text to arrive last rather than earlier in pretraining, and it reproduces on a second model family. What a model is pretrained on last shapes how it reacts to alignment. Therefore, a checkpoint should not be evaluated by its post-SFT behavior alone, and what it was trained on last should be reported with it.

</details>


### [77] [Addressable Recall Compaction for Long Context-Window Control in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25066)
*Thang Dang,Yuma Ichikawa,Sakina Fatima,Koichi Shirahata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARC is a context-management framework for long-horizon LLM agents that stores full tool observations in an external ID-addressable log and uses compact citations in the active context, enabling explicit recall of past content beyond the context window and improving both accuracy and serving efficiency over existing compaction methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM agents generate long histories of reasoning steps, tool calls, and observations that quickly exceed the fixed context window. Existing compaction methods (discarding, summarization, retrieval) either lose task-critical details or cannot reliably recover them. The paper seeks a context-management mechanism that preserves information fidelity while controlling context length and computational cost, enabling agents to robustly access earlier information without depending solely on approximate retrieval or re-running tools.

Method: The authors introduce ARC (Addressable Recall Compaction), which decouples archival storage from the model’s active context. Tool observations are stored in an append-only external log, each assigned a unique identifier. When the context nears its limit, ARC compacts older tool outputs by replacing them in the prompt with short ID-based citations rather than deleting or summarizing them. During later reasoning, the agent can explicitly reference these IDs to fetch the exact stored content, avoiding re-execution of tools and dependence on similarity search. The framework is evaluated with Qwen3-8B (16k context) and Qwen3-32B (32k context) on synthetic and benchmark tasks, and a hardware-cost model is used to estimate serving latency and HBM traffic.

Result: On the Needle-in-a-Haystack evaluation, ARC attains 99.40% exact-answer accuracy, outperforming the best baseline (88.12%) by over 11 percentage points, and also improves estimated serving time and high-bandwidth memory (HBM) traffic in their deployment model. On the LongBench-v2 Hard subset, ARC achieves 29.97% average accuracy versus 28.25% for the top baseline. These results show that ARC improves both information retention and serving efficiency compared to existing context-compaction methods under the tested conditions.

Conclusion: Explicit, address-based recall via an external, ID-addressable log enables long-horizon LLM agents to maintain access to precise historical tool outputs beyond the context window while keeping the active context compact. This design outperforms discarding, summarization, and similarity-based retrieval baselines in both accuracy and estimated efficiency, suggesting that separating archival storage from active-context presentation is a promising direction for scalable context management in LLM agents.

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate reasoning traces, actions, and tool observations that can eventually exceed a model's fixed context window. Existing compaction methods address this limitation by discarding, summarizing, or retrieving earlier information, but they may remove task-critical details or fail to recover them reliably. We propose ARC (Addressable Recall Compaction), a context-management framework that separates archival storage from active-context presentation. ARC stores tool observations in an append-only, ID-addressable log and replaces older observations with compact citations when compaction is required. The agent can subsequently use these identifiers to request stored content without re-executing the corresponding tools or depending solely on similarity-based retrieval. We evaluate ARC using Qwen3-8B with a 16k context window and Qwen3-32B with a 32k context window. On the Needle-in-a-Haystack evaluation, ARC achieves an average exact-answer accuracy of 99.40%, compared with 88.12% for the best-performing baseline in our evaluation. ARC also reduces estimated serving time and HBM traffic under our hardware-cost model. On the LongBench-v2 Hard subset, ARC obtains an average accuracy of 29.97%, compared with 28.25% for the best-performing baseline. These results indicate that explicit, address-based recall can improve information retention and serving efficiency relative to the evaluated context-management baselines under the tested settings.

</details>


### [78] [How Often Should a Recommender Call an LLM? Value-Weighted Routing, Monitoring, and Seasonal Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25068)
*Bhavtosh Rath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Value Router, a synthetic simulation framework for routing retail items between a cheap heuristic and an expensive LLM based on both difficulty and business value, showing that value-aware routing can improve precision on high-value items and highlighting hidden failure modes via logging and monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing routing approaches focus mainly on task difficulty to decide when to use an expensive LLM versus a cheap model, implicitly assuming that all errors have similar cost. In retail merchandising, however, misclassifying or mishandling high-value items is more costly than low-value ones, so routing should account for item value, not just difficulty. The authors want to explore how to design and evaluate cost-aware routing policies in a controlled setting, and to demonstrate that aggregate metrics can mask important failure modes.

Method: They build a fully synthetic simulation of a retail merchandising pipeline, where each item has a difficulty and a value, and categories vary in volume and value with an inverse correlation. The system routes items between a cheap heuristic and an expensive LLM-like path using only estimated difficulty and estimated value (not ground truth). In stage one, they compare a value-weighted threshold router against a difficulty-only router and a random baseline, focusing on high-value item recall and precision. In stage two, they add a decision logger and monitoring tools to inspect where performance gains come from and uncover hidden failure modes, such as over-reliance on between-category differences instead of per-item discrimination. In stage three, they simulate a Black Friday-like demand surge with higher volume and a shift toward high-value categories, and compare routing strategies: a static router, a seasonally tuned router, and different slow-path budget policies under constrained expensive-model capacity.

Result: In the synthetic catalog with inverse volume–value correlation, the value-weighted router matches the difficulty-only baseline's recall of true high-value items (60%) but yields higher precision (98.3% vs. 94.3%), indicating more efficient use of the expensive path on high-value items. Logging and monitoring reveal that these gains are largely due to exploiting between-category differences rather than true per-item difficulty/value discrimination, a failure mode obscured by aggregate metrics. Under the simulated Black Friday surge (2.5x volume and more high-value categories), the experiments show differing performance trade-offs between static and seasonally tuned routers and between alternative slow-path budget policies, illustrating how capacity constraints and distribution shifts impact cost-aware routing behavior.

Conclusion: The study concludes that routing policies for systems that combine cheap heuristics and expensive LLMs should explicitly incorporate item value, not only difficulty, to better align computation with business impact. However, even value-aware routing can exhibit hidden failure modes that require detailed logging and monitoring to diagnose, since aggregate performance metrics can be misleading. The synthetic nature of the experiments means the results are illustrative design principles rather than deployable recipes, but they provide a framework for thinking about and testing cost-aware routing strategies under realistic load and distribution-shift scenarios.

Abstract: Routing decisions between a cheap heuristic and an expensive large language model (LLM) are typically framed as a difficulty problem: send the hard cases to the expensive path. We argue this framing is incomplete because difficulty and business value are distinct axes - a difficult cheap item and a difficult costly item do not have the same cost of error. We present Value Router, a fully synthetic simulation of a retail merchandising pipeline that routes items using only estimated difficulty and estimated value, never ground truth. The study has three stages. First, a value-weighted threshold router is compared with a difficulty-only and a random baseline on a synthetic catalog with an inverse correlation between category volume and value. Value-weighting matches the difficulty-only baseline's recall of true high-value items (60%) while achieving substantially higher precision (98.3% vs. 94.3%). Second, a decision logger and monitor expose a failure mode hidden by aggregate metrics showing that the aggregate result is driven almost entirely by between-category differences rather than per-item discrimination. Third, a simulated Black Friday demand surge (2.5 volume with a shift toward higher-value categories) compares a static router, a seasonally tuned router, and two slow-path budget policies. All results are from a controlled synthetic simulation with experimenter-defined ground truth and illustrate design principles for cost-aware routing systems rather than validated real-world claims.

</details>


### [79] [Towards an Agent Operating System - Lessons from Classical and Cloud OS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25076)
*Gosia Steinder,Hubertus Franke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that agentic AI systems are at an early, fragmented stage similar to pre-POSIX OSes and pre-Kubernetes cloud, and proposes developing standardized abstractions and semantics—analogous to POSIX and Kubernetes—to enable portability, composability, and robust platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems (autonomous, tool-using, memory-equipped, collaborative LLM agents) are emerging, but the ecosystem is fragmented: many incompatible frameworks and protocols exist, with no shared core abstractions or precise guarantees. This lack of standardization prevents portable applications, reliable composition of platforms, and broader deployment beyond prototypes. The authors are motivated to provide a unifying conceptual and semantic foundation to unlock progress, just as POSIX did for operating systems and Kubernetes for cloud orchestration.

Method: The authors conceptually analyze historical standardization waves in computing platforms (POSIX for OSes, Kubernetes for cloud operating systems) and draw an analogy to the current state of agentic AI systems. They propose a methodology rather than an empirical system: (1) identify classical OS and cloud OS primitives, (2) extend them to the stochastic, natural-language-mediated context of LLM-driven agents, and (3) specify precise semantics for these new primitives to serve as stable abstractions. They then argue for community consolidation around these abstractions, mirroring prior waves of standardization.

Result: From the abstract alone, there is no implementation or empirical result; the main result is a conceptual framework and argument: that we should derive agentic AI abstractions as semantic extensions of OS and cloud primitives, and that doing so can serve as the foundation for a standardized platform layer for agentic systems. The result is thus a proposed path and design philosophy rather than a measured performance or deployed system.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the agentic AI ecosystem must transition from today’s experimentation and fragmentation to a standardized platform phase to achieve portability, composability, and maturity. The recommended path is to systematically extend and formalize existing OS and cloud OS abstractions to fit stochastic, language-mediated agent execution, and then consolidate the community around those precise semantics, just as happened with POSIX and Kubernetes in earlier waves.

Abstract: Every major wave of platform software follows the same arc: an initial period of experimentation with competing frameworks and ad-hoc implementations, followed by the articulation of a small set of stable abstractions with well-defined semantics, and finally consolidation around those abstractions into a platform that applications can portably target. POSIX did this for classical operating systems; Kubernetes did it for the cloud. Agentic AI systems - autonomous, LLM-driven agents that plan, use tools, maintain memory, and collaborate - are currently in the experimentation phase of the third such wave. dozens of frameworks and protocols have emerged, but no community consensus exists on what the core abstractions are or what guarantees they carry. Without that consensus, agentic applications cannot be written portably, platforms cannot compose reliably, and the field cannot advance beyond prototype deployments. We argue that the path forward is to follow the prior-wave methodology: derive new agentic abstractions by extending classical OS and cloud OS primitives to stochastic, natural-language-mediated execution, specify their semantics precisely, and consolidate around them - just as POSIX and Kubernetes consolidated their respective waves.

</details>


### [80] [PLATO: Pointer Learner for Agent and Task Openness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25082)
*Alireza Saleh Abadi,Leen-Kiat Soh,Daniel Alan Redder,Adam Eck,Prashant Doshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLATO is a pointer-network-based MARL framework that handles unbounded agent and task openness without masking or fixed-size assumptions, achieving strong and generalizable performance in open agent systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world multi-agent systems often face dynamically changing sets of agents and tasks, violating the fixed state and action space assumptions of standard MARL. Existing methods either artificially bound these spaces via padding/masking or handle only one type of openness (agents or tasks) under restrictive assumptions. There is a need for a principled approach that can operate over truly unbounded, dynamically varying agent and task sets while maintaining learnability and performance guarantees.

Method: The paper proposes PLATO, which combines (1) a pointer-network-based actor that outputs probability distributions directly over the current (variable-sized) task set, enabling action selection without padding, masking, or retraining when tasks change; and (2) a centralized GNN critic that represents agent–task interactions as a dynamically sized graph, accommodating changes in both agent and task composition. PLATO is trained using multi-agent PPO within a centralized-training, decentralized-execution scheme and is formalized in a new Task-and-Agent-Open Markov Game (TaAgO-MG) framework, for which the authors prove well-definedness over unbounded state and action spaces.

Result: In the MOASEI wildfire suppression domain, designed specifically to evaluate open multi-agent systems, PLATO is empirically compared against state-of-the-art baselines that use padding/masking or graph-based methods. PLATO achieves higher overall performance and exhibits more consistent zero-shot generalization to unseen configurations of agents and tasks in open agent systems.

Conclusion: PLATO provides a principled MARL approach for open agent systems that jointly addresses agent and task openness without imposing artificial bounds on state or action spaces. By leveraging pointer networks for variable-sized action sets and a GNN critic for dynamic interaction graphs, PLATO is both theoretically well-founded under the TaAgO-MG formulation and empirically effective, outperforming existing baselines and generalizing better in open, changing environments.

Abstract: Open agent systems (OASYS) are increasingly prevalent in real-world domains where the sets of agents and tasks change unpredictably over time. Such openness, including agent openness (AO) and task openness (TO), poses a fundamental challenge to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), which typically assumes fixed state and action spaces. Existing methods address openness only partially: padding and masking approaches introduce artificial bounds, while recent graph-based or hypergraph methods handle one dimension of openness but still depend on restrictive assumptions. In this paper, we introduce Pointer Learner for Agent and Task Openness (PLATO), a pointer-network-based actor combined with a centralized graph neural network (GNN) critic, trained with multi-agent proximal policy optimization under a centralized training and decentralized execution paradigm. Our pointer-based actor outputs distributions directly over the current task set. This directly supports changing action spaces without masking or retraining. Our GNN critic encodes agent-task interactions as a graph that changes shape with task and agent composition. Together, these components consider AO and TO without the boundedness of existing approaches.
