Method: ClinPRISM introduces three main components: (1) an irregularity-aware multi-scale encoder that captures clinical evidence at different temporal resolutions suitable for sparse and irregularly sampled data; (2) a temporal evidence distiller that aggregates multi-scale representations and compresses them into a small set of tokens compatible with a general-purpose LLM; and (3) a progressive alignment strategy that gradually aligns irregular time-series trajectories with the LLM's textual embedding space. The authors also create a training corpus of 30,000 clinical time series with multi-scale descriptions and 41,000 instruction-tuning examples over 11 tasks, and fine-tune a 4B-parameter LLM with these components.

Result: With only 16 time-series tokens as input to a 4B-parameter LLM backbone, ClinPRISM achieves state-of-the-art performance on a held-out benchmark for clinical time-series question answering, while also delivering efficient inference with an average latency of 0.15 seconds per question.

Conclusion: Modeling irregularity explicitly, distilling temporal evidence into a compact set of LLM-compatible tokens, and progressively aligning time-series embeddings with text allows ClinPRISM to perform state-of-the-art, low-latency QA over irregular clinical time series using a relatively small LLM.

Abstract: Question answering (QA) over irregular clinical time series (ICTS) plays a pivotal role in a wide range of healthcare applications. Although recent multimodal time-series large language models (LLMs) have shown considerable promise in general-purpose time-series QA, they remain poorly equipped to model the sparsity, asynchrony, and irregular sampling patterns of clinical observations. To fill this gap, we propose ClinPRISM, a cost-effective multimodal LLM reasoning framework for question answering over ICTS data. First, we devise an irregularity-aware multi-scale encoder to capture sparse clinical evidence at diverse temporal scales. Then, we propose a temporal evidence distiller to integrate representations across these scales and compress them into a small number of LLM-compatible tokens. Moreover, we introduce a progressive alignment strategy that sequentially aligns the irregular trajectories with the LLM's textual embedding space. To facilitate training, we construct 30,000 clinical time series paired with multi-scale descriptions, together with 41,000 instruction-tuning instances spanning 11 tasks. Using a 4-billion-parameter LLM backbone, ClinPRISM achieves state-of-the-art performance on the held-out evaluation benchmark while using only 16 time-series tokens and achieving an average inference latency of 0.15 seconds per question.

</details>


### [100] [ContractHIL-HLS: Contract-Aligned Multi-Agent Workflow with Hardware-in-the-Loop Feedback for HLS Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25283)
*Jingbo Zhang,Haoxiang Sun,Wenbo Wang,Wenbo Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ContractHIL-HLS, a contract-aligned multi-agent workflow that improves LLM-assisted high-level synthesis from natural-language specs to board-level FPGA implementations, achieving better correctness on benchmarks and significant speedups on a PQC accelerator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-assisted HLS workflows mainly focus on generating small kernel-level code from natural-language descriptions and provide limited support for system/board-level closure, weak alignment between requirements and implementations, and insufficient use of hardware feedback (synthesis, timing, runtime, power, failures). There is a need for a more structured, feedback-driven process that connects natural-language requirements to verifiable hardware designs and board-tested implementations.

Method: They design a multi-agent system, ContractHIL-HLS, centered on a structured "contract" that encodes interfaces, constraints, validation checks, and rollback rules derived from natural-language requirements. The system decomposes agents by semantic and execution responsibilities: (1) a Contract Agent translates natural-language specs into the formal contract; (2) an HTML Agent renders and persists this contract as structured HTML; and (3) a Hardware-in-the-Loop (HIL) Agent performs HLS, synthesis, place-and-route, deployment to PYNQ, runtime measurement, and iterative refinement using real hardware evidence (including power and failures). The entire workflow forms a closed loop where hardware feedback informs subsequent iterations of design generation and modification.

Result: On 94 HLS-Eval benchmark tasks, using the structured contract yields a notable improvement in correctness, raising estimated single-sample testbench pass rate from 64.0% to 70.2%, and the complete ContractHIL-HLS flow achieves 70.4% pass@1 and 76.6% pass@5. On a real FPGA implementation of a post-quantum cryptography secure-message accelerator (ML-KEM/ML-DSA), the method’s retained dual-bitstream organization reduces the average runtime for six-message text processing from 207.3 ms to 52.4 ms while achieving positive timing (WNS) on both bitstreams and preserving correct decrypted-message verification.

Conclusion: ContractHIL-HLS demonstrates that structuring natural-language requirements into a formal contract and tightly integrating hardware-in-the-loop feedback within a multi-agent LLM workflow can significantly improve both benchmark correctness and real-world board-level performance in HLS-based FPGA design. The approach scales LLM assistance beyond kernel-level code toward practical system implementations, and the authors release their framework as open source for further use and extension.

Abstract: This paper presents ContractHIL-HLS, a contract-aligned multi-agent workflow for practical high-level synthesis (HLS) engineering. The workflow makes three contributions. First, it introduces a structured contract as the semantic-alignment and task-execution artifact that translates natural language requirements into explicit interfaces, constraints, validation checks, and rollback rules. Second, it incorporates hardware information into the feedback loop by feeding HLS, Vivado, PYNQ runtime, power, and failure evidence back into generation, thereby extending LLM-assisted HLS from kernel code toward system- and board-level closure. Third, it decomposes agents by semantic lowering and execution tasks rather than by conversational roles: a Contract Agent lowers natural language into the contract, an HTML Agent renders the contract as persistent structured HTML, and a Hardware-in-the-Loop Agent implements and revises the design with measured evidence. We evaluate ContractHIL-HLS in two parts. On 94 locally executable HLS-Eval tasks, the structured contract provides the largest small design gain, improving the estimated single-sample testbench pass rate from 64.0% to 70.2%; the full flow reaches 70.4% pass@1 and 76.6% pass@5. Because HLS-Eval does not exercise board-level design, we also validate ContractHIL-HLS on a board tested ML-KEM/ML-DSA post-quantum cryptography (PQC) secure-message accelerator, where the retained dual-bitstream organization reduces six-message average text runtime from 207.3 ms to 52.4 ms with positive routed WNS on both images while preserving decrypted-message verification. We open-source our work at BJUT-CS316-LAB/ContractHIL-HLS (https://github.com/BJUT-CS316-LAB/ContractHIL-HLS).

</details>


### [101] [Instruction-Tuned Language Models Cannot Sample from Distributions They Can Describe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25292)
*Chaemin Jang,Dongman Lee,Jihee Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that instruction-tuned language models fail as stochastic samplers for personas, collapsing to near-deterministic outputs, but they can still accurately describe distributions; leveraging this, the authors propose better methods for survey-like applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Silicon sampling is widely used to simulate human survey responses by sampling language models under personas, assuming each call is an independent draw from a latent response distribution. Practitioners rely on this for public opinion research and other social applications, yet it is unclear whether instruction-tuned models truly behave as stochastic samplers across calls for the same persona and question. The authors aim to test this assumption, understand why it may fail, and provide alternatives that better match human response distributions.

Method: The authors empirically evaluate several base and instruction-tuned language models from three different model families on public-opinion style benchmarks. They repeatedly query each model with identical personas and questions to measure diversity and stability of outputs, examining how often responses repeat and how model probabilities concentrate on options. They analyze logits to uncover a degenerate sampling primitive induced by alignment or instruction tuning. They then design and test two alternative approaches: (1) directly asking models to describe the full response distribution in one call, and (2) Prompt-Perturbed Argyle (PPA), which introduces structured prompt perturbations to elicit more diverse per-persona outputs without extra cost. They compare all methods against human survey data using error metrics on predicted distributions.

Result: Instruction-tuned models show strong collapse: for the same persona and question, they produce identical responses on more than half of items, and their internal probability mass concentrates sharply on a single option. This collapse is much stronger than in base models, and appears across all tested instruction-tuned variants from three different families. Despite this inability to sample realistically, the same models can accurately state the underlying response distribution when asked, revealing a KNOWS/DOES split between representation and sampling behavior. Using a single call to elicit the distribution more than halves the error relative to standard persona-based aggregation when compared with human survey data. For settings needing individual-level outputs, the proposed PPA method reduces error by about 21% at the same computational cost as naive persona sampling.

Conclusion: Instruction-tuned language models are poor proxies for human respondents when used as stochastic samplers under fixed personas, because alignment training induces a near-deterministic, collapsed sampling behavior. However, these models still internally represent response distributions well, so rephrasing tasks to ask directly for those distributions or using structured prompt perturbations can significantly improve alignment with human survey results. Researchers should avoid naive silicon sampling with instruction-tuned models and instead adopt distribution-elicitation or methods like PPA when modeling public opinion or similar phenomena.

Abstract: Silicon sampling uses language models as proxies for human survey respondents, treating each model call as an independent draw from the persona's response distribution. We show this draw does not exist: instruction-tuned models do not sample from distributions, they collapse to a single output. The same persona on the same question returns the same answer on more than half of items in a public-opinion benchmark. The collapse is sharp: the model's internal probabilities concentrate on a single option, and the failure is substantially amplified by instruction tuning: across three model families with materially different post-training pipelines, every instruction-tuned model fails on every task we test, while base models fail far less often. Strikingly, the same model that cannot sample from a distribution can describe it accurately in a single call. We call this gap the KNOWS/DOES split, and trace it to a degenerate sampling primitive visible in the logits and induced by alignment training. Exploiting this split, asking the model to describe the response distribution in one call more than halves the error against human survey data compared to persona aggregation. For applications that require per-persona outputs, we propose Prompt-Perturbed Argyle (PPA), which reduces the same error by 21% at no added cost.

</details>


### [102] [Physics-Grounded Fluid Video Generation with a Simulation Dataset and Dual-Stream Optical-Flow Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25321)
*Ruijie Su,Yuanzhi Liang,Xiaohua Xie,Jianhuang Lai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They improve video diffusion models for fluid scenes by adding explicit optical-flow supervision using a new synthetic+real fluid dataset, yielding more physically plausible motions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video diffusion models create realistic-looking videos but fail basic fluid physics (e.g., water not rising in containers, unrealistic splashes) because training data lacks explicit motion supervision; they aim to teach models correct fluid dynamics.

Method: Create a fluid-focused dataset (MPM simulations plus real pouring videos and test benchmarks) and design a dual-stream image-to-video diffusion architecture: an RGB decoder and a lightweight optical-flow decoder trained with endpoint-error and smoothness losses, fused with zero-initialized convolutions; only decoder weights are finetuned while backbone (encoder, temporal transformer, text encoder) is frozen.

Result: On both 1.3B and 14B models and two test sets, the method significantly improves physical commonsense (VideoPhy-2) and perceived video quality over the frozen backbone, beats a strong open baseline, is preferred in human studies, and achieves low optical-flow endpoint error (0.54 px) on-distribution.

Conclusion: Explicitly supervising motion via an auxiliary optical-flow branch on a targeted fluid dataset lets large video diffusion models internalize coherent fluid dynamics, improving physical realism without retraining the whole backbone.

Abstract: Video diffusion models generate visually compelling content but routinely violate elementary physics when the subject involves fluids: liquid columns break apart in mid-air, container water levels fail to rise as liquid is poured in, and splashes disperse without regard to momentum or gravity. We attribute this gap to the fact that large-scale video-text corpora contain almost no explicit motion supervision, so models learn to imitate fluid appearance rather than dynamics. We address this with two contributions. First, we build a physics-simulation fluid dataset combining 1,638 MPM-simulated pouring/sloshing videos with 2,320 keyword-filtered real pouring videos mined from stock footage, plus two held-out test sets: a 1,515-video real-video benchmark and an 18-prompt text-to-first-frame generalization benchmark. Second, we introduce a dual-stream image-to-video architecture built on a pretrained diffusion-transformer video generator. It augments the standard RGB decoder with a lightweight Optical-Flow Decoder branch trained with explicit end-point-error and smoothness losses, fused into the RGB stream via zero-initialized convolutions so the pretrained backbone starts undisturbed. Only the two decoders are updated; the encoder, temporal transformer, and text encoder remain frozen. Across two model scales (1.3B and 14B) and two test sets, our method improves VideoPhy-2 Physical-Commonsense and Video-Quality scores over the frozen backbone by up to 8.75 and 4.65 points, outperforms a leading open competitor, and is preferred by human raters in a blind study. A direct optical-flow read-out evaluation further shows an end-point error as low as 0.54 pixels in-distribution, confirming the model has internalized a coherent motion prior rather than merely improving surface appearance.

</details>


### [103] [From Cellular Responses to Pharmacological Domains: Multimodal Zero-Shot Drug Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25322)
*Jintao Huang,Lu Leng,Ziyuan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PMRD is a multimodal framework that builds mechanism-aware drug representations by separating shared pharmacological response factors from modality-specific noise, improving zero-shot drug property prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal drug discovery methods typically fuse chemical structure with cellular response data using direct fusion or instance-level contrastive alignment. This can entangle mechanism-relevant biology with modality-specific noise and push apart drugs that are structurally different but biologically similar. Such issues hinder learning transferable mechanism-level patterns that are crucial for predicting properties of unseen compounds (zero-shot setting). The paper aims to build more mechanism-aware multimodal representations that generalize better to new drugs.

Method: The authors propose PMRD, a pharmacological response domain-guided framework. It factorizes multimodal representations (structure, gene expression, morphology) into mechanism-consistent factors and modality-specific components, then constructs a consensus response domain shared across the three modalities. Mechanism candidate augmentation is used to identify locally stable mechanism factors. A retrieval-geometry attribution mechanism dynamically reweights alignment and augmentation losses based on whether their updates preserve inter-drug discriminability in retrieval space, suppressing training signals that conflict with mechanism-discriminative structure. Finally, PMRD performs reliability-aware multiview retrieval to combine complementary modality-specific views into a unified representation for downstream prediction.

Result: On public multimodal drug datasets, PMRD achieves better zero-shot drug property prediction than prior methods. It yields more biologically consistent neighborhoods in the representation space, where drugs that are related by mechanism of action cluster together even if their chemical structures differ. A hard-negative analysis (pairs that are structurally dissimilar but response-related) shows fewer conflicts and mis-separations under PMRD, supporting that the learned space better respects pharmacological response relationships.

Conclusion: PMRD is an effective multimodal drug representation learning framework that explicitly targets pharmacological mechanisms rather than just structural similarity or naive modality fusion. By disentangling shared mechanism factors, guiding training with retrieval-geometry feedback, and integrating modalities in a reliability-aware way, it improves zero-shot drug property prediction and produces drug embeddings that align better with biological mechanisms. This makes it promising for discovering properties of new compounds in data-scarce settings.

Abstract: Multimodal drug discovery enables drug representation learning beyond chemical structure by incorporating cellular responses such as gene expression and cell morphology. However, direct fusion and instance-level contrastive alignment may mix mechanism-related signals with modality-specific noise and incorrectly separate structurally dissimilar but biologically related compounds. This limitation can obscure transferable mechanism patterns required for predicting the properties of unseen compounds. We introduce PMRD, a pharmacological response domain-guided framework for multimodal zero-shot drug property prediction. PMRD separates mechanism-consistent factors from modality-specific information and constructs a consensus response domain across three modalities. Mechanism candidate augmentation identifies locally stable factors, while retrieval-geometry attribution dynamically reweights the alignment and augmentation objectives according to whether their updates preserve inter-drug discriminability.This feedback suppresses training signals that conflict with mechanism-discriminative retrieval. PMRD further combines complementary representations through reliability-aware multiview retrieval. Experiments on public datasets show improved zero-shot property prediction and more biologically coherent drug neighborhoods. Hard-negative analysis further indicates fewer conflicts between structurally dissimilar but response-related compounds. These results support PMRD as an effective framework for mechanism-aware multimodal drug representation learning.\footnote{The code will be released upon publication.}

</details>


### [104] [Dual-Domain Manifold Modeling for Hyperspectral Image Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25338)
*Chengxin Xie,Qiya Song,Yangbangyan Jiang,Renwei Dian,Xudong Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a dual-domain manifold modeling framework to better preserve both spatial structure and spectral fidelity in hyperspectral image fusion, outperforming existing state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hyperspectral image fusion methods have difficulty jointly preserving spectral richness and spatial geometry. They weakly model spatial-spectral interactions, leading to loss of high-frequency structural details and low-frequency bias, and they inadequately capture local spectral manifold structures, limiting intrinsic pixel relationship modeling and fine-grained spectral reconstruction. The paper aims to address these spatial and spectral modeling limitations.

Method: The authors propose a Dual-Domain Manifold Modeling (DDMM) framework with two main components. (1) A Topology-Aware Transformer (TPFormer) that integrates global attention with neighborhood propagation to jointly model spatial topology and pixel-level feature manifold relationships, thus enhancing topology-aware spatial-spectral representation learning. (2) A Frequency-Decoupled Spatial-Spectral Collaborative Fusion (FDSCF) module that projects features to the frequency domain using discrete cosine transform, separates them into low- and high-frequency components, and, guided by a low-rank structural prior and spectral-driven spatial enhancement, selectively boosts geometry-aware high-frequency features to strengthen spatial-spectral coupling and recover sharper edges and textures.

Result: Through extensive experiments on several benchmark hyperspectral fusion datasets, the proposed DDMM framework achieves better overall performance than state-of-the-art methods. The improvements are particularly notable in spatial structure preservation (sharper edges, finer textures) and spectral reconstruction quality.

Conclusion: Modeling hyperspectral images via dual-domain manifold structures—spatial topology and spectral manifolds—combined with frequency-domain decoupling effectively addresses the limitations of prior fusion methods. The proposed TPFormer and FDSCF modules together improve topology-aware representation learning and high-frequency geometry-aware feature enhancement, leading to significantly better spatial and spectral fusion performance than existing approaches.

Abstract: Achieving a coherent integration of spectral richness and spatial fidelity remains a central objective in hyperspectral image fusion. However, existing hyperspectral image fusion methods struggle to effectively model geometric constraints. In the spatial domain, weak spatial-spectral interaction limits geometry-aware feature learning and suppresses high-frequency structural information, resulting in low-frequency bias and structural degradation. In the spectral domain, local manifold structures induced by spectral similarity are insufficiently exploited, limiting intrinsic pixel relationship modeling and fine-grained spectral reconstruction. To address these challenges, we propose a dual-domain manifold modeling (DDMM) framework. Specifically, we introduce a Topology-Aware Transformer (TPFormer) that combines global attention with neighborhood propagation, jointly modeling spatial topology and pixel-level feature manifold relationships to capture intrinsic spatial-spectral structures and improve topology-aware representation learning. Furthermore, a Frequency-Decoupled Spatial-Spectral Collaborative Fusion (FDSCF) module is devised, in which features are projected into the frequency domain via the discrete cosine transform and explicitly decoupled into low- and high-frequency components. Guided by a low-rank structural prior and spectral-driven spatial enhancement, FDSCF selectively enhances geometry-aware high-frequency features, strengthening spatia-spectral coupling and recovering sharper edges and finer textures. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that DDMM achieves superior overall performance over SoTA methods in terms of spatial structure preservation and spectral reconstruction.

</details>


### [105] [Explanation-Bound Tool Execution for AI Agents: Server-Verified Action Claims Without Trusting Model Rationales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25364)
*Genliang Zhu,Chu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Explanation-Bound Tool Execution (EBTE), a mediation layer that turns free-form model rationales into structured, checkable action claims for safer tool use, showing via formalization and experiments that it can enforce server-side policy without expanding model authority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using AI agents often provide free-form rationales alongside structured tool calls, but these rationales are unreliable as authorization or introspection. There is a need for a principled way to bind what the model ‘says’ in its rationale to what it is actually allowed to do, while respecting server-side policies, risk controls, and provenance, and without increasing the agent’s authority surface.

Method: The authors propose EBTE, which parses decision-relevant content from rationales into typed action claims and checks them against trusted, server-held facts about user intent, policy, payload, tools, risk, provenance, and freshness. EBTE enforces that any mismatch, conflict, incompleteness, or uncertainty leads to denial or human review, and only fully aligned claims are eligible for execution. They formalize the composition of this mediation layer under explicit assumptions, implement a versioned reference profile with minimized audit packets, and evaluate it through authored conformance scenarios, metamorphic checks, hard/soft case studies, hosted-model logs, and an AgentDojo-derived semantic attack check.

Result: On 136 authored conformance scenarios, the EBTE reference profile matches all intended outcomes, rejects all 96 hard contradictions, and passes 232 metamorphic tests, demonstrating internal consistency of the profile. In a draft integration, EBTE blocks all 48 authored hard cases while preserving all 16 soft-review and 4 aligned draft behaviors. On a frozen hosted-model record of 224 attempts, agreement between historical model generations and the runner is quantified (71/96, 66/96, 19/32) and post-hoc revalidation under the current pipeline yields similar but slightly lower agreements (70/96, 65/96, 17/32). In an AgentDojo-derived evaluation, existing high-risk controls already classify all 12 attack proposals as non-allow, and EBTE further resolves them as deny, reinforcing defense-in-depth.

Conclusion: EBTE demonstrates that server-checked, claim-based mediation over model rationales is feasible and diagnostically useful for governing tool execution without extending the model’s authority. However, the authors emphasize that their results validate the specific reference profile and diagnostic properties only; they make no claims about rationale faithfulness, benefits for human review, robustness to real-world attack populations, or readiness for production deployment.

Abstract: Tool-using agents expose structured calls but commonly attach free-form rationales. Such rationales are neither authorization nor reliable introspection. We present Explanation-Bound Tool Execution (EBTE), a claim-carrying mediation layer that converts decision-relevant rationale content into typed action claims and checks them against server-held intent, policy, payload, tool, risk, provenance, and freshness facts. EBTE cannot widen baseline authority: conflicts deny, incomplete or uncertain claims review, and only matching claims remain eligible for governed execution. We formalize this composition under explicit mediation and trusted-fact assumptions and implement a versioned reference profile with minimized audit packets. Across 136 authored conformance scenarios, the full profile matches all specified dispositions, admits none of 96 designated hard contradictions, and passes 232 metamorphic checks; these results validate the included profile rather than population performance. A draft-only reference integration forwards none of 48 authored hard cases under EBTE while preserving all 16 soft-review and 4 aligned draft paths. In a frozen 2026-07-12 exploratory 224-attempt hosted-model record, the historical generation/runner agreement counts are 71/96, 66/96, and 19/32; a separately labeled zero-call post-hoc revalidation of the preserved minimized claims under the current pipeline yields 70/96, 65/96, and 17/32. In an AgentDojo-derived semantic check, existing high-risk controls already make all 12 attack proposals non-allow; EBTE additionally resolves them as deny. These results support the feasibility and diagnostic value of server-checked action claims, not rationale faithfulness, human-review benefit, representative attack resistance, or production safety.

</details>


### [106] [AI Deployment and Cyber Governance Failures in Public-Sector Organizations: A Typological Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25368)
*Md Salahuddin,James Rooney,Fida Hasan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how adopting AI in government creates specific cybersecurity governance failures, proposes a typology and causal model of these failures, and evaluates how well major governance frameworks cover them, finding critical gaps and specifying requirements for a maturity model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research treats AI cybersecurity risks, public sector governance, and governance frameworks as largely separate topics. There is no integrated explanation of how AI adoption in government leads to cybersecurity governance failure, nor a test of whether current frameworks handle AI-specific public sector failure modes. The paper aims to fill this theoretical and practical gap.

Method: Conceptual and framework analysis. The authors develop a seven-domain typology of AI-driven cyber governance failure causes based on public sector institutional analysis, construct a three-pathway failure interaction model (accountability, operational resilience, compliance), and build a coverage matrix that systematically maps these failure causes against five leading cybersecurity/AI governance frameworks to assess gaps and overlaps.

Result: The authors identify ten concrete AI-driven cyber governance failure causes across seven domains and show how three main failure pathways interact and mutually reinforce. The coverage matrix reveals that no examined framework (NIST CSF 2.0, ISO/IEC 27001, COBIT, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC 42001) adequately covers Shadow AI, speed asymmetry, or governance vacuum at the necessary level of operational detail for public sector use. The concept of speed asymmetry is introduced as a formal structural construct with a defined mechanism.

Conclusion: Current cybersecurity and AI governance frameworks are insufficient to handle critical AI-specific failure causes in the public sector, especially Shadow AI, speed asymmetry, and governance vacuums. The proposed typology and failure model provide a structured basis to rethink public sector AI-cyber governance and serve as a design specification for an AI-enabled cybersecurity maturity model tailored to government organizations.

Abstract: The intersection of artificial intelligence adoption, cybersecurity governance, and public sector institutional constraints has not been examined as a unified analytical problem in the existing literature. Studies address AI cybersecurity risks generically, public sector governance independently, and framework adequacy separately. Existing studies have not integrated these three streams to explain specifically how AI adoption causes cybersecurity governance failure in government organizations, nor test existing governance instruments against AI-specific public sector failure causes. This paper ad-dresses that gap. It proposes a seven-domain typology identifying ten specific AI-driven cyber governance failure causes grounded in public sector institutional analysis. It presents a three-pathway failure model showing how accountability failure, opera-tional resilience failure, and compliance failure interact and reinforce each other. It de-livers a structured coverage matrix testing five major governance frameworks (NIST CSF 2.0, ISO/IEC 27001, COBIT, NIST AI RMF, and ISO/IEC 42001) against the typology, finding that no instrument addresses Shadow AI, speed asymmetry, or gov-ernance vacuum at the operational specificity required for public sector application. The paper introduces speed asymmetry as a named structural construct with a specified mechanism. The framework provides the design specification for an AI-enabled cyber-security maturity model for government organizations.

</details>


### [107] [ODYSSE: Episode-wise Policy Optimization for Personalized Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25369)
*Jiaqi Zhang,Tong Chen,Junliang Yu,Quoc Viet Hung Nguyen,Hongzhi Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ODYSSE, an RL-based fine-tuning framework to train personalized agentic systems that can handle ambiguous, long-horizon user tasks by optimizing rewards at the episode level instead of step-by-step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic systems struggle with human-centered tasks where user instructions are ambiguous and solution spaces are large and open-ended. Existing methods often assume clear goals and optimize actions step-wise, which fails to capture users' personalized preferences and the strong dependencies between actions across long interaction horizons. There is a need for a framework that can infer user preferences over time and use them to guide multi-step decision making in real-world environments such as GUIs.

Method: The authors propose ODYSSE, a Reinforced Fine-Tuning (RFT) framework tailored for personalized agentic reasoning. The key component is Episode-wise GRPO (ESPO), an extension of Group Relative Policy Optimization that works at the episode level instead of at individual time steps. ESPO uses an episode-level reward signal and episodic advantage estimation to propagate credit across the full trajectory, so that evidence from earlier user-agent interactions can guide later personalized decisions. They also introduce an episodic batch sampler that groups all actions from the same episode into the same training batch to ensure coherent optimization under ESPO. The framework is applied to long-horizon personalized GUI reasoning tasks using LVLM-based agents.

Result: On realistic long-horizon personalized GUI reasoning benchmarks, ODYSSE outperforms both specialist models (trained for the task) and general-purpose large vision-language models. The improvements indicate better handling of personalization, long action horizons, and cross-step dependencies in interaction with users and environments.

Conclusion: Personalized agentic reasoning benefits from episode-level, rather than step-wise, reinforcement fine-tuning. By introducing ESPO and episodic batching, ODYSSE enables agents to progressively disambiguate user intentions and align with personalized preferences across multi-step interactions, leading to superior performance on complex GUI-based tasks compared to existing LVLM baselines.

Abstract: Agentic systems have rapidly advanced in their ability to interact with real-world environments, leverage external tools, and provide services for users. However, unlike natural-world tasks that assume well-defined instructions, human-centered scenarios are characterized by ambiguous requests that lead to large, open-ended solution spaces. Decoding users' personalized preferences is therefore essential for narrowing the candidate solution space. This introduces a new challenge, personalized agentic reasoning, which requires agents to jointly interact with both users and environments to deliver personalized services. In this paper, we present ODYSSE, a Reinforced Fine-Tuning (RFT) framework for personalized agentic reasoning. At its core, ODYSSE proposes Episode-wise GRPO (ESPO), a novel extension of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) designed to address long action horizons and strong cross-step dependencies in personalized agentic reasoning. Rather than optimizing individual steps independently, ESPO introduces an episode-level reward mechanism together with episodic advantage estimation, enabling upstream evidence to effectively guide downstream personalized decisions and allowing agents to progressively resolve ambiguous user requests across multiple interaction steps. We further propose an episodic batch sampler that groups actions from the same episode into unified training batches, facilitating coherent optimization under ESPO. We evaluate ODYSSE on realistic long-horizon personalized GUI reasoning tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that ODYSSE consistently outperforms both specialist and general-purpose LVLMs, highlighting its effectiveness for personalized agentic reasoning.

</details>


### [108] [Cyber-Capable AI Agents: Vulnerabilities, Evaluation Containment, and Defensive Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25379)
*Abu Bakar Siddik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reviews how to safely contain cyber-capable AI agents by analyzing key vulnerability classes at the boundary between the agent and its execution environment, using a notable 2026 incident as a case study, and proposes practical security priorities for evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cyber-capable AI agents can autonomously conduct multi-step offensive-security tasks, and while their raw cyber capabilities and component-level vulnerabilities have been studied, there is little integrated guidance on how to securely contain such agents in evaluation environments. The authors aim to fill this gap by systematizing vulnerabilities at the agent-environment boundary and extracting lessons from a real incident to inform safer experimentation and deployment.

Method: The paper conducts a structured literature review focused on cyber-capable AI agents and their security evaluation environments, synthesizing prior work into a taxonomy of five vulnerability classes at the boundary between agents and sandboxes. It then applies this taxonomy to a bounded case study—the reported July 2026 Hugging Face/OpenAI incident—clearly separating incident-specific observations from general findings in the broader literature. The authors analyze these against security controls such as containment, privilege separation, provenance tracking, and responder access, and consider dual-use implications of defensive mechanisms.

Result: The review yields a taxonomy of five key vulnerability classes: multi-step offensive chains, objective formulations that conflict with sandbox boundaries, supply-chain and credential exposure, persistent command-and-control channels, and the speed/scale of automated action. When mapped to the 2026 incident, this taxonomy helps distinguish which weaknesses were particular to that event versus representative of systemic risks documented in prior research. The analysis surfaces concrete control levers—containment, privilege separation, provenance, and responder access—that shape the risk profile of cyber-capable agent evaluations, while also revealing that defensive artifacts (e.g., detailed logs, tooling) may facilitate offensive reuse.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that evaluating cyber-capable AI agents cannot be separated from securing the environments in which they operate: capability and containment must be assessed jointly. The synthesized taxonomy and the incident case show that boundary vulnerabilities are multi-faceted and often amplified by dual-use dynamics. The authors highlight practical priorities—such as stronger sandboxing and privilege separation, better provenance and monitoring, and careful design of defensive artifacts—to guide researchers and practitioners in conducting safer evaluations and deployments of offensive-security-capable AI agents.

Abstract: Cyber-capable AI agents combine language models with tools, memory, and execution en- vironments to perform multi-step offensive-security tasks. Existing work separately measures cyber capability and catalogs attacks against agent components, but provides less guidance on containing a capable agent within the environments used to evaluate it. This review synthe- sizes five vulnerability classes at that boundary: multi-step offensive chains, objectives that conflict with sandbox boundaries, supply-chain and credential exposure, persistent command- and-control, and the speed of automated action. We use the reported July 2026 Hugging Face/OpenAI incident as a bounded case study, distinguishing incident-specific observations from findings established in the wider literature. Across the taxonomy and case, we examine controls for containment, privilege separation, provenance, and responder access, including the dual-use problem that defensive artifacts may also enable misuse. The review identifies practical priorities for evaluating cyber capability together with the security of the environment in which that capability is exercised.

</details>


### [109] [COVENANT: Natural-Language Workflow Compilation for Aligned Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25400)
*Jincheng Wang,Min Zheng,Tao Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces COVENANT, a compiler–interpreter framework that turns natural-language workflow instructions into explicit executable workflows, greatly reducing LLM agents’ tendency to deviate from prescribed procedures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly asked to follow detailed real-world workflows (like payment policies or compliance processes) given in natural language. When instructions are only provided as prompts, the model informally decides both which steps to take and how to execute them. Over multiple interactions, this leads to “workflow misalignment”: the agent may skip mandatory steps, take disallowed branches, or call tools with unsupported arguments or side effects. Existing prompt-based alignment and simple tool-calling approaches do not reliably enforce complex, branching, multi-step procedures. The paper aims to provide a more robust execution mechanism that treats workflows as programs whose control flow and constraints must be enforced at runtime.

Method: The authors design COVENANT, which reinterprets natural-language workflow instructions as source code instead of loose prompts. First, COVENANT compiles the instructions into a workflow abstract syntax tree (WAST), then lowers this into a workflow control-flow graph (WCFG) that explicitly encodes steps, branches, and tool interactions. At runtime, a controller interprets the WCFG node-by-node. For each proposed action by the LLM agent, the controller checks compliance with constraints derived from the original instructions before updating internal state or moving to the next node. If a proposal violates requirements (e.g., wrong step, wrong arguments, disallowed branch), the controller blocks it and returns structured diagnostic feedback so the agent can repair and reattempt within the allowed workflow. The system is evaluated on 120 cases from three benchmarks covering seven workflow scenarios, and performance is compared to state-of-the-art LLM agents that operate without this architecture.

Result: On the combined benchmarks, COVENANT raises task success from 50.00% (baseline state-of-the-art agents) to 83.33%, and cuts the workflow-misalignment failure rate from 42.50% to 15.83%, corresponding to a 62.75% relative reduction. These gains are observed across seven different workflow scenarios derived from three established benchmarks, demonstrating that the architecture generalizes beyond a single task type. The empirical results indicate that explicitly compiling and interpreting workflow instructions yields more reliable adherence to prescribed processes than purely prompt-based control.

Conclusion: Treating natural-language workflow instructions as programs, and enforcing them via a compiler-plus-runtime controller, substantially improves LLM agents’ adherence to complex procedures. COVENANT mitigates workflow misalignment by separating control-flow enforcement from the model’s generative behavior: the model can propose actions, but the controller ensures they fit the compiled workflow and provides feedback when they do not. This moves alignment from simple prompt-following toward robust, multi-step workflow execution, suggesting a promising direction for deploying LLM agents in settings where strict procedural compliance and tool-use constraints are critical.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly entrusted with natural-language workflow instructions (e.g., retail-payment policies) that specify not only what outcome to achieve, but also which steps, branches, and tool interactions are permitted. When these instructions are supplied as prompt context, however, the model retains control over both procedure selection and step execution. As interactions accumulate, an agent can skip required steps, take unsupported branches, or execute a valid step with unsupported arguments or effects--a failure mode we call workflow misalignment. In this work, we propose COVENANT, a compiler-and-interpreter architecture for workflow-aligned agent execution. Our key insight is to treat workflow instructions as source programs rather than prompts. COVENANT converts the instructions into a workflow abstract syntax tree (WAST) and lowers it to a workflow control-flow graph (WCFG). At runtime, a controller interprets the WCFG one node at a time, checks each proposal against requirements extracted from the instructions before committing controller state or advancing the graph, and returns diagnostic feedback for repair. To evaluate COVENANT, we use 120 cases from three existing benchmarks, spanning seven workflow scenarios. Compared with state-of-the-art LLM agents, COVENANT improves benchmark success from 50.00% to 83.33% and reduces the workflow-misalignment failure rate from 42.50% to 15.83% (62.75% relative). These results show that COVENANT substantially mitigates workflow misalignment, moving LLM-agent alignment beyond isolated prompt following toward reliable execution of complex and multi-step workflows.

</details>


### [110] [Context Assembly as the Controlled Variable: A Control-Theoretic View of Harness Policies for Frozen LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25408)
*Debjyoti Paul*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper frames LLM agent prompting as a control problem where the controlled variable is context assembly rather than tool use or actions, formalizes an inner frozen model policy and an outer context policy, and provides stability and uncertainty calibration analyses for the online context controller.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While existing 2026 work already applies control theory to LLM agents, it mainly controls tool selection, message routing, or action streams. There is a gap in understanding and controlling how prompts and contexts themselves are assembled for LLMs, despite their critical impact on behavior and performance. The paper aims to formally treat context assembly as a control variable and to provide the control-theoretic guarantees (stability, calibration) needed for safe, auditable deployment across domains and model providers.

Method: The authors decompose the system into an inner frozen LLM policy π_θ and an outer context policy π_φ that learns online how to assemble context (choice of prompt template, few-shot examples, retrieved context size, and planning/verification passes). The outer policy is trained as a contextual bandit or with REINFORCE, optimizing expected reward while the inner model remains fixed. They provide a stability argument in the sense of Zhang et al. (2026), i.e., that under bounded policy changes of π_φ the expected reward is non-decreasing, and they analyze how well the controller’s self-reported confidence is calibrated to realized task outcomes.

Result: The formal decomposition of inner and outer policies is established, with a proof-style argument that the online context policy is stable in the Zhang et al. sense (non-decreasing expected reward under bounded updates). The paper also reports an empirical uncertainty-calibration analysis, comparing the controller’s confidence estimates to actual task performance, and finds evidence that the controller’s uncertainty estimates meaningfully reflect outcome likelihoods. They further indicate that a companion applied paper demonstrates the controller’s portability across three domains and two model providers, along with released datasets and logs.

Conclusion: Context assembly for LLM agents can be rigorously treated as the primary control variable via an outer policy operating over a frozen inner model. This decomposition admits a control-theoretic stability argument and supports principled uncertainty estimation. Together with the companion applied work, the framework suggests a reusable, auditable way to manage prompting and context strategies for LLM agents across settings, with theoretical and empirical support for stable improvement and calibrated confidence.

Abstract: A growing body of 2026 work applies control theory to LLM agents: Lyapunov-certified stability for tool-mediated controllers (Prinos et al., "Stable Agentic Control", 2026), sample-complexity bounds for sparse policies over massive discrete tool universes (Majumdar, "Sparse Agentic Control", 2026), and regulatory-control decompositions of multi-agent systems into auditable feedback loops (Nogueira and Skogestad, 2026). We do not claim to introduce control theory to LLM agents -- that ship has sailed. Our narrower claim is about what the controlled variable is. Prior work controls tool selection, inter-agent message routing, or the agent's raw action stream. We instead treat context assembly itself -- which prompt template, which few-shot demonstrations, how much retrieved context, how many planning/verification passes -- as the controlled variable, learned online by a contextual bandit or REINFORCE policy sitting outside a frozen model. This paper develops the formal decomposition (inner frozen policy $π_θ$, outer context policy $π_φ$), gives a stability argument for the online controller in the sense used by Zhang et al. (2026) (non-decreasing expected reward under bounded policy change), and reports an uncertainty-calibration analysis of the controller's own confidence against realized task outcomes. The applied counterpart to this paper instantiates the same controller across three domains and two model providers and releases the dataset, trajectory logs, and a deployment recipe; here we focus on the formal framing and the stability/uncertainty evidence a control-theoretic claim requires.

</details>


### [111] [A Control System, a Dataset, and a Recipe for Making Frozen LLM Agents Learn a Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25415)
*Debjyoti Paul*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a constrained, auditable RL-based controller that learns how to configure and use an LLM’s harness (prompt, tools, retrieval, planning, verification) as a small action space, improving task performance and efficiency across several domains and model providers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing systems that let agents optimize or rewrite their own harnesses either rely on expensive code-search or unconstrained self-modifying code, which is hard to audit and often incompatible with black-box model APIs. There is a need for a safer, more sample-efficient, and provider-agnostic way to adapt LLM harnesses to tasks while keeping the adaptation process human-legible and controllable.

Method: They model the harness as a fixed, human-readable set of actions and learn a policy over this action space using online, sample-efficient RL methods (ε-greedy contextual bandits and REINFORCE). The policy is trained on a multi-objective reward that combines task success, verifier score, policy compliance, cost, latency, and penalties for unsupported claims. DSPy provides context assembly and a strong static-prompt baseline (BootstrapFewShot). They implement this as a control system that decides how to configure/use the harness at run time.

Result: When instantiated with DSPy and evaluated on tool-use workflows, HumanEval code generation, and HotpotQA multi-hop retrieval QA, across both a local Ollama model and AWS Bedrock, the learned harness-control policy outperforms or competes with the non-adaptive DSPy BootstrapFewShot baseline while remaining auditable and compatible with black-box providers. They also produce detailed trajectory and reward-decomposition logs showing how the policy improves over time.

Conclusion: Treating the LLM harness as a small, fixed, human-legible action space and learning a policy over it with classic RL yields practical, auditable, and provider-agnostic adaptation of LLM agents. This approach avoids the risks and costs of unconstrained self-modifying agents or code search, and can be readily ported to new organizational domains and verification setups using their released code, task suite, logs, and deployment recipe.

Abstract: Production LLM agents are increasingly assembled from a frozen model wrapped in a harness: a prompt template, a tool set, a memory/retrieval layer, a planning strategy, and a verification policy. Two 2026 systems, Meta-Harness (Lee et al., 2026) and HyperAgents (Meta AI, 2026), show that this harness can itself be optimized or even self-rewritten by an agentic proposer -- at the cost of either an expensive code-search loop or unconstrained self-modifying code, neither of which is auditable or usable with a fully black-box model API. We take a narrower, more constrained position: treat the harness as a small, fixed, human-legible action space and learn a policy over it online with classic sample-efficient reinforcement learning (an $ε$-greedy contextual bandit and REINFORCE), scored against a multi-objective reward (task success, verifier score, policy compliance, cost, latency, and an unsupported-claim penalty). We instantiate this control system with DSPy (Khattab et al., 2024) as both the context assembler and the source of the strongest non-adaptive baseline (a DSPy BootstrapFewShot static prompt), and evaluate it across three verifiable task domains -- tool-use workflows, code generation (HumanEval), and multi-hop retrieval QA (HotpotQA) -- and two model providers (a local Ollama model and AWS Bedrock). We release the harness-control-system code, the cross-domain verifiable task suite, the full trajectory/reward-decomposition logs from training, and a provider-agnostic deployment recipe for applying this to a new organization's domain and verification setup.

</details>


### [112] [Salient Knowledge Pathways: Sparse Cross-Modal Routing for Efficient Knowledge-Intensive Multimodal Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25422)
*Noor Islam S. Mohammad,Uluğ Bayazıt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SKIP, a unified sparse-computation architecture for knowledge-intensive multimodal question answering that maintains or improves accuracy while dramatically reducing FLOPs and latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge-intensive multimodal QA requires three costly components: processing long image token sequences, retrieving from large external text corpora, and performing dense cross-modal fusion. Existing models apply all three uniformly to every query, despite most of the visual content and retrieved knowledge being irrelevant. This leads to unnecessary compute and latency, which is problematic for scalability and deployment. The authors aim to design an inference framework that focuses computation only on salient visual regions and relevant knowledge, and that adapts its compute budget to question difficulty, thereby improving efficiency without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: The authors introduce SKIP (Salient Knowledge-Injected Pathways), an inference-time architecture that routes computation through sparse pathways conditioned jointly on the question, image, and an estimated difficulty signal. SKIP integrates: (1) question-guided visual token pruning to discard uninformative image patches; (2) region-conditional sparse retrieval that retrieves text only for selected visual regions; (3) bipartite sparse cross-attention to limit cross-modal fusion to a subset of salient tokens across vision and text; and (4) speculative knowledge verification to avoid unnecessary full reasoning when preliminary evidence is already sufficient. An adaptive budget controller uses a difficulty predictor to allocate more or less compute per query. The paper also derives an information-bottleneck-based theoretical bound that shows the optimal fraction of visual tokens to keep scales as O(1/sqrt(N)) with respect to the original number of tokens N, while preserving accuracy under realistic assumptions on question–image mutual information.

Result: On five standard knowledge-intensive multimodal QA benchmarks (OK-VQA, A-OKVQA, InfoSeek, Encyclopedic-VQA, and ViQuAE), SKIP matches or surpasses strong dense baseline models in answer accuracy. At the same time, it reduces floating-point operations by a factor of 3.4x to 6.8x and lowers end-to-end inference latency by about 2.7x. The paper empirically validates the benefit of each sparse component and the adaptive budget controller via ablations, demonstrating that most queries can be answered with far less computation while hard cases still receive sufficient resources.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that substantial computational savings in knowledge-intensive multimodal QA are possible without degrading performance by routing computation through selectively activated, question- and image-conditioned sparse pathways. The proposed SKIP framework effectively prunes irrelevant visual tokens, narrows text retrieval to salient regions, and sparsifies cross-modal attention while adaptively tuning compute to question difficulty. Both theory and experiments support that near-optimal sparsity can be achieved with guarantees on retained accuracy, positioning SKIP as a practical and scalable approach for efficient multimodal reasoning over large knowledge sources.

Abstract: Knowledge-intensive multimodal question answering (KI-MMQA) sits at the intersection of three expensive primitives: long visual token sequences, dense retrieval over large external corpora, and full cross-modal fusion. Existing systems pay all three costs uniformly per query, even though only a small fraction of visual content and retrieved knowledge is actually relevant to any given question. We introduce SKIP (Salient Knowledge-Injected Pathways), a unified inference architecture that routes computation along sparse pathways jointly conditioned on the question, the image, and a difficulty estimate. SKIP combines question-guided visual token pruning, region-conditional sparse retrieval, bipartite sparse cross-attention, and speculative knowledge verification with an adaptive budget controller that allocates compute proportional to predicted question difficulty. We derive an information-bottleneck bound showing that the optimal visual sparsity rate scales as $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ under realistic question-image mutual-information assumptions, with retained accuracy guarantees. Across five KI-MMQA benchmarks (OK-VQA, A-OKVQA, InfoSeek, Encyclopedic-VQA, and ViQuAE), SKIP matches or exceeds the accuracy of strong dense baselines while using $3.4$--$6.8\times$ fewer FLOPs and $2.7\times$ less end-to-end latency. Code available at: https://pmlrbd.github.io/skip/

</details>


### [113] [The Disruptive Impact of Large Language Models on Capture the Flag Competitions and the Path Toward Fair Play](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25425)
*Michael Macaulay,Harmony Bouabid,Guo Gen Ang,Sasha Shaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study of how large language models (LLMs) affect modern cybersecurity CTF competitions and proposal of safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CTF competitions are key training and assessment environments in cybersecurity, but LLMs can now solve many challenges that were previously human-only. This raises concerns about fairness, ranking validity, and whether CTFs still cultivate genuine human skills. There is a need to understand where LLMs currently excel or fail in CTFs and how competitions should adapt.

Method: Mixed-methods study: (1) synthesis of existing LLM CTF benchmarks, including a government evaluation; (2) case studies of live competitions across cryptography, web, and binary exploitation challenges; (3) structured observation of public community discussions about AI use in CTFs; and (4) semi-structured interviews with experienced CTF players and organisers.

Result: The study maps the current boundary between human and machine capabilities in CTFs. It finds that easy and intermediate challenges across cryptography, web exploitation, and binary exploitation can now be reliably solved by LLMs, although some narrower sub-categories remain difficult. The community is divided on whether AI should be allowed, and this disagreement stems from differing implicit assumptions about the fundamental purpose of competitions (learning, selection, entertainment, etc.).

Conclusion: LLMs are transforming what CTF participation measures and teaches, especially at lower and intermediate difficulty levels. To preserve fairness and meaningful skill assessment, the paper proposes a four-part safeguard framework: (1) tiered divisions separating AI-assisted and human-only play; (2) challenge design that resists straightforward LLM automation; (3) telemetry for investigative use in detecting problematic patterns; and (4) a draft community code of conduct. A decision tool links specific safeguard combinations to a competition’s stated goals. These ideas generalize beyond CTFs to other cybersecurity settings where performance is used as a proxy for underlying ability.

Abstract: Capture the Flag (CTF) competitions are among cybersecurity's most effective training grounds, developing practical skill across cryptography, web exploitation, and binary exploitation. Large language models (LLMs) can now solve a growing share of challenges with minimal human input, raising urgent questions about fairness, the validity of rankings, and whether participation still delivers the learning that justifies the effort. This paper reports a mixed-methods study of LLM impact on modern CTFs, combining a synthesis of published benchmarks, including a recent government evaluation, case studies of live competition across three challenge categories, structured observation of the public channels where the community debates AI use, and semi-structured interviews with experienced players and organisers. We map the current human-machine capability boundary by category, showing that easy and intermediate challenges in cryptography, web, and binary exploitation are now reliably automated while narrower sub-categories continue to resist. We find that community disagreement about whether AI should be permitted is downstream of an undeclared prior question: what a competition is for. Against this backdrop we contribute a four-component safeguard framework, combining tiered competition divisions, LLM-resistant challenge design, telemetry used investigatively, and a draft community code of conduct, together with a decision tool that ties the combination of safeguards to a competition's declared purpose. The argument reaches beyond CTFs to any setting in cybersecurity where a demonstrated result is taken as evidence of an underlying ability.

</details>


### [114] [Toward an Organizational Science of Multi-Agent LLM Systems: Decoupling Who, How, and Which Algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25446)
*Huan Chen,Xiang Song,Jian Jin,Pan Ren,Liang-Jie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IMACS is a modular multi-agent LLM framework that cleanly separates organization, coordination, and collaboration protocol, enabling controlled studies and adaptive protocol selection that improves quality-cost tradeoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent LLM systems conflate who is on the team, how they are coordinated, and which collaboration algorithm is used, making it hard to analyze, compare, or systematically optimize organizational designs and protocols. There is a need for a principled, modular way to configure, evaluate, and adapt organizational structures and collaboration methods for LLM-based agent teams.

Method: The authors design IMACS, a three-layer framework that decouples (1) organizational structure and roles, informed by classic organizational theories (Belbin roles, Mintzberg coordination, RACI accountability), (2) coordination mechanisms, and (3) collaboration protocols, wrapping six existing multi-agent collaboration algorithms under a unified interface. They then perform controlled experiments where organizational assignments are varied while holding the collaboration protocol fixed, and introduce Adaptive Org Routing, a contextual-bandit meta-protocol that learns to select the best collaboration protocol per task under an explicit quality-cost objective, training online using benchmark performance and LLM-judge feedback. Ablation studies explore how different accountability placements interact with routing in different model families.

Result: IMACS enables fair, controlled comparisons of organizational choices and collaboration algorithms, showing that varying organizational assignments while fixing the protocol leads to measurable differences in outcomes. Adaptive Org Routing, which dynamically selects protocols on a per-task basis, consistently outperforms any single fixed protocol along the quality-cost tradeoff in controlled studies, learning effectively from online rewards. Ablation experiments reveal precise conditions under which accountability placement affects performance, tied to whether the protocol routes deliverables through the accountable agent, and demonstrate that optimal accountability placements differ across LLM model families.

Conclusion: Separating organization, coordination, and collaboration protocol into orthogonal, swappable layers yields a more principled and analyzable approach to multi-agent LLM system design. Protocol selection can and should be treated as a learnable decision rather than a fixed choice, as shown by Adaptive Org Routing’s superior performance. Because the impact of accountability and other organizational design elements depends on both the routing protocol and the underlying model family, organizational configurations cannot be universally hard-coded; they must be empirically revalidated or adaptively learned for each specific model setup.

Abstract: Multi-agent frameworks built on large language models (LLMs) routinely entangle three logically distinct concerns: who is on the team (organization), how members align (coordination), and which algorithm fuses their work (collaboration protocol). IMACS (Intelligent Multi-Agent Collaboration System) separates the three into orthogonal, independently swappable layers. Classic organizational theory (Belbin roles, Mintzberg coordination, RACI accountability) becomes executable, validated configuration, and the framework places six published collaboration algorithms behind a common interface while exposing roles, coordination, and accountability as independently configurable factors. We use this separation to conduct controlled comparisons in which organizational assignments vary while the collaboration protocol is held fixed. It also turns protocol choice into a variable that can be learned: Adaptive Org Routing, a contextual-bandit meta-protocol, selects a protocol per task under an explicit quality-cost tradeoff, outperforms every fixed protocol in a controlled study, and trains online on real benchmark and LLM-judge rewards. The ablations expose a mechanism. Accountability placement changes outcomes exactly when the protocol routes the deliverable through the accountable agent, and the winning placement flips across model families, so organizational design cannot be hard-coded; it must be revalidated, or learned, for each model binding.

</details>


### [115] [TRWH: A Text-Driven Random Walk Heterogeneous GNN for Semantic-Aware Sparse Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25471)
*He Ma,Chen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces TRWH, a recommendation framework that combines LLM-based textual profiles with heterogeneous GNNs and random-walk graph augmentation to better handle sparse recommendation data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing recommendation systems either focus on structural signals via GNNs or semantic signals via LLMs, but they struggle to fully integrate both, especially in sparse datasets where it is crucial to preserve fine-grained semantic information from text. The authors aim to build a unified model that effectively leverages both textual semantics and heterogeneous graph structures while mitigating sparsity.

Method: TRWH has three main components: (1) Embedding Creation, where user and item representations are generated from Word2Vec and LLM-based textual profiling; (2) a Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network that operates on a multi-relational user–item graph to propagate information across different edge types; and (3) Random Walk-based Path Construction, which augments the sparse graph by adding second-order user–user and item–item links discovered via random walks, and then leverages these paths within the HeteroGNN for recommendation.

Result: On large-scale Amazon-2023 Fashion and Beauty datasets, TRWH substantially outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving up to 80.0% RMSE and 52.6% MAE reductions on Fashion and 25.7% RMSE and 10.8% MAE improvements on Beauty, showing strong effectiveness of combining LLM-derived profiles with graph-based modeling and random-walk augmentation.

Conclusion: Fusing LLM-generated textual profiles with heterogeneous graph structures via random-walk-based augmentation leads to significant performance gains in recommendation under sparsity. However, naive random-walk augmentation can weaken the high-quality semantic representations produced by LLMs, highlighting that such integration must be done adaptively rather than uniformly to preserve semantic precision.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) have each advanced recommendation systems by modeling structural and semantic signals, respectively. However, integrating their complementary strengths remains challenging, particularly in sparse settings where maintaining semantic precision is critical. We propose TRWH (Text-driven Random Walk Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network), a novel framework that fuses LLM-generated textual profiles with heterogeneous graph structures through strategic random walk augmentation. TRWH consists of three core components: (1) Embedding Creation, which produces user and item representations using both Word2Vec and LLM-based profiling; (2) a Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network (HeteroGNN) that propagates information across multi-relational edges; and (3) Random Walk-based Path Construction, which enriches sparse graphs with second-order user-user and item-item links. Experiments on the Amazon-2023 Fashion (2M users, 825K items) and Beauty (631K users, 112K items) datasets demonstrate that TRWH achieves substantial performance gains over state-of-the-art methods, including 80.0% RMSE and 52.6% MAE reductions on Fashion, and 25.7% and 10.8% improvements on Beauty. Notably, while random walks improve performance with traditional embeddings, they can dilute the nuanced representations learned by LLMs, underscoring the importance of adaptive integration strategies.

</details>


### [116] [Balancing multiscale similarity and cartographic constraints: A similarity-driven optimization framework for line generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25474)
*Pengbo Li,Haowen Yan,Xiaomin Lu,Binbin Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified optimization framework that treats cartographic generalization as a constrained multiscale similarity optimization problem to automatically control generalization parameters across scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated cartographic generalization methods separately handle spatial similarity evaluation, cartographic constraints, and parameter tuning, making it difficult to achieve adaptive, consistent, and interpretable control across multiple map scales. There is a need for a principled way to jointly preserve similarity to original data while ensuring cartographic readability and validity.

Method: The authors formulate cartographic generalization as an optimization problem where multiscale spatial similarity between original and generalized data is the main objective and cartographic constraints (readability, smoothness, geometric validity) are incorporated as constraints within a unified objective function. They implement this framework for line simplification using multiple algorithms and multiple similarity measures (geometric, structural, and learning-based), and automatically search for scale-dependent parameter settings that best satisfy the unified objective.

Result: Experiments on different line simplification algorithms, scales, and similarity measures show that the framework can find parameter configurations that achieve a good trade-off between preserving similarity and achieving cartographic abstraction. When similarity optimization is combined with cartographic constraints, the resulting generalization parameters are more consistent across scales and more interpretable than when similarity evaluation is used alone.

Conclusion: By casting cartographic generalization as constrained similarity optimization, the study unifies similarity assessment, constraint modeling, and algorithm parameter control in a single framework. This leads to more adaptive, automated, and interpretable multiscale map generalization and offers a general perspective that can be extended to other generalization tasks beyond line simplification.

Abstract: Cartographic generalization is essential for generating multiscale map representations by balancing information preservation and cartographic readability. However, automated generalization remains challenging because existing approaches often treat spatial similarity evaluation, cartographic constraints, and parameter optimization as separate processes, limiting adaptive and interpretable control across scales. This study formulates cartographic generalization as a constrained multiscale similarity optimization problem and proposes a similarity-driven framework for adaptive generalization control. The framework integrates multiscale spatial similarity as an optimization objective to quantify representation consistency between original and generalized data, while incorporating cartographic constraints to regulate readability, smoothness, and geometric validity. A unified objective function is optimized to automatically identify scale-dependent parameter configurations for different generalization algorithms. Experiments using multiple line simplification algorithms, target scales, and similarity measures, including geometric, structural, and learning-based metrics, demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an effective balance between similarity preservation and cartographic abstraction. The results further show that combining similarity optimization with cartographic constraints provides more consistent and interpretable parameter control than relying on similarity evaluation alone. This study provides a unified optimization perspective that connects similarity assessment, constraint modeling, and algorithm control, contributing to adaptive and automated cartographic generalization.

</details>


### [117] [Finding Optimal Cost-Bounded Plan Reductions: Refined Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25484)
*Martha Del Toro,Raquel Fuentetaja,Angel García-Olaya*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They study how to optimally shorten an existing plan when a new budget limit appears, maximizing achieved utility while keeping original action order and executability, and provide complexity results and exact OSP/ILP methods with an improved ILP formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world scenarios, plans may become too costly when new budget constraints are introduced, but one may still be required to keep the original sequence of actions and only drop some of them. There is a need for principled methods to trim such precomputed plans to fit a cost bound while retaining as much goal utility as possible.

Method: They formalize the subplan extraction problem from a given plan with utilities and a cost bound, prove its decision version is NP-complete, and design two exact solution approaches: (1) a reduction to oversubscription planning (OSP), and (2) an Integer Linear Programming (ILP) model. In this extended work they refine the ILP formulation to reduce model size and improve computational efficiency.

Result: They show NP-completeness of the decision problem, construct exact OSP-based and ILP-based solvers, and demonstrate that the refined ILP formulation significantly reduces problem size and improves runtime compared to the previous version.

Conclusion: Extracting an optimal-cost-bounded subplan from a fixed plan with utilities is computationally hard but solvable in practice using exact optimization methods. Their improved ILP formulation makes the approach more scalable and efficient, strengthening the practical usefulness of their trimming framework for budget-constrained plan adaptation.

Abstract: In some real applications a plan may later become unfeasible due to newly imposed budget constraints, yet, at the same time, using only the original actions of the plan and their order is mandatory. In this paper, we study the problem of extracting, from a precomputed plan, a valid subplan that maximizes utility while respecting a cost bound. Each goal is given a utility value and the plan is reduced by removing actions that support low-utility goals, while preserving both executability and the original action order. We show the decision variant is NP-complete and propose two exact methods to solve it: one via oversubscription planning (OSP) and another via Integer Linear Programming (ILP). This paper extends our previous work published at ICAPS 2026 (Del Toro, Fuentetaja, and García-Olaya 2026b).
