While the core framework remains as introduced there, we further introduce a refined ILP formulation that significantly decreases the model size and improves computational efficiency.

</details>


### [118] [CoTinyVLA: Chain-of-Thought Distillation for a Sub-Billion-Parameter Vision-Language-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25487)
*Minhyeok Lee,Chiyoung Kim,Chanhoe Gu,Seongrok Kim,Sanghyuk Roy Choi,Donghwan Hwang,Donghun Ryu,Seokhyun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoTinyVLA is a compact 0.9B-parameter vision-language-action model that matches or surpasses much larger (3–7B) models on the LIBERO-Plus robustness benchmark by using structured supervision instead of scaling model size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art VLA systems for robust robotic manipulation rely on very large backbones (3–7B parameters), which exceed the memory and compute budgets typical of embedded robots. There is a need for models that retain or improve robustness to visual and task perturbations while being small enough to run efficiently on limited hardware.

Method: The authors build CoTinyVLA, a 0.9B-parameter model based on Qwen3.5-0.8B, and improve robustness via three structured-supervision strategies: (1) dual-view temporal inputs with 16 history frames per step, annotated with textual camera and time markers; (2) hierarchical chain-of-thought distillation from a 35B teacher into an episode-level “Plan” and chunk-level “Think” spans capturing task phase, gripper state, and next subaction; and (3) paraphrase augmentation that expands 40 base language commands into 800 variants. They perform extensive ablations on how frames are allocated across two cameras and time and on the contribution of each supervision component, and test in closed-loop control on LIBERO-Plus.

Result: On the LIBERO-Plus robustness benchmark (10,030 perturbed tasks across seven perturbation dimensions), CoTinyVLA achieves 90.8% (Spatial), 87.3% (Object), 86.6% (Goal), and 80.7% (Long), outperforming the strongest 7B baseline by 4.7, 2.8, 15.9, and 3.0 points, respectively, with statistically significant margins. It performs especially well on difficult perturbations like Robot Initial States, reaching 73.6% on the Goal suite vs. 39.9% for the best baseline. Frame allocation choices alone account for an 8.6-point difference at equal image budgets. Closed-loop inference uses only about 2.25 GiB of GPU memory.

Conclusion: Structured supervision—via dual-view temporal encoding, hierarchical CoT distillation, and paraphrased language augmentation—allows a compact 0.9B-parameter backbone to exceed all existing baselines on a challenging robustness benchmark, while staying within stringent memory limits. The episode-level Plan representation is shown to be crucial for performance, as contradicting or removing it leads to massive drops in success, suggesting that carefully designed supervision can substitute for sheer model scale in VLA robots.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models translate natural-language commands into robot action sequences, but leading systems on the LIBERO-Plus robustness benchmark use three- to seven-billion-parameter backbones whose memory demands can exceed embedded robotic budgets. We present CoTinyVLA, a 0.9B-parameter action model on a Qwen3.5-0.8B backbone that obtains that robustness by structuring supervision instead of enlarging the model. Three components target different axes of the problem: dual-view temporal input of 16 history frames per step with textual camera and time markers; hierarchical chain-of-thought (CoT) distillation from a 35B teacher into an episode-level Plan and a chunk-level Think span over task phase, gripper state and next subaction; and paraphrase augmentation expanding 40 base commands into 800 variants. On LIBERO-Plus, spanning 10,030 perturbed tasks across seven perturbation dimensions, CoTinyVLA reaches 90.8% on Spatial, 87.3% on Object, 86.6% on Goal and 80.7% on Long, leading the strongest 7B baseline on all four suites by 4.7, 2.8, 15.9 and 3.0 points, with every margin interval excluding zero. The gains concentrate on the hardest axes of the benchmark: across the eleven published baselines none exceeds 53.2% on Robot Initial States in any suite, whereas CoTinyVLA reaches 73.6% on Goal against 39.9% for the strongest baseline. Ablations show the three components to be separable by perturbation axis, and at a matched image budget how frames are divided between the two cameras and across time accounts for 8.6 points on its own. Closed-loop inference peaks at 2.25 GiB of allocated GPU memory, and paired interventions show the episode Plan to be load-bearing: replacing it with an empty or contradictory span costs 40 to 45 points of success. Structured supervision thus lets a 0.9B backbone exceed all of them. Code: https://github.com/BrainJellyPie/CoTinyVLA

</details>


### [119] [Are the High-weight Neurons the Important Ones in Image Classification Neural Networks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25529)
*Qitao Chen,Dongfu Yin,F. Richard Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether neuron weight magnitude truly reflects neuron importance in CNNs for image classification, using targeted experiments rather than relying on common assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many techniques such as pruning, backdoor defense, and interpretability implicitly assume that neurons with larger weights are more important. However, this weight-importance equivalence has not been rigorously validated, which can misguide model compression, defense strategies, and analysis. The paper aims to clarify this relationship.

Method: The authors propose a neuron importance assessment framework with three types of experiments on CNNs trained on CIFAR-10 and Mini-ImageNet: (1) overlap analysis between neurons ranked by weight magnitude and by impact on accuracy, (2) perturbation experiments where top-k high-weight neurons and randomly selected neurons are perturbed to measure resulting accuracy degradation, and (3) ablation plus retraining experiments where neurons in specific weight ranges (e.g., top 0.1%, top 10%) are removed and the model is retrained to see how much accuracy can be recovered.

Result: They find that only about 25% of the top 10% high-weight neurons overlap with neurons that strongly impact accuracy, and this overlap decreases in lower weight intervals. Perturbing the top 10% high-weight neurons can degrade accuracy by 45–80% under certain operations, versus only 3–7% for random perturbations, yet about one-third of these high-weight neurons have little effect. Ablation plus retraining shows that removing the top 10% high-weight neurons causes a permanent 10–20% accuracy loss, while ablating only the top 0.1% still allows nearly full recovery. Some low-weight neuron intervals, when perturbed, cause 10–17% degradation, similar to mid-range high-weight neurons.

Conclusion: Neuron importance is not a simple monotonic function of weight magnitude: many high-weight neurons are not crucial, and some low-weight neurons are functionally important. The presumed equivalence between large weights and importance is challenged, suggesting that pruning, defense, and interpretability methods should use more nuanced neuron importance measures. This understanding can guide better encryption of truly critical neurons and more effective pruning of non-critical ones.

Abstract: As neural network models for image classification advance, neurons play critical roles in pruning, backdoor defense, and interpretability. Yet existing work lacks clarity on the weight-importance relationship. We address this with a neuron importance assessment method using three experiments: quantifying overlap between high-weight and accuracy-impacting neurons, analyzing high-weight neuron perturbation effects, and testing post-retraining accuracy after high-weight neuron ablation. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and Mini-ImageNet reveal key patterns. Overlap analysis shows top 10\% high-weight neurons overlap with important ones by only about 25\% at maximum, dropping further in subsequent intervals. Perturbation tests find top 10\% high-weight neurons cause 45-80\% accuracy degradation under certain operations compared to 3-7\% for random perturbations, but a third of them show minimal impact. Ablation-retraining results show removing top 10\% high-weight neurons leaves accuracy 10-20\% below baseline with no recovery, while ablating top 0.1\% allows near-full recovery. Notably, some low-weight intervals show 10-17\% degradation when perturbed, comparable to mid-range high-weight neurons. These results confirm not all high-weight neurons are important: their importance is nonlinear. Low-weight neurons also contribute significantly. This challenges weight-importance equivalence, offering refined neuron role insights. It supports applications like encryption prioritizing critical high-weight neurons and pruning removing non-critical ones, advancing neural network analysis.

</details>


### [120] [Entangled by Design: Spurious Intra-Variable Signal Routing in Tabular In-Context Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25532)
*Athanasios Vlontzos,Giorgos Papanastasiou,Bernhard Kainz,Sotirios Tsaftaris*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that in-context learning on tabular data can latch onto spurious parts of a composite feature and that this failure mode is systematic, quantifiable, and fixable with simple training-time perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-context learners (ICL) used on tabular data are expected to infer causal relationships from examples at inference time. In real-world settings, observed features often bundle true causal signals with environment-specific artefacts. Models may therefore learn to rely on these artefacts, leading to silent failure when deployed in new environments (e.g., another hospital). The authors aim to formally characterise and quantify this spurious reliance ("spurious routing") and understand how context size and model expressiveness affect it, then design practical mitigations that do not require full causal knowledge.

Method: They model a composite feature as X = [C; αS; η], where C is a causal signal, S is a spurious signal confined to a distinct subspace, and η is noise. They study a specific linear ICL mechanism, ridge ICL, and show theoretically that such a learner cannot know whether C or S drives outcomes given only in-context data; its predictions inevitably route through the signal that appears stronger in-context. They derive a closed-form expression for causal vs spurious routing, with a causal-to-spurious routing measure CSR ∝ ρ_S/ρ_C, where ρ_C and ρ_S quantify alignment of C and S with labels. They then validate these predictions both for the analytical linear model and empirically for TabPFN, a powerful pretrained tabular ICL model. They additionally explore the role of context size and model capacity, and design two mitigations—environment-stratified contexts and S-swap augmentation—requiring only weak environment labels and no explicit identification of C versus S.

Result: For ridge ICL, they prove that spurious routing is unavoidable: the learner aligns with whichever of C or S appears more predictive based on in-context statistics, independent of context length. Empirically, TabPFN exhibits the same qualitative failure mode. Their analytic CSR expression matches measured routing behaviours with very high correlation (r = 0.997 for linear ICL and r = 0.979 for TabPFN). Increasing context length strengthens the model’s commitment to whichever signal dominates in-context, thereby increasing spurious routing by up to 1.74× in settings where S is dominant; more expressive models are more vulnerable in high-spurious, entangled regimes, showing a +2.22 CSR gap. Their proposed S-swap augmentation dramatically reduces spurious routing—by 74% for linear ICL and 98.8% for TabPFN—and increases TabPFN’s sensitivity to the causal signal by 8.4×.

Conclusion: Spurious routing in composite feature representations is an inherent failure mode for in-context learners on tabular data, even in simple linear settings and with arbitrarily long contexts. The problem worsens as context size grows and as models become more expressive, because they more confidently exploit the dominant in-context correlation, which is often spurious across environments. Nonetheless, the behaviour is predictable with a simple closed-form characterisation and can be mitigated without full causal knowledge, using environment-stratified contexts and S-swap augmentation. These interventions shift the model’s reliance from spurious to causal subspaces rather than making it indifferent, suggesting a robust path toward more causally aligned in-context learners in practice.

Abstract: Consider a model trained at a single hospital to predict patient recovery, where the measured feature $X$ bundles the patient's true health signal ($C$) with a systematic artefact from that hospital's equipment ($S$). Within that hospital, the artefact correlates with outcomes through unmeasured confounders such as patient demographics; an in-context learner rationally routes predictions through $S$, not $C$, and fails silently when deployed at a new hospital with different equipment. We formalise this as \emph{spurious routing in composite representations}: when a feature $X = [C;\,αS;\,η]$ encodes a causal signal $C$ and a spurious signal $S$ in distinct subspaces, the ICL cannot determine which drives predictions. We prove that under ridge ICL, a linear in-context learner, this routing is unavoidable regardless of context size; TabPFN, a state-of-the-art pretrained tabular ICL model, shows qualitatively consistent behaviour empirically. We derive a closed-form characterisation, $\mathrm{CSR} \propto ρ_S/ρ_C$, confirmed at $r = 0.997$ for linear ICL and $r = 0.979$ for TabPFN. Contrary to intuition, larger context sharpens commitment to the dominant in-context signal, amplifying spurious routing by up to $1.74\times$; in the high-spurious corner, more expressive models show greater vulnerability empirically ($+2.22$ CSR gap at high entanglement). We introduce two lightweight mitigations: environment-stratified context construction and S-swap augmentation, that require only weak environment labels and no knowledge of the causal partition. S-swap reduces spurious routing by $74\%$ for linear ICL and $98.8\%$ for TabPFN, with TabPFN's causal sensitivity increasing $8.4\times$ simultaneously: the model does not become agnostic, it reroutes through the causal signal.

</details>


### [121] [From Training to Deployment: Post-Hoc Causal Feature Identification via Sensitivity Ratios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25546)
*Athanasios Vlontzos,Giorgos Papanastasiou,Bernhard Kainz,Sotirios Tsaftaris*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a post-hoc, model-agnostic statistic (NSR) that distinguishes causal from spurious features across multiple environments where only spurious features shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners need to know, after training a model, which features it truly relies on causally versus spurious correlations, especially in domains like clinical or genomics data where environments differ systematically. Existing methods often need access to the training process or cannot be applied post-hoc, leaving a gap for deployed black-box models.

Method: They define the Normalised Sensitivity Ratio (NSR), computed from a model’s per-environment sensitivities to each feature under a structured-shift assumption: only means of spurious features change while causal mechanisms and marginals are stable. NSR is the squared coefficient of variation of sensitivity across environments: causal features yield low NSR (stable sensitivity), spurious features high NSR (sensitivity tracks shift). They analyze NSR under a linear structural causal model with at least three non-degenerate environments, derive exact identifiability conditions, characterize failure modes (weak shifts, degenerate geometry, proxy attenuation), give quantitative conditions for when the regime holds, and provide finite-sample convergence rates under null and alternative hypotheses. They then empirically test NSR on synthetic data and real-world tasks across multiple model families.

Result: Theoretically, NSR exactly identifies causal versus spurious features under a linear SCM and at least three suitably distinct environments, with clear finite-sample rates and quantified failure regimes (e.g., weak shifts and proxy effects cause power loss of order ε^4 or (1−α)^4). Empirically, NSR perfectly separates causal from spurious features on synthetic data satisfying the assumptions (AUROC 1.0), yields consistent feature rankings across five different model classes (Kendall τ ≥ 0.529), and on a real bike-sharing dataset recovers 6 of 8 known causal features with high precision (Precision@7 = 0.75), all without retraining or altering the models.

Conclusion: NSR provides a principled, post-hoc, model-agnostic diagnostic to distinguish causal from spurious features in settings where environments mainly differ in spurious-feature means. Under its assumptions it is provably identifiable and statistically well-behaved, and in practice it works across diverse architectures and real data without needing to change training. Practitioners also get explicit quantitative checks (shift strength, geometry, proxy attenuation) to judge when NSR’s conclusions are trustworthy.

Abstract: Given a model that is already trained, which features does it rely on causally versus spuriously? Existing methods require access to the training procedure and cannot answer this post-hoc. We introduce the \textbf{Normalised Sensitivity Ratio~(NSR)}, a post-hoc, model-agnostic diagnostic for this question under a structured-shift regime: environments differ primarily in the mean of spurious features while the causal mechanism and causal marginals remain stable, as in multi-site clinical data or multi-batch genomics. Within this regime, causal features induce constant model sensitivity across environments while spurious features track shift. NSR formalises this as the squared coefficient of variation of per-environment sensitivity. Under a linear structural causal model (SCM) with $K\ge3$ non-degenerate environments, NSR achieves exact identification (Theorem~1). We fully characterise failure: weak shifts ($O(\varepsilon^4)$ collapse), degenerate geometry, and proxy attenuation ($O((1-α)^4)$), giving practitioners quantitative criteria for assessing whether the regime holds. Finite-sample rates are $O_p(n^{-1})$ under the null and $O_p(n^{-1/2})$ under the alternative. Experiments confirm all theoretical predictions on synthetic data (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 1.000$ under conditions satisfying the regime), show consistent rankings across five model families (Kendall $τ\ge0.529$), and recover six of eight causal features on bike-sharing data (Precision@7 $= 0.75$) without modifying any trained model.

</details>


### [122] [Distilling Temporal Search and Reasoning: Evolving LLMs for Future Prediction via Harness-Assisted Efficient Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25554)
*Wanxu Cai,Zhengyu Chen,Huaisheng Zhu,Wei Wang,Jingang Wang,Qiang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a time-truncation harness for LLM-based future event prediction that enforces temporal cut-offs during tool-integrated reasoning, improving temporal search, data quality, and downstream distillation for forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing future event prediction with LLMs often relies on external agent frameworks whose forecasting ability disappears without the framework, and current tool-integrated reasoning methods are designed for static multi-hop retrieval, not temporal forecasting. A central challenge is that using historical queries risks temporal leakage, effectively turning forecasting into mere retrieval. Prior work either uses only static snapshots or heavily filters data via rejection sampling or unresolved fresh queries, which wastes data and reduces synthesis efficiency. The authors are motivated to create a method that enables efficient, temporally-aware reasoning over historical data while minimizing leakage and data waste, and that can be distilled into standalone models.

Method: The authors design a time-truncation harness that applies a strict temporal cut-off at every reasoning step, so the model can only access information available up to that time. This allows Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR) style deep search over historical events while controlling temporal leakage. They construct a large-scale corpus of forecasting-oriented interactions collected under this harness, and introduce a process-based metric to evaluate temporal search behavior and data quality. They analyze how the harness affects temporal breadth of search and the proportion of high-quality trajectories. Finally, they run distillation experiments in which student models are trained on harness-intervened data to internalize better temporal search and reasoning patterns.

Result: Empirically, the time-truncation harness yields interactions with broader temporal search breadth and a higher fraction of high-quality data compared to baselines, which increases sampling efficiency and reduces dependence on complex annotation rubrics. The process-based metric confirms improved temporal reasoning behavior. In distillation experiments, student models trained on harness-intervened data outperform those trained on alternative data, achieving the best forecasting performance among compared setups. This demonstrates that the harness produces superior training signals for temporal reasoning and forecasting.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that enforcing temporal cut-offs via a time-truncation harness is an effective way to adapt tool-integrated reasoning for future event prediction. It mitigates temporal leakage, improves the efficiency and quality of collected forecasting data, and enables successful distillation of better temporal search and reasoning abilities into parametric models. The approach supports more robust, harness-independent forecasting performance and reduces the need for data-inefficient filtering strategies or complex rubric-based evaluations.

Abstract: Future event prediction carries broad social impact yet remains challenging. SOTA approaches augment LLMs with external agent frameworks whose predictive capability vanishes once the harness is removed. While recent Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR) internalizes deep search for multi-hop retrieval of facts, forecasting further demands temporal search and reasoning over historical trends and dynamic shifts. The key obstacle is data: historical queries induce temporal leakage that degrades forecasting into retrieval. Prior works either freeze information gathering with static observations, or rely on rejection sampling or unresolved fresh queries that discard vast amounts of data, degrading synthesis efficiency. We propose a time-truncation harness that enforces a temporal cut-off at every turn, enabling TIR-style sampling from historical events, reducing temporal leakage and reliance of rejection sampling or unsolved queries, increasing the sampling efficiency. We further build a large-scale corpus and a process-based metric and show that our harness naturally induces a broader temporal breadth of search and raises the proportion of high-quality data, further increasing the efficiency and reducing the reliance on complex rubrics. Distillation experiments show that students trained on harness-intervened data achieve the best performance, demonstrating harness-assisted model evolving that turns higher quality temporal search and reasoning data into a parametric advancement of the students.

</details>


### [123] [Agent Skills Matter: Inferring Proprietary Skills from Execution Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25560)
*Jianing Geng,Ruiqi He,Zekun Fei,Biao Yi,Ruijie Wang,Zheli Liu,Xia Hu,Xuansheng Wu,Qingkai Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Skill Leakage and SigLeak, a method to reconstruct proprietary agent skills from observed execution trajectories, showing that even benign interactions can reveal procedural knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agent skills become monetized, providers keep them proprietary by hiding code and artifacts. However, their behaviors remain observable through trajectories. The authors are motivated by the security and IP risks that such observable behavior can still leak enough information to reconstruct the underlying proprietary skills, even when only benign queries and no ground-truth labels are available.

Method: They formalize Skill Leakage as reconstructing proprietary skills from observable trajectories with no reference answers. They propose SigLeak, a black-box framework that: (1) generates diverse, decision-rich diagnostic tasks, (2) collects and contrasts trajectories from agents with and without the target skill enabled, (3) identifies recurring behavioral signatures that are attributable to the skill, and (4) iteratively refines a reconstructed skill implementation from these isolated patterns. SigLeak operates across different scenarios, model families, and agent frameworks, focusing on behavioral signatures rather than internal model access.

Result: Across five different scenarios, three base model families, and three agent frameworks, SigLeak matches or outperforms three baselines in nearly all configurations. On average, it improves task success rate by 6.88 percentage points over the skill-disabled reference and achieves the highest scores on SkillSim, the proposed metric that measures both coarse- and fine-grained semantic similarity between the reconstructed skill and the original proprietary one.

Conclusion: Benign execution trajectories constitute a behavioral side channel that can be exploited to reconstruct proprietary skills, meaning that hiding implementation artifacts is insufficient to protect procedural know-how. SigLeak demonstrates that recurring behavioral signatures can be extracted and turned into effective approximations of private skills, highlighting a significant security and IP-leakage risk for marketplaces and cloud-hosted agent interfaces.

Abstract: Agent skills package reusable procedures that improve downstream performance. Their lightweight, portable form enables marketplace monetization and private deployment behind cloud-hosted agent interfaces, giving providers incentives to keep high-value skills proprietary. Yet hiding the artifacts does not conceal their behavioral effects, which remain observable in execution trajectories and form a behavioral side channel. We define this exposure as Skill Leakage: reconstructing proprietary skills from trajectories elicited by benign queries, without reference answers or success labels. We introduce SigLeak, a black-box framework that exploits recurring skill signatures in agent behavior. It constructs diverse, decision-rich diagnostic tasks, contrasts matched skill-enabled and skill-disabled trajectories, and iteratively refines a reconstructed skill from the isolated patterns. Across five scenarios, three model families, and three agent frameworks, SigLeak outperforms or matches three baselines in nearly every setting. It raises the success rate by 6.88 percentage points over the skill-disabled reference on average and achieves the highest overall SkillSim, our metric for coarse- and fine-grained semantic similarity. These results show that benign execution trajectories can expose proprietary procedural knowledge. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SigLeak-D1DB.

</details>


### [124] [Matrix-Free Photoacoustic Image Reconstruction via Sensor-Token Self-Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25576)
*Mary John,Shibili Said,Imad Barhumi,Sherzod Turaev,Mohamed Yahia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Transformer-based Sensor Attention Network (SAN) that reconstructs photoacoustic tomography images directly from raw sensor time-series data, avoiding the computationally costly system matrix at inference while improving image quality and speed over classical and learned baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photoacoustic tomography reconstruction from sparse-view sensor data is an ill-posed inverse problem. Existing approaches, such as iterative compressive-sensing solvers and unrolled neural networks, still rely on the explicit system matrix at inference, which is computationally intensive and hinders real-time clinical use. There is a need for a method that both improves reconstruction quality and removes dependence on the system matrix during deployment.

Method: The authors design a Sensor Attention Network (SAN), a Transformer-based model that treats each sensor’s full time series as a token and maps the raw acoustic measurements directly to an image, without using the system matrix at inference. For training and evaluation, they construct an analytical k-space H-matrix and validate it against the k-Wave pseudo-spectral solver, tuning k-space apodization and Gaussian temporal damping to reduce mismatch. SAN is trained with a vessel-weighted loss on 488 augmented samples and tested on 46 held-out samples, and its performance is compared to ISTA, split-Bregman total variation (SBTV), and learned ISTA (LISTA).

Result: The analytical k-space H-matrix matches the k-Wave solver well, achieving a mean per-sensor Pearson correlation of 0.919 ± 0.049, and apodization plus temporal damping reduce energy-normalized mismatch by 49%. On the test set, SAN achieves superior reconstruction performance with the highest mean SSIM (0.522), PSNR (22.09 dB), and the lowest NMSE (0.233) compared to ISTA, SBTV, and LISTA. Statistical tests (paired t-tests and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests) show SAN significantly outperforms LISTA in PSNR, NMSE, and Pearson correlation (p < 1e-8), and outperforms ISTA and SBTV across all fidelity metrics.

Conclusion: By using a Transformer-based Sensor Attention Network that directly maps raw sensor time-series data to images and bypasses the system matrix at inference, the method significantly improves reconstruction quality and reduces computation time by at least an order of magnitude, enabling real-time photoacoustic tomography reconstruction suitable for clinical settings.

Abstract: Photoacoustic tomography (PAT) combines the optical absorption contrast of biological tissue with the spatial resolution of ultrasound, yet recovering the initial pressure distribution from sparse-view sensor measurements remains an ill-posed inverse problem. Iterative compressive-sensing solvers and unrolled deep networks both retain a dependence on the system matrix at inference, which leaves real-time clinical reconstruction computationally expensive. This paper proposes the Sensor Attention Network (SAN), a Transformer-based architecture that treats the full time series of each sensor as a token and maps raw measurements directly to the reconstructed image without invoking the system matrix at inference. For training and benchmarking, an analytical k-space H-matrix is constructed and validated against the k-Wave pseudo-spectral solver under matched geometry, achieving a mean per-sensor Pearson correlation of 0.919 +/- 0.049, with k-space apodization and Gaussian temporal damping acting synergistically to reduce the energy-normalized mismatch by 49%. Trained with a vessel-weighted loss on 488 augmented samples and evaluated on 46 held-out samples against ISTA, split-Bregman total variation (SBTV), and learned ISTA (LISTA), SAN attains the highest mean SSIM (0.522) and PSNR (22.09 dB) and the lowest NMSE (0.233). Paired t-tests and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm the superiority of SAN over LISTA on PSNR, NMSE, and Pearson correlation at p < 1e-8, and over ISTA and SBTV on all fidelity metrics. By bypassing the H-matrix at inference, SAN reduces reconstruction time by at least an order of magnitude, supporting real-time PAT reconstruction.

</details>


### [125] [How Small Can You Go? A Controlled Study of LoRA Rank, Target Modules, and Quantization Trade-offs for Text-to-SQL on a 60M-Parameter Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25583)
*Mahendra Singh Rathor,Anagheem Azzam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies how much accuracy is lost when using parameter-efficient fine-tuning (LoRA) and low-bit quantization (QLoRA) on a small, fully reproducible T5-small model for text-to-SQL, relating accuracy to system metrics like memory, latency, and throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While PEFT and quantization are widely used to adapt large language models under compute and memory constraints, their trade-offs (accuracy vs. efficiency) are usually explored only on very large models where experiments are costly and hard to fully reproduce. The authors want a clean, controlled setting to precisely quantify how much accuracy each efficiency technique and hyperparameter choice actually costs, and to present adaptation as a constrained resource trade-off rather than a pure accuracy maximization problem.

Method: Use a fixed 60M-parameter encoder-decoder model (T5-small) on a single text-to-SQL benchmark (WikiSQL) and run a controlled, single-factor-at-a-time study varying: (i) LoRA rank r in {2,4,8,16,32}, (ii) which model modules are adapted, and (iii) numerical precision via QLoRA with INT8 and NF4 quantization. For each configuration, measure task exact-match accuracy plus system-level metrics: number of trainable parameters, peak training GPU memory, inference latency, and throughput. Compare these to full fine-tuning as a reference.

Result: LoRA with rank r=16 attains 59.6% exact-match accuracy, within 11.6 percentage points of full fine-tuning (71.2%), while training under 1% of model parameters and reducing peak GPU memory by 31%. Increasing LoRA rank beyond 16 does not yield additional measurable accuracy gains. QLoRA with INT8 and NF4 quantization achieves similar accuracy (52.8% and 53.2%) at much lower memory consumption (0.60 GB for both), indicating strong memory-efficiency benefits at some accuracy cost.

Conclusion: In this controlled T5-small + WikiSQL setting, moderate-rank LoRA (r≈16) provides most of the benefits of full fine-tuning at a small accuracy loss and substantial savings in trainable parameters and memory, with no benefit from higher ranks. QLoRA with INT8 or NF4 offers a highly memory-efficient alternative with acceptable accuracy, making it attractive for deployment under strict memory constraints. Carefully characterizing these trade-offs in a small, reproducible setup helps guide principled choices of PEFT and quantization configurations under resource budgets.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) and low-bit quantization are now standard tools for adapting language models under tight compute budgets, yet their interaction is most often studied on billion-parameter models where the design space is expensive to explore. We ask a complementary question: on a specific, fully reproducible 60M-parameter encoder-decoder model (T5-small) and a single-table text-to-SQL benchmark (WikiSQL), how much task accuracy does each efficiency knob actually cost? We run a controlled, single-variable study over (i) LoRA rank r in {2, 4, 8, 16, 32}, (ii) the set of adapted modules, and (iii) numerical precision. We report task accuracy alongside system-level metrics including trainable parameters, peak training memory, inference latency, and throughput, and frame adaptation as a constrained trade-off rather than an accuracy-only objective. Our results show that LoRA with r=16 recovers within 11.6 percentage points of full fine-tuning accuracy (59.6% vs. 71.2% exact-match) while training fewer than 1% of parameters and consuming 31% less peak GPU memory. Within this setting, rank beyond r=16 yields no measurable accuracy gain. QLoRA with INT8 and NF4 quantization achieves comparable accuracy (52.8% and 53.2%) at dramatically lower memory cost (0.60 GB each), demonstrating a compelling trade-off for memory-constrained deployments. All code, configurations, and logs are released for full reproducibility.

</details>


### [126] [A Density-Matrix Framework for Electronic-Structure Analysis of Functional-Group and Salt Effects in Lithium-Metal Electrolytes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25597)
*Mingkang Liu,Huize Yu,Yanbin Gao,Nan Yao,Xiang Chen,Lei Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces EMolStudio, an AI platform that predicts and analyzes electronic structure (density matrices, frontier orbitals, electrostatic properties, Li+ bonding) for a huge space of functionalized molecules and Li+ solvation clusters, enabling rapid exploration of how functional groups and salt anions tune lithium-electrolyte reactivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing lithium-metal battery electrolytes requires understanding how molecular functional groups, Li+ solvation, and anion participation jointly control reactivity. The determining physics is encoded in the electronic structure (charge distribution, frontier orbitals, bonding), which is best obtained from quantum-chemical calculations. However, brute-force quantum chemistry over large, chemically diverse molecular and solvation-shell design spaces is computationally prohibitive, and existing ML models typically do not target the required electronic readouts or the complex Li+ solvation environments. A new, efficient, and electronically faithful modeling framework is needed.

Method: The authors develop EMolStudio, an AI-based electronic-structure platform centered on density-matrix prediction. The workflow: (1) generate a library of functionalized organic molecules; (2) explicitly construct Li+ first-solvation-shell clusters with different lithium salts; (3) train a model to predict the one-particle density matrix, followed by an idempotency projection step to enforce quantum-mechanical consistency; and (4) post-process the density matrix to obtain key electronic readouts—frontier orbital energies and localization, electrostatic potential, Li+–donor bond order, and electron localization functions. They apply this pipeline to 163,655 functionalized molecules and 22,500 Li+ solvation clusters involving four salt anions, and systematically analyze how functional groups and anion identities modulate the electronic structure.

Result: For isolated molecules, specific functional groups (CO2Me, CN, F/CF3, sulfonyl) are shown to induce chemically distinct patterns in frontier orbital energies, electrostatic potentials, and Li+–donor contact strength, in line with known π*-acceptor, inductive, and polarization effects, and these effects accumulate sublinearly as more substituents are added. For explicit Li+ solvation shells, the salt anion strongly controls where the frontier orbitals localize: with LiTDI, the HOMO remains localized on the anion across the entire solvent library, whereas with LiDFOB, the HOMO stays on the anion but the LUMO localization depends sensitively on the solvent functional groups. These trends reveal how specific combinations of salts and functional groups reshape the electronic environment around Li+.

Conclusion: EMolStudio efficiently maps functional-group and salt choices to detailed electronic-structure descriptors in lithium-metal electrolytes, connecting molecular design knobs to hypotheses about lithium-bond formation, desolvation energetics, and interphase reaction pathways. The platform overcomes the scalability limits of direct quantum chemistry while preserving electronic fidelity, offering a systematic way to screen and rationalize electrolyte chemistries in terms of their underlying electronic structure.

Abstract: The reactivity of lithium-metal electrolytes arises from the interplay of molecular functional groups, Li$^+$ solvation, and salt-anion participation. This interplay operates through the redistribution of electron density across donor, anion, and cation centers, which is most directly read out from the electronic structure resolved in space. Quantum-chemical calculations deliver such readouts faithfully, yet become computationally demanding across this multidimensional design space, and machine-learning electronic-structure models seldom cover chemically diverse solvation shells or electrolyte-relevant readouts. Here, we present a density-matrix-centered AI platform (EMolStudio) for electronic-structure prediction and analysis. Its workflow integrates molecular functionalization, explicit Li$^+$ first-shell assembly, density-matrix prediction with idempotency projection, and readouts of frontier orbitals, electrostatic potential, Li$^+$-donor bond order, and electron localization. We apply EMolStudio to 163,655 functionalized molecules and 22,500 explicit Li$^+$ first-shell clusters across four lithium salts. We find that 1) at the molecular scale, functionalization distinguishes CO$_2$Me, CN, F/CF$_3$, and sulfonyl groups by chemically distinct changes in frontier levels, electrostatic potential, and Li$^+$-donor contact, consistent with $π^*$-acceptor, inductive, and polarization contributions, with sublinear accumulation at higher degrees of functionalization; 2) in explicit solvation shells, anion identity reshapes frontier-orbital localization: LiTDI anchors the HOMO on the anion across the entire library, whereas LiDFOB pairs an anion-hosted HOMO with strongly functional-group-dependent LUMO hosting. EMolStudio thereby translates functional-group and salt choices into electronic-structure hypotheses relevant to lithium-bond formation, desolvation, and interphase reactions.

</details>


### [127] [Computational Extraction of Legal Causes via al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim: A Set-Theoretic Formalization for Closed Fiqh Chapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25605)
*Elnaser Abdelwahab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes a classical Islamic juristic method (al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim) using set theory and algorithms to derive minimal legal rules from complete verdict truth tables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In classical usul al-fiqh, jurists use al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim to identify the effective legal cause (‘illah) behind rulings, but this process is informal, non-algorithmic, and hard to implement computationally or verify rigorously. The authors aim to provide a precise, formal, and computationally executable version of this method, enabling systematic extraction of candidate legal causes from structured data about juristic verdicts.

Method: The authors translate the traditional method into set-theoretic terms, treating attributes and rulings as elements of finite sets and representing a closed chapter of jurisprudence as a complete truth table of verdicts over a finite vocabulary of concepts. They then design a computational algorithm that operates on this truth table to extract minimal operational rules: it searches for minimal sets of attributes that determine the ruling, identifies structural generators, and prunes logically redundant attributes by exploiting the table’s completeness and finiteness.

Result: Given a complete truth table for a closed chapter and a finite vocabulary of school-relative concepts, the algorithm successfully computes minimal structural generators of the ruling and systematically removes all attributes that are logically redundant for determining the verdict. The output is a reduced set of minimal rule structures that still fully reproduce the verdicts in the chapter.

Conclusion: The paper shows that the classical method of al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim can be rigorously captured using set theory and realized as a concrete algorithm. Under the assumption of a finite concept vocabulary and complete ruling table, the framework can automatically derive minimal candidate ‘ilal (causes) consistent with all given verdicts, thereby offering a principled computational aid for juristic reasoning and for further substantive evaluation by human jurists.

Abstract: This paper presents a set-theoretic formalization of the classical usuli method of al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim (Examination and Division) for extracting legal causes ('ilal) within closed chapters of jurisprudence. A computational algorithm is introduced that extracts minimal operational rules from a truth table of juristic verdicts. The principal result is that, given a complete truth table for a closed chapter, the algorithm computes the minimal structural generators of the ruling and eliminates all logically redundant attributes. The resulting structures constitute admissible candidate causes for subsequent juristic evaluation. The framework is conditional upon the availability of a finite school-relative concept vocabulary and a complete ruling table for the chapter under investigation.

</details>


### [128] [Multi-Sensor Alignment for Weather Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25612)
*Samsad Alam,Devyani Lambhate,Aditya Mohan,Vishal Kumar,Vaibhav Katewa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They develop alignment methods so simulated weather matches real-world weather characteristics across sensors, improving robustness of 3D detection in autonomous driving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulated adverse weather is used because real data is scarce, but if the simulations do not accurately match real-world weather characteristics and are not consistent across different sensors, perception models may perform unrealistically well and not generalize to real conditions.

Method: They propose two alignment methods for simulated weather: (1) Reference Dataset Alignment Method (ReDAM) that aligns fog intensity with a reference real-world dataset; (2) Unified-weather-edit, inspired by Weather-edit, that aligns the positioning of rain and snow particles across sensors. They validate ReDAM with statistical tests and Unified-weather-edit with geometrical tests, then compare 3D detection models trained on aligned vs non-aligned simulations and perform finetuning of sensor-fusion models on multi-sensor aligned simulations.

Result: They find that 3D detection models evaluated/trained on non-aligned weather simulations appear overly optimistic compared to those using aligned simulations. The aligned multi-sensor simulations lead to improved robustness of 3D object detection models after finetuning.

Conclusion: Weather simulations must be carefully aligned in intensity and particle geometry with real-world data and across sensors; their ReDAM and Unified-weather-edit methods provide such alignment, yielding more realistic evaluations and more robust 3D detection models for autonomous vehicles.

Abstract: Perception tasks for autonomous vehicles need to work satisfactorily in adverse weather conditions. Due to lack of real-world weather datasets, weather simulations are a promising alternative. To ensure simulations closely mirror real-world weather data, it's crucial that they represent the same weather characteristics, including severity and particle positioning, across different sensors. To achieve this, we propose the Reference Dataset Alignment Method (ReDAM) for weather intensity alignment in fog and Unified-weather-edit (inspired by Weather-edit[1]) for particle positioning alignment in rain and snow. We validate both alignment methods using statistical and geometrical tests, respectively. We find that 3D detection models for non-aligned versions tend to be overly optimistic as compared to aligned versions. We also show the aligned-multi-sensor simulation's effectiveness for achieving robustness for 3D object detection task by finetuning existing sensor fusion models on it.

</details>


### [129] [Beyond Epistemia: Epistemic Schizologia and Large Language Models as Techno-Semiotic Machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25620)
*Federico Cabitza,Gianluca Colombo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper critiques existing explanations of how large language models (LLMs) create an illusion of knowledge (“Epistemia”) and instead reconceives LLMs as techno-semiotic machines that automate part of the social practice of writing, showing how this creates a split between fluent language and responsible knowing, and proposing design principles to repair that split.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quattrociocchi et al. argue that LLMs cause “Epistemia,” where people feel like they know without engaging in real epistemic labor. However, they frame the issue as a comparison between humans (embodied, social knowers) and isolated models, implicitly treating epistemic legitimacy as something internal to individual agents. The authors see this as insufficient and want a framework that treats LLMs and humans as parts of socio-technical practices rather than separate epistemic agents, in order to better understand and address the epistemic risks of LLMs.

Method: Conceptual and philosophical analysis drawing on Carlo Sini’s philosophy of practices, writing, signs, and technics. The authors (1) reconceptualize LLMs as techno-semiotic machines that automate a phase of written semiosis; (2) introduce and analyze the concepts of epistemic schizologia, eikotic closure, algorithmic authority, and epistemic self-misrecognition; and (3) use this reframing to articulate design and governance implications for hybrid human–AI epistemic practices.

Result: They develop the notion of LLMs as machines that generate plausible linguistic configurations from the historical archive of human writing, and on that basis introduce the key concept of “epistemic schizologia” to name a systematic split between surface linguistic outputs and the socially embedded circuits of interpretation, evidence, criticism, and responsibility. They further identify mechanisms (eikotic closure, algorithmic authority, self-misrecognition) that intensify this split and show that the central unit of analysis must be whole practices in which prompts, outputs, interpretations, verifications, and uses are entangled, not the model in isolation.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that to respond to Epistemia we must shift from treating LLMs as quasi-autonomous knowers to seeing them as components within socio-technical practices of writing and knowing. This preserves a sharp distinction between generating fluent language and achieving responsible understanding, and it underwrites a design program focused on inspectable genealogies of outputs, contestability of results, distributed responsibility across human and machine actors, strengthening users’ epistemic agency, and systematically evaluating hybrid human–AI epistemic practices.

Abstract: Quattrociocchi and colleagues warn that the fluent outputs of large language models may allow linguistic plausibility to substitute for epistemic evaluation, producing the condition they call *Epistemia*: the experience of possessing knowledge without undertaking the practices through which judgment would ordinarily be warranted. This article accepts that diagnosis but challenges its explanatory framework, which compares an embodied, socially situated human knower with an isolated generative model thereby locating epistemic legitimacy in capacities internal to autonomous agents. Drawing on Carlo Sini's philosophy of practices, writing, signs, and technics, we propose instead to understand a large language model (LLM) as a *techno-semiotic machine* that automates a phase of written semiosis by producing plausible linguistic configurations from the sedimented archive of human writing. From this perspective, *Epistemia* is one consequence of a broader phenomenon that we call *epistemic schizologia*: the socio-technical cleavage between signs as linguistically accomplished expressions and signs as moments within socially embedded circuits of interpretation, evidence, criticism, verification, and responsibility. This cleavage is reinforced by *eikotic closure*, through which a plausible continuation is presented with the finality of an epistemic result, and by algorithmic authority and epistemic self-misrecognition. The relevant unit is therefore not the model alone but the complete practice in which generated inscriptions are prompted, interpreted, verified, contested, used, and made consequential. This reframing preserves the distinction between linguistic production and responsible understanding while grounding a design programme centred on inspectable genealogy, contestability, distributed responsibility, epistemic agency, and the evaluation of hybrid human--AIpractices.

</details>


### [130] [Quotient Dynamics, Effective Curvature, and Implicit Bias in Positive Quadratic Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25624)
*Pengcheng Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies training dynamics and implicit bias of positive quadratic networks via their low-rank matrix quotient structure, linking factor-space optimization to Riemannian optimization on the PSD manifold and characterizing curvature, recovery, and interpolation behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the quotient structure of low-rank positive quadratic networks (parameterized as UU^T) determines optimization trajectories, curvature properties, recovery guarantees, and which interpolating solutions gradient-based methods implicitly select, especially in overparameterized, underdetermined settings.

Method: Model positive quadratic networks as rank-r PSD matrices Q=UU^T and analyze the quotient geometry R^{d×r}_*/O(r). Show that factor gradient flow corresponds exactly to Riemannian gradient flow on the PSD manifold. For quadratic regression with Gaussian rank-one measurements, compute population and empirical curvature (effective Hessian), prove deviation bounds, design a spectral initializer, and analyze local convergence of gradient flow and gradient descent. Study underdetermined commuting regimes via entropy mirror flow in joint spectral coordinates to characterize interpolation bias.

Result: On the full-rank stratum, the factor-space modulo orthogonal equivalence is identified with the rank-r PSD manifold, and gradients are horizontal, yielding exact projection of factor gradient flow to quotient Riemannian flow and an exact congruence recursion for gradient descent on predictors. For quadratic regression, the effective Hessian is the empirical measurement Gram form restricted to the tangent space, with computed population curvature and uniform deviation bounds. A spectral initializer is constructed, and local exponential (continuous-time) and linear (discrete-time) convergence is established under Gaussian rank-one measurements. In underdetermined commuting regimes, factor gradient flow equals an entropy mirror flow and converges to Bregman projections; isotropic initialization selects minimum-trace solutions as epsilon→0, with finite-step descent deviating from continuous-time selection by O(η). Numerical experiments support the theory.

Conclusion: The quotient geometry of low-rank positive quadratic networks fully determines their training dynamics and implicit bias: factor optimization is equivalent to Riemannian optimization on the PSD manifold, enabling precise curvature and recovery analysis. In underdetermined settings, gradient-based methods implicitly select minimum-trace (entropy-regularized) interpolants determined by initialization and step size, with rigorous convergence guarantees and empirical validation.

Abstract: Positive quadratic networks admit the low-rank representation f_U(x)=x^top UU^top x, where Uinmathbb{R}^{dtimes r} is identifiable only up to right orthogonal multiplication, representing a rank-r PSD matrix Q=UU^top. We study how this quotient structure governs training dynamics, curvature, recovery, and interpolation bias. On the full-column-rank stratum, we identify mathbb{R}^{dtimes r}_*/O(r) with the rank-r PSD manifold. For smooth objectives L(U)=ell(UU^top), the Euclidean factor gradient is horizontal. Thus, factor gradient flow projects exactly to quotient Riemannian gradient flow, while finite-step gradient descent induces an exact congruence recursion for the predictor. For quadratic regression, we derive the effective Hessian at interpolators as the empirical measurement Gram form restricted to the tangent space relative to the quotient metric. Under Gaussian rank-one measurements, we compute population curvature, prove uniform deviation bounds for the empirical normal operator, construct a spectral initializer, and establish local exponential convergence for gradient flow and linear convergence for small-step descent. Recovery guarantees are explicit but conservative due to reliance on full-space second-moment control. In underdetermined commuting regimes, factor gradient flow becomes an exact entropy mirror flow in joint spectral coordinates. Strictly positive initializations converge to Bregman projections onto the interpolation set. With isotropic initialization q(0)=varepsilon^2mathbf{1}, predictors approach the minimum-trace solution set as varepsilondownarrow0, resolving nonuniqueness via weighted entropy within the invariant joint spectral algebra. Finite-step descent selects interpolants differing from continuous-time Bregman projections by O(eta). Numerical experiments verify these quotient identities, curvature predictions, recovery behaviors, and selection laws.

</details>


### [131] [Joint Text-Audio Alignment for EEG-to-Text Decoding in Chinese Speech Production and Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25626)
*Tian Zheng,Xurong Xie,Xinxin Zhu,Xiaolan Peng,Feng Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes EEGAlign, a framework that decodes large-vocabulary Chinese sentences from non-invasive EEG by jointly aligning brain signals with both text and audio representations, achieving state-of-the-art closed-set sentence classification performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-invasive EEG-based speech decoding could enable communication for people with severe speech and motor impairments, but decoding Chinese sentences is very difficult due to the large character set, low SNR, and inter-subject variability. Existing methods rely on either text semantics or acoustic features alone, which cannot simultaneously provide both sentence-level discriminability and fine temporal resolution for large-vocabulary Chinese decoding.

Method: The authors introduce EEGAlign, a parameter-efficient framework that aligns EEG signals simultaneously with text embeddings (BGE-M3) and audio speech features (wav2vec 2.0) using contrastive learning. After learning this joint alignment, they apply a CTC-based character sequence decoder for sentence-level Chinese decoding. They evaluate on the ChineseEEG-2 dataset in both Reading Aloud and Passive Listening conditions and conduct ablation studies on using single vs dual alignment axes.

Result: On the ChineseEEG-2 dataset, EEGAlign achieves state-of-the-art closed-set sentence classification, with up to 82.37% Top-1 accuracy for Reading Aloud EEG and 41.43% for Passive Listening EEG among 101 candidate sentences. Ablation studies demonstrate that leveraging both the text and audio alignment axes improves performance over using either one individually.

Conclusion: Jointly aligning EEG with both semantic text embeddings and acoustic speech features enables more accurate decoding of large-vocabulary Chinese sentences from non-invasive EEG than previous single-axis approaches. EEGAlign establishes a new state-of-the-art on ChineseEEG-2 and represents, to the authors’ knowledge, the first strong demonstration of non-invasive decoding of large-vocabulary Chinese sentences during overt speech production in a relatively large closed-set scenario.

Abstract: Decoding speech information directly from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) into text provides a potential non-invasive neural communication pathway for individuals with severe speech and motor impairments. Compared with invasive approaches such as electrocorticography, EEG is safer and more widely deployable, yet substantially more challenging to decode.This challenge is exacerbated for Chinese sentence decoding, which must handle a high-dimensional output space with thousands of characters, severe inter-subject variability, and low signal-to-noise ratios for text alignment.Existing methods commit to a single supervisory axis---either text semantics or audio acoustic features---yet neither can simultaneously satisfy the demands of sentence-level discriminability and fine-grained temporal resolution required for large-vocabulary Chinese decoding. We introduce EEGAlign, a novel parameter-efficient framework that jointly aligns EEG with two axes---text alignment with BGE-M3 text embeddings and audio alignment with wav2vec~2.0 speech features via contrastive learning followed by CTC character-sequence decoding. On ChineseEEG-2 data, EEGAlign yields state-of-the-art closed-set sentence classification performance, reaching up to 82.37% Top-1 accuracy on Reading Aloud EEG and 41.43% on Passive Listening EEG out of 101 candidates. Ablation studies show that the two alignment axes are highly complementary: combining them yields consistently better performance than either alone. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study on decoding large-vocabulary Chinese sentences from non-invasive EEG during overt speech production, and achieving strong classification performance with relatively large closed-set candidate-sentence setting.

</details>


### [132] [Engine-Equal, Human-Unequal: A Reproducible Outcome Skew in Engine-Assessed Equal Chess Positions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25655)
*Jesung Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Engine-equal chess opening positions show small but robust systematic outcome skews for humans, favoring White or Black in stable, reproducible ways not captured by engine evaluation alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In modern chess, strong engines evaluate many opening positions as essentially equal, yet human results in practical play might systematically deviate from this neutrality. The authors want to know whether, among positions that engines judge as equal and that occur naturally in large-scale online play, there are stable, reproducible biases in human outcomes – and whether engine evaluation alone is sufficient to predict human performance.

Method: They select a large set of opening positions from Lichess games that Stockfish 18 evaluates within 10 centipawns of zero at stable depth. For each position, they compute an “outcome skew”: the difference between actual human game results and the results predicted from players’ ratings. They test the stability of these skews across multiple independent partitions: disjoint sets of player accounts (primary split), different time periods, rating bands, and an out-of-sample month 8 months later. On the primary split, each position’s skew is measured separately in two disjoint account groups; they then regress one group’s skew on the other, controlling for rating and opening-family effects, to obtain a replication slope. They also probe robustness to varying engine-evaluation bands, search depths, rating calibration methods, and popularity thresholds, and they examine thinking time differences by side in these positions.

Result: They find that many engine-equal positions exhibit consistent outcome skews in human play: some systematically favor White, others Black, beyond what ratings predict. The replication slope between disjoint account groups is about 0.69 overall (95% CI [0.65, 0.74] with clustering by opening family), rising to 0.94 for the most popular, best-measured positions, showing strong reproducibility of position-specific skews. These effects hold across alternative partitions (time, rating bands), an out-of-sample month, and within blitz and rapid separately, and persist under stricter engine-equality and robustness checks. The typical absolute skew is small (median |δ| ≈ 0.018, about 2 percentage points of White score), but reliably replicates. In these positions, the side that is empirically disadvantaged also tends to spend more thinking time. Engine evaluation, even when confident, is therefore not a sufficient statistic for predicting human outcomes.

Conclusion: Among positions that chess engines deem essentially equal, humans still experience systematic, reproducible biases in results that depend on the specific position. These “human outcome skews” are stable across disjoint player populations, time, rating bands, and robustness checks, with the disadvantaged side typically thinking longer. Thus, strong engine evaluations near 0 do not fully capture human practical chances; position identity matters for human outcomes even when engines declare equality. The study is observational and deliberately leaves causal explanations (e.g., which cognitive or stylistic factors create these skews) to a separate pre-registered randomized companion experiment.

Abstract: Among chess opening positions that a strong engine judges essentially equal (Stockfish 18 evaluation within 10 centipawns of zero, depth-stable) and that humans actually reach on Lichess (October 2025; 1,661 positions, 16.1M occurrences), human results are not balanced. Positions carry outcome skews, each the gap between its games' actual results and what the players' ratings predict, whose directions are stable properties of the naturally-reached position: some positions favour White, others Black. These skews reproduce across three re-partitions -- disjoint player-account sets (primary), time, and disjoint rating bands -- and on an out-of-sample month eight months later. On the primary split, each position's skew is measured once in each account group, and the replication slope asks how well one measurement predicts the other after removing rating and opening-family effects: one means undiminished carry-over; zero, no linear relation. We find 0.69 (family-clustered 95% CI [0.65, 0.74]), rising to 0.94 on the most-popular, best-measured positions. The slope's value depends on the position mix. Existence is the invariant claim: it survives every tighter evaluation band, search depth, calibration, and popularity cutoff we test, and replicates within blitz and rapid separately. The typical skew is small (median $|δ| \approx 0.018$, about two percentage points of White score), yet it reproduces, position by position, across disjoint accounts. At these positions the disfavoured side also thinks longer. Even where the evaluation is most confident, it is not a sufficient statistic for human outcomes. The result is observational, and the causal question is left to a pre-registered randomised companion study.

</details>


### [133] [OrchBench: Evaluating Multi-Agent Orchestration Plans in Isolation via Deterministic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25656)
*Zhenzhen Ren,Jiyan He,Xinpeng Zhang,Zhenxing Qian,Ke Han,Shuxin Zheng,GuoBiao Li,Xiaoqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OrchBench is a simulation-based benchmark to evaluate multi-agent orchestration plans independently of worker quality, offering cheap, interpretable metrics that correlate well with real executions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent systems, complex tasks are decomposed into interdependent subtasks that must be orchestrated efficiently. Existing evaluations use full end-to-end runs, which mix together the effects of orchestration quality, agent competence, tools, and environment, and become very costly as workflows scale. There is a need for a way to evaluate just the orchestration plan—how subtasks are scheduled, assigned, and coordinated—without incurring the cost and noise of full executions.

Method: The authors construct OrchBench, which starts from real-world tasks and encodes them as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) capturing task dependencies with controlled size and parallelism. Given such a DAG, a context limit per agent, and a total agent budget, an orchestration planner must assign subtasks to agents, specify information flows between agents, and choose how much information to retain (retention ratios). A deterministic simulator then executes this plan without running actual worker agents, computing interpretable metrics of result quality, makespan (latency), and token cost.

Result: Simulated scores from OrchBench show strong correlation with real execution quality scores from Claude Code (Pearson r = 0.816), while using only 1.3% of the tokens and 10.3% of the wall-clock time required for real runs. Experiments across different planners and workflow scales reveal that maintaining task-critical information flow is more beneficial than merely adding more agents, and that excessive parallelism can hurt due to rising coordination failures.

Conclusion: OrchBench provides an efficient, low-cost, and interpretable way to benchmark and diagnose multi-agent orchestration strategies in isolation from worker behavior. Its strong correlation with real execution outcomes and its ability to surface trade-offs between information preservation, parallelism, and coordination establish it as a useful tool for comparing and improving multi-agent orchestration plans.

Abstract: Complex tasks often decompose into parallelizable yet interdependent subtasks, making orchestration critical to the performance of multi-agent systems (MAS). Existing evaluations typically rely on end-to-end execution, which conflates orchestration-plan quality with worker capabilities, tool reliability, and environmental noise. Moreover, the time and token costs of real execution grow rapidly with workflow scale, making systematic evaluation expensive. We present OrchBench, a simulation-based benchmark for evaluating multi-agent orchestration plans in isolation. Starting from real-world tasks, OrchBench constructs directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) that encode task dependencies, with controlled sizes and degrees of parallelism. Given a DAG, a per-agent context limit, and an agent budget, the evaluated planner assigns subtasks to agents and specifies cross-agent information transfers and their retention ratios. A deterministic simulator evaluates the resulting plan without invoking worker agents and returns interpretable measures of result quality, makespan, and token cost. The simulated scores produced by OrchBench correlate strongly with quality scores from Claude Code executions, achieving a Pearson correlation of \(r=0.816\), while requiring only \(1.3\%\) of the tokens and \(10.3\%\) of the wall-clock time. Across diverse planners and workflow scales, we find that preserving task-critical information is more important than simply increasing the number of agents, and the benefits of parallelism diminish as coordination failures accumulate. These results establish OrchBench as an efficient and interpretable benchmark for comparing and diagnosing multi-agent orchestration plans.

</details>


### [134] [CoRT: Counterfactual Replay for Token-Level Rubric-Guided Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25659)
*Bo-Wen Zhang,Junwei He,Wen Wang,Song-Lin Lv,Wentao Ma,Rongyi Lin,Shuhan Zhong,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CoRT, a token-level credit allocation method for rubric-based reinforcement learning in language models, which improves performance over standard response-level GRPO without needing an auxiliary token scorer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In rubric-based reinforcement learning for language models, structured criteria are collapsed into a single scalar reward per response, and the resulting advantage is broadcast uniformly to all tokens. This ignores that different rubric criteria often depend on specific spans, formatting, or semantic choices, leading to poor within-response credit assignment and potentially suboptimal learning. The authors aim to exploit rubric structure more effectively without adding complex auxiliary models.

Method: The authors propose CoRT, a token-level credit weighting scheme compatible with rubric-conditioned GRPO. For each sampled response, they perform counterfactual replay by rescoring that same response under two prompts: the original rubric-conditioned prompt and a matched prompt without rubric criteria. They compute tokenwise log-likelihood contrasts between these two scoring passes as a proxy for each token’s dependence on the rubric context. These contrasts are then mapped into bounded, response-normalized token weights, which are used to redistribute the signed GRPO advantage across tokens. This preserves the original response-level reward and avoids training any separate token-scoring or relevance model.

Result: Across multiple instruction-tuned language models and different levels of reward granularity, CoRT consistently outperforms standard response-level GRPO in most experimental comparisons, with an average performance increase of 4.4 percentage points. It also remains competitive with alternative methods that explicitly learn token-level credit assignment, despite not requiring a separate relevance-learning stage.

Conclusion: Policy-internal counterfactual likelihood contrasts—obtained by rescoring responses with and without rubric conditioning—provide an effective, lightweight signal for within-response credit allocation. CoRT leverages this idea to improve rubric-based RL training for language models while maintaining the simplicity and stability of GRPO and avoiding the overhead of auxiliary token-level scorers.

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning enriches language model training by evaluating model outputs against explicit criteria. Yet in GRPO-style pipelines, these structured judgments are reduced to a scalar response-level reward and converted into a response-level advantage, which is broadcast uniformly to all generated tokens. This leaves no explicit mechanism for allocating credit within a response, even when different criteria are grounded in different spans, formatting decisions, or semantic choices. We propose CoRT, a token-level credit weighting method for rubric-conditioned GRPO. Instead of training an auxiliary token scoring model, CoRT uses counterfactual replay to rescore the same sampled response under the original rubric-conditioned prompt and a matched criteria-free prompt. The resulting tokenwise log-likelihood contrasts serve as a proxy for dependence on the rubric context. CoRT maps these contrasts to bounded, response-normalized weights and uses them to redistribute the signed GRPO advantage across tokens, without introducing an auxiliary scorer or changing the response-level reward. Experiments across instruction-tuned models and reward granularities show that CoRT improves over matched response-level GRPO in the vast majority of comparisons, with an average gain of 4.4 percentage points. The method remains competitive with learned token-level credit baselines while avoiding a separate relevance-learning stage. These results suggest that policy-internal counterfactual likelihood contrasts provide an effective training signal for within-response credit allocation while retaining the simplicity and stability of GRPO.

</details>


### [135] [OmniDelta: Skill-Driven Budget Allocation for Token Compression in OmniLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25669)
*Haoyang Huang,Wenjie Huang,Tianqi Xu,Hongyaoxing Gu,Kang Tan,Yikai Fu,Yuhao Shen,Tianyu Liu,Baolin Zhang,Jun Zhang,Xinyi Hu,Jun Dai,Shuang Ge,Lei Chen,Yue Li,Mingchen Wang,Meng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniDelta is a training-free, skill-driven token budget allocation and pruning framework for omni-modal LLMs that significantly improves the accuracy–efficiency trade-off for long audio–video inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omni-modal LLMs can process text, audio, and video jointly but suffer from very long audio–video token sequences, which cause high memory usage and slow inference. Existing token compression approaches mostly assume a fixed token budget and focus only on which tokens to keep, neglecting how to allocate that budget across and within modalities. Naive strategies like using direct query-to-audio/video similarity for inter-modal allocation or uniform per-modality token budgets can be unreliable—potentially discarding important evidence while keeping redundant content. There is a need for a training-free method that intelligently allocates limited token budgets both across modalities and within each modality to maximize task performance under tight resource constraints.

Method: The paper introduces OmniDelta, a training-free framework that optimizes token budget allocation in two stages. First, it performs intent-aware inter-modal budget allocation by constructing audio and video ‘skill pools’ that map query demands (e.g., whether a question is more audio- or video-centric) to how many tokens each modality should be allocated. Second, it conducts content-aware intra-modal allocation: given each modality’s budget, it redistributes tokens across audio segments and video frames by measuring local content complexity and temporal redundancy, assigning more tokens to complex or information-dense regions and fewer to redundant ones. These local budgets are then combined with existing pruning strategies, so that the total retained-token ratio is fixed but the spatial and temporal distribution of retained tokens is optimized without extra training.

Result: Across four audio–video benchmarks and two Qwen2.5-Omni models, OmniDelta yields a better accuracy–efficiency Pareto frontier compared with baseline pruning/compression methods. For example, on Qwen2.5-Omni-7B at a 25% overall token retention ratio, OmniDelta reduces GPU memory usage by 22.0% and delivers a 1.64× end-to-end inference speedup relative to full-token inference, while maintaining or improving task accuracy. Similar gains are observed across different pruning ratios and model scales, indicating robust benefits in both efficiency and performance.

Conclusion: OmniDelta demonstrates that intelligent, training-free token budget allocation—both across modalities (driven by query intent and skill pools) and within modalities (guided by local complexity and redundancy)—can substantially improve the efficiency of omni-modal LLMs without sacrificing accuracy. By decoupling budget allocation from token selection and remaining compatible with existing pruning methods, OmniDelta offers a general and practical approach to scaling omni-modal understanding under memory and latency constraints, setting a new Pareto frontier for accuracy versus computational cost on audio–video tasks.

Abstract: Emerging Omni-modal Large Language Models (OmniLLMs) enable unified understanding of text, audio, and video, but their long audio-video token sequences introduce substantial memory and inference costs. Existing compression methods mainly focus on selecting important tokens under fixed budgets, leaving the preceding budget-allocation problem underexplored. We show that direct query-to-audio/video similarity is unreliable for inter-modal budget allocation, and that uniform intra-modal budgets can miss key evidence while retaining redundant content.
