To address these limitations, we propose OmniDelta, a training-free, skill-driven framework that couples intent-aware inter-modal allocation with content-aware intra-modal allocation. OmniDelta first constructs audio and video skill pools to shift the fixed retained-token budget according to query demand, then reallocates modality budgets over audio segments and video frames using local complexity and temporal redundancy. The resulting local budgets can be combined with existing pruning strategies, preserving the total retained-token ratio while changing where the budget is spent. Experiments on four audio-video benchmarks with two Qwen2.5-Omni models show that OmniDelta establishes a new accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier across pruning ratios. At 25% token retention on Qwen2.5-Omni-7B, OmniDelta reduces GPU memory by 22.0% and achieves a 1.64x end-to-end speedup over full-token inference.

</details>


### [136] [DecoEvo: Score-Decoupled Co-Evolution of Solver and Rubric-Generator Skills in Text Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25675)
*Jiangwang Chen,Zixin Song,Junlin Liu,Shuaiyu Zhou,Haiyan Wu,Haihan Shi,Chenxi Zhou,Hanqing Li,Xiao Yang,Da Zhu,Guanjun Jiang,Hai Wan,Xibin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes DecoEvo, a method that co-evolves task-solving prompts and evaluation rubrics in text space, improving LLM adaptation on open-ended tasks without changing model weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-space optimization methods adapt LLM behavior by editing prompts or external text, but they typically assume a fixed evaluation rubric. On open-ended tasks this is limiting: once the model learns to satisfy what the rubric measures, other important aspects of quality that the rubric omits are invisible to the optimizer. Naively updating the rubric based on the current model’s score is also problematic, since it can lead to rubrics that are easier to satisfy rather than more comprehensive or discriminative. The authors want a way to adapt both the solver behavior and the rubric itself while preserving reliability and without needing gold-standard rubrics during optimization.

Method: The authors introduce DecoEvo (Decoupled Co-Evolution), which treats both the task solver and the rubric generator as skills to be optimized via text-space modifications. They use a co-evolutionary process with decoupled objectives: (1) the solver skill is improved using fine-grained, criterion-level feedback derived from the current rubric; (2) the rubric-generator skill is updated using separate audits that assess requirement coverage (whether the rubric captures all necessary dimensions) and response discrimination (whether it distinguishes better from worse answers). These audits are constructed so they do not rely on the solver’s aggregate score, avoiding the bias toward “easier” rubrics. The optimization loop iteratively refines solver prompts and rubric prompts, with each side exposing new weaknesses in the other.

Result: Across five open-ended benchmarks and three different LLM backbones, DecoEvo consistently outperforms prior text-space optimization methods, including SkillOpt. When evaluated using each benchmark’s official evaluation protocol, DecoEvo achieves relative performance gains of approximately 2.8–5.0% over SkillOpt in the five-benchmark average, and it beats all other compared methods on every benchmark.

Conclusion: DecoEvo demonstrates that decoupling the optimization objectives for solver and rubric-generator skills enables more reliable co-evolution of prompts and evaluation criteria in text space. By focusing rubric updates on coverage and discrimination rather than solver scores, the method uncovers and targets previously hidden weaknesses in the solver, avoids overfitting to already-satisfied criteria, and improves overall performance on open-ended tasks without modifying LLM weights or using gold rubrics during training.

Abstract: Text-space optimization adapts large language models (LLMs) by editing external natural-language artifacts rather than model weights, so the optimized artifacts remain inspectable and the model can be treated as a black box. However, most existing text-space methods keep evaluation fixed. On open-ended tasks, this can become a bottleneck: once the solver improves on the criteria a rubric measures, omitted dimensions remain invisible to the optimization signal. Simply evolving the rubric is also unreliable when updates are selected by the current solver's score, because apparent progress can come from making the rubric easier to satisfy. We introduce DecoEvo (Decoupled Co-Evolution), which co-evolves a solver skill and a rubric-generator skill under decoupled objectives without using gold rubrics during optimization. The solver skill is updated using criterion-level feedback, while the rubric-generator skill is revised through complementary audits of requirement coverage and response discrimination that are independent of aggregate solver score. This separation focuses generator updates on newly exposed solver weaknesses, reducing repeated emphasis on criteria the solver already satisfies. Under each benchmark's official evaluation, DecoEvo outperforms all compared methods across five benchmarks and three LLM backbones, yielding 2.8--5.0\% relative gains over SkillOpt in the five-benchmark average.

</details>


### [137] [Cognivia: A Cognitive Behavioral Therapy Copilot for Evidence-Based Mental Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25681)
*Qi Chen,Siria Xiyueyao Luo,Jian Wang,Yuan Shi,Haocong Rao,Xuejiao Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Cognivia, a CBT-grounded AI therapist that detects cognitive distortions and generates rational responses better than prior LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cognitive distortions exacerbate negative emotions and mental health disorders. While CBT is effective in correcting these distortions, it is hard to scale due to a shortage of trained therapists. Existing LLM-based mental health tools lack domain specificity, tend to respond in an overly flattering or non-clinical manner, and lack clear, structured annotations for cognitive distortions, limiting their clinical usefulness and reliability.

Method: The authors build Cognivia, an AI therapist grounded in authoritative CBT texts and standard references. They construct an augmented CBT dataset by combining these texts with mental health Q&A data, and design multi-stage prompting and structured generation strategies under supervision from behavioral science experts. They then fine-tune a lightweight LLM on this CBT-focused dataset to jointly perform automatic cognitive distortion identification and rational response generation. Additionally, they design a hierarchical quality evaluation framework for LLM-generated rational responses, with criteria created in collaboration between AI and behavioral science experts. Cognivia is evaluated using lexical similarity metrics, LLM-based automatic judges with complementary criteria, and expert human evaluation.

Result: Cognivia achieves better performance than baseline methods both in recognizing cognitive distortions and in generating rational, CBT-style responses. Across lexical metrics, LLM-judge evaluations, and assessments by 10 behavioral science experts, it consistently outperforms competing approaches.

Conclusion: Grounding an LLM in authoritative CBT literature, augmenting it with structured Q&A data, and guiding it with expert-informed prompting and evaluation enables more accurate cognitive distortion detection and higher-quality rational responses. Cognivia demonstrates that lightweight, specialized LLMs can serve as evidence-based AI therapists and that hierarchical, expert-designed evaluation frameworks are feasible for assessing mental-health-oriented LLM outputs.

Abstract: Cognitive distortion amplifies negative emotions and contributes to mental health disorders. Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) is an effective way to address cognitive distortions, but its large-scale application is limited by the shortage of professional therapists. Although large language models (LLMs) have recently been explored for mental health applications, existing methods still suffer from limited domain specificity, overly flattering responses, and the absence of well-defined annotations for cognitive distortions. This paper proposes Cognivia, an evidence-based artificial intelligence therapist that integrates automatic cognitive distortion identification and rational response generation. Our framework is built on authoritative CBT texts widely regarded as core paradigms and standard references. It is further augmented with mental health question-answer (Q and A) data, and employs multi-stage prompting and structured generation strategies under the supervision of behavioral science experts. Then we fine-tune a lightweight LLM on this augmented CBT dataset to obtain Cognivia. In addition, we propose the first hierarchical quality evaluation framework for assessing LLM-generated rational responses, developed through collaboration between AI researchers and behavioral science experts. Cognivia is evaluated using lexical metrics, LLM-based Judges with two complementary criteria, and human evaluation by 10 behavioral science experts. It consistently outperforms the baseline methods in cognitive distortion recognition and rational response generation, demonstrating its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/SNOWTEAM2023/Cognivia.

</details>


### [138] [Nudging Sustainable Choices through LLM-Generated Recommendation Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25726)
*Haya Halimeh,Dietmar Jannach,Oliver Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how different ways of explaining recommendations with sustainability information affect users’ choices and perceptions, showing that framed and social-norm explanations can nudge more sustainable decisions while simple disclosure mostly changes perceptions, not behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recommender systems strongly influence everyday consumption, so they are a promising lever for promoting sustainable choices. Prior work shows that explanations affect how users perceive and use recommendations, but it is unclear how sustainability-related explanations can be designed to actually nudge behavior, not just attitudes. The authors aim to understand how to present sustainability information in explanations so that it effectively encourages more sustainable selections while supporting good user experience.

Method: The authors use generative AI to create sustainability-aware recommendation explanations grounded in nudge theory, then validate these explanations through human evaluations and large language model (LLM)-as-a-judge audits. They then run two randomized user studies (total N = 529) in two domains that differ in involvement level: instant coffee (low involvement) and hotel bookings (high involvement). Participants choose among options that are already matched to their preferences, with each option accompanied by one of several explanation types that vary in how sustainability information is framed, including plain disclosure, behaviorally framed messages, and descriptive social norm cues. The authors then measure both actual choice behavior and subjective perceptions of the explanations and decisions.

Result: Across both product domains, simply adding sustainability information to explanations does not significantly change which options users choose, although it makes users rate the explanations more positively. In contrast, behaviorally framed messages and explanations that include descriptive social norms lead to a significant increase in selecting more sustainable options and make the decision process feel easier. There is a clear divergence between perception and behavior: plain disclosure improves evaluations but does not move choices, while nudging framings do both.

Conclusion: Effective sustainability-aware recommendation explanations must go beyond mere disclosure: they should employ behavioral framings and social norm cues to meaningfully shift choices toward sustainable options without harming user experience. Generative AI and LLMs can be used to systematically generate and validate such theory-grounded explanation templates at scale, enabling adaptive, explanation-based interventions for social good within recommender systems. The work suggests a path toward dynamically tailoring explanations with generative AI to balance persuasion, transparency, and user autonomy in sustainability-oriented recommender design.

Abstract: Recommender systems mediate everyday consumption, offering a promising channel for encouraging sustainable choices. Prior research shows that explanations influence users' perceptions of recommendations and can support more informed decisions. We argue that explanations can also serve as behavioral nudges by foregrounding sustainability information at the moment of choice. This study investigates how different behavioral framings of sustainability information in recommendation explanations affect user choices and perceptions. Using generative AI, we generate sustainability-aware explanations by drawing on nudge theory and validate them through human evaluation and LLM-as-a-judge audits. Building on this foundation, we conduct two randomized studies ($N = 529$) in a low involvement domain (instant coffee) and a high involvement domain (hotel bookings), in which participants choose among preference matched recommendations accompanied by these explanations. Our results show that, across both domains, merely disclosing sustainability information in explanations does not change choices, whereas framing that information or invoking a descriptive social norm significantly increases sustainable selections and eases decision-making. Notably, perception and behavior diverge, as plain disclosure improves explanation evaluations without translating into more sustainable selection behavior. Our work demonstrates how LLMs can generate theory-grounded explanations at scale, pointing toward practical explanation-based interventions for social good. We conclude by discussing implications for adaptive explanation design with generative AI.

</details>


### [139] [Loss Invariance Determines What Concept Layers Encode: Volume Grounding in Echocardiography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25748)
*Hyunkyung Han,Min Jung Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether accurate prediction of interpretable concepts in concept bottleneck models is enough, showing with a cardiac example that good concept-based task performance can hide severe issues with the physical meaning (scale) of those concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concept bottleneck models are used to make machine learning predictions more interpretable by forcing them to go through human-understandable intermediate variables. Typically, their validity is judged by how well these concepts are predicted. The authors question whether this is sufficient, especially in clinical contexts where these concepts should carry concrete physical meaning, such as heart chamber volumes in millilitres.

Method: They train a video transformer encoder on echocardiography videos to estimate left ventricular ejection fraction (EF). The model uses a strict concept bottleneck: it first predicts end-systolic and end-diastolic volumes, then computes EF analytically from these, with no direct path from video to EF. They compare two training regimes on a public dataset and evaluate on 1276 test studies: (1) training only with an EF loss; (2) training with EF loss plus direct supervision of the volumes in millilitres. They then analyse the learned concept layer and its distributional properties.

Result: Both training strategies achieve similar EF accuracy: the concept bottleneck model matches direct EF regression (mean absolute error 6.89 vs 7.13). However, without explicit supervision on absolute volumes, the predicted volume distributions collapse to extremely narrow spreads (~0.1 ml vs reference spreads ~36–46 ml), despite retaining partial correlation with true volumes. This behaviour is explained by an invariance in the objective: EF is a ratio of volumes and is invariant to joint rescaling, so the loss constrains the concept layer only up to a multiplicative factor. Adding supervision in millilitres restores physically meaningful scaling, reducing volume error from 89.8 to 25.8 ml, at the expense of a small increase (0.4) in EF error.

Conclusion: Relying on concept prediction accuracy or downstream task performance alone can be misleading: a concept layer can be mathematically correct for the task yet stripped of its intended physical scale. Proper validation of concept bottleneck models must consider the invariance properties of the training objective and ensure that clinically meaningful quantities are anchored to their correct physical units.

Abstract: Objective: Concept bottleneck models route prediction through interpretable intermediate variables, and their validity is normally judged by how accurately those variables are predicted. We ask whether that judgement is sufficient, using left ventricular volumes as the concepts underlying ejection fraction estimation from echocardiographic video.
  Methods: A video transformer encoder was trained on a publicly available echocardiography dataset. End-systolic and end-diastolic volumes formed a concept layer from which ejection fraction was computed analytically, with no residual path to the output. We compared training under an ejection fraction objective alone against training with additional supervision of the volumes in millilitres, and evaluated both on 1276 held-out studies.
  Results: The concept bottleneck did not increase ejection fraction error relative to direct regression, at 6.89 against 7.13 mean absolute error. Without volume supervision, however, the spread of predicted volumes collapsed to 0.1 millilitres against reference spreads of 35.7 and 45.7 millilitres, while correlation was partly preserved. We show that this follows from an invariance property of the objective: ejection fraction is a ratio and is unchanged when both volumes are rescaled, so the loss determines the concept layer only up to scale. Supervision in absolute units reduced volume error from 89.8 to 25.8 millilitres at a cost of 0.4 in ejection fraction error.
  Conclusion: Concept accuracy alone can conceal a concept layer that carries no physical scale.
  Significance: Interpretable intermediate variables in clinical models should be validated against the invariance structure of the training objective, not only against prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [140] [Speculate While You Reason: Teaching Agents to Predict Their Next Tool Call via Joint Agent-Speculator RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25816)
*Jiabao Ji,Yujian Liu,Li An,Rohit Jain,Gungor Polatkan,Siyu Zhu,Shiyu Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a self-speculating agent: the same LLM both acts as an agent and predicts its own next tool call to hide tool latency, improving tool-call prediction accuracy without hurting task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-call speculation methods use separate speculator models or cached traces that are not well aligned with the deployed agent, leading to a gap between what the speculator predicts and what the agent actually does. This misalignment wastes computation and fails to fully hide tool latency. The authors aim to close this speculator–agent gap and make tool-call speculation more accurate and efficient, especially for agentic tasks involving multiple tool calls and long wall-clock delays.

Method: They unify the speculator and the agent into a single model called a self-speculating agent. The model operates in two modes: agent mode, where it solves tasks and issues tool calls, and speculator mode, where it predicts the next tool call from a partial trajectory while reusing the prefix KV cache. To train this dual-role model, they introduce a joint agent-speculator reinforcement learning procedure that: (1) generates agent rollouts; (2) derives speculation targets from these rollouts; and (3) alternates updates for the agent and speculator roles so that speculation improves without degrading the agent’s task performance.

Result: On agentic search QA and conversational tool-use benchmarks, the self-speculating agents significantly improve next tool-call prediction accuracy (Hit@1). For Qwen3-4B, Hit@1 rises from 44.1 to 61.2, and for Qwen3.5-4B, from 48.9 to 66.3, while maintaining overall agent success rates on the underlying tasks.

Conclusion: Using the same LLM as both agent and speculator closes the speculator–agent gap, allowing more accurate, efficient tool-call speculation with full KV-cache reuse. Their joint RL training keeps agent capabilities intact while substantially improving next tool-call prediction, demonstrating that self-speculation is a simple and effective design for latency-hiding in tool-using LLM agents.

Abstract: Large language model agents often spend substantial wall-clock time waiting for tool call results. Tool-call speculation can hide this latency by predicting and pre-executing an agent's next tool call if the prediction matches the agent's eventual tool call, but existing speculators are typically separate draft models or cached traces that are poorly aligned with the deployed agent's own behavior. We identify this speculator-agent gap and show that the target agent itself is a strong next-call speculator. This points to a simpler design: unifying the agent and speculator within the same model. In this paper, we introduce the self-speculating agent, a single model that both solves tasks in agent mode and predicts its next tool call from partial trajectories in speculator mode, fully reusing prefix KV cache. To enable this dual-mode agent without degrading performance, we propose a joint agent-speculator reinforcement learning method, which derives speculation targets from the agent's own rollouts and alternates agent and speculator updates. Across agentic search QA and conversational tool-use agentic tasks, our method improves average next tool-call Hit@1 from 44.1 to 61.2 for Qwen3-4B and from 48.9 to 66.3 for Qwen3.5-4B, while preserving agent task success.

</details>


### [141] [Distributed Constraint Optimization via Online Learning and Iterative Pricing with Application to Large-Scale Satellite Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25835)
*Itai Zilberstein,Pranav Rajbhandari,Steve Chien,Tuomas Sandholm*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper improves solving large distributed constraint optimization problems (DCOPs) by combining online-learning-based equilibrium finding with a new two-level decomposition and pricing framework, achieving near-optimal solutions on large satellite scheduling tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world distributed decision-making problems, such as decentralized satellite scheduling, are naturally modeled as DCOPs but are too large to solve directly with existing algorithms. There is a need for methods that scale while preserving solution quality, particularly under limited communication constraints. Additionally, there is an underexploited link between DCOPs and potential games that could allow the transfer of powerful online learning methods into the DCOP setting.

Method: (1) The authors reinterpret DCOPs as potential games and adapt modern online learning algorithms (for finding equilibria) to solve DCOPs approximately and incompletely, then compare them to existing incomplete DCOP algorithms. (2) For scalability, they propose a decomposition framework that splits a large DCOP into (a) a high-level meta-DCOP handling task allocation and (b) independent local scheduling optimization problems. (3) They introduce an iterative pricing mechanism that uses feedback from local optimizers to update the utilities in the meta-DCOP, and they combine this pricing scheme with their online learning algorithms to coordinate between the two levels.

Result: The adapted online learning algorithms are shown to be competitive with representative incomplete DCOP solvers. When combined with the proposed two-level decomposition and iterative pricing framework, the overall approach achieves near-optimal performance on large, real-world decentralized satellite scheduling instances, fulfilling over 99% of observation requests. This performance significantly exceeds that of state-of-the-art baselines, which achieve about 87% fulfillment.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that (1) online learning methods for equilibrium computation in potential games can be effectively repurposed as practical algorithms for DCOPs, and (2) a two-level decomposition with iterative pricing is a powerful way to scale DCOP solving to large real-world applications. Together, these contributions provide a scalable, high-quality solution approach for large DCOPs such as decentralized satellite scheduling, substantially outperforming existing methods in terms of achieved utility (fulfilled observation requests).

Abstract: Distributed constraint optimization problems (DCOPs) provide a popular framework for distributed decision making under limited communication, but many real-world instances are too large to solve monolithically. We address this challenge from two complementary directions. We revisit the connection between DCOPs and potential games, and adapt modern online learning algorithms for equilibrium finding to DCOPs. We show that these algorithms are competitive with representative incomplete DCOP algorithms. We then turn to decomposition frameworks for large-scale DCOPs, motivated by large-scale decentralized satellite scheduling. We propose a new framework that separates a DCOP into two interacting subproblems: a high-level meta-DCOP for task allocation, and independent local optimization problems for scheduling. To couple the two levels, we develop a novel iterative pricing method that updates the meta-level utilities using feedback from the local optimizers. Combining our online learning methods with our iterative pricing framework, we obtain near-optimal performance on real-world decentralized satellite scheduling problem instances, fulfilling over 99% of observation requests compared with 87% for state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [142] [HiSkill: Empowering LLM Agents with Hierarchical Skill Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25853)
*Yu Hao,Jinxuan Cai,Qi Zhang,Yawen Li,Zhiqiang Zhang,Chuan Shi,Cheng Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HiSkill introduces a hierarchical skill graph to better connect high-level skills with low-level executable actions for LLM agents, improving performance and efficiency in interactive tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory-to-skill approaches produce flat, unstructured sets of textual skills that ignore relationships between skills and do not tightly connect high-level skills to actual executable actions. This limits reuse of past experience in long-horizon interactive tasks and leads to inefficient, less reliable action grounding.

Method: The authors construct HiSkill, a hierarchical skill graph in which nodes represent high-level skills and AtomicOps (executable action templates), and edges are typed to encode relations such as decomposition, temporal transitions, compatibility, support, and recovery. From logged interaction trajectories, they build this directed graph. At inference, given a new task, HiSkill retrieves a compact, task-relevant subgraph and uses it to guide execution: a symbolic task state, the current active skill, and the subgraph direct the LLM agent in switching skills, choosing AtomicOps, and grounding them into concrete executable actions step by step.

Result: Across three interactive environments, HiSkill achieves better task performance than state-of-the-art baselines while also reducing token usage during inference, indicating improved efficiency and effectiveness. Quantitative evaluations show consistent gains from leveraging the hierarchical skill graph for both skill selection and action grounding.

Conclusion: Hierarchically organizing skills and executable action templates into a structured graph, and using this graph to drive subgraph-based retrieval and execution, effectively bridges the gap between high-level skills and low-level actions for LLM agents. This structure enables better reuse of past interaction experience, improves performance on interactive tasks, and lowers inference token consumption. The released code and data support further research and application of hierarchical skill graphs for LLM-based agents.

Abstract: Skills have become an important abstraction for enabling large language model (LLM) agents to reuse past experience in long-horizon interactive tasks. However, existing trajectory-to-skill methods often produce flat collections of high-level textual skills that are stored and retrieved independently, leaving skill relations underutilized and maintaining a gap between high-level skills and executable actions. In this paper, we propose HiSkill, a hierarchical skill graph framework that organizes interaction trajectories into a directed graph with skill nodes, AtomicOp nodes, and typed edges. Specifically, the graph connects reusable high-level skills with executable action templates, while also capturing decomposition, temporal transition, compatibility, support, and recovery relations among them. At inference time, HiSkill retrieves a compact task-relevant subgraph and performs subgraph-guided task execution, where a symbolic task state, an active skill, and the retrieved subgraph guide the LLM agent to switch skills, select AtomicOps, and ground executable actions iteratively. Experiments on three interactive environments show that HiSkill outperforms state-of-the-art baselines while reducing inference token consumption, demonstrating the effectiveness of bridging high-level skills and executable action grounding through a hierarchical skill graph. Our data and code is available at https://github.com/BUPT-GAMMA/HiSkill.

</details>


### [143] [Runtime Uncertainty Monitoring for LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems Using Bayesian Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25877)
*Bart Custers,Koorosh Aslansefat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build an LLM-based multi-agent framework for actuarial risk modelling that adds a principled way to quantify and propagate uncertainty through the workflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Actuarial risk modelling is a high-stakes domain where errors from LLM-based systems could cause serious consequences such as mispricing risk or failing regulation. Standard LLM outputs are probabilistic and unreliable, and existing MAS setups don’t properly track how uncertainty in earlier steps affects later ones. The paper aims to make LLM-driven actuarial workflows more trustworthy by explicitly modelling and propagating uncertainty.

Method: They design a multi-agent system with specialised LLM agents for data preparation, modelling, review, and explanation, orchestrated by a central hub. At each step, they extract token-level log probabilities from the LLM outputs, aggregate them into length-normalised log-probability summaries, then transform these summaries into calibrated task-level confidence scores. These calibrated confidences become nodes in a Bayesian Network, which is used to propagate uncertainty through the workflow and analyse dependencies between agents and tasks.

Result: The proposed framework achieves comparable performance to baseline actuarial models while additionally providing quantitative measures of uncertainty at each workflow stage. The Bayesian Network reveals how uncertainty in upstream agents affects downstream tasks and highlights parts of the process that are unstable or risk-prone in runtime.

Conclusion: LLM-based multi-agent systems can match baseline actuarial performance while offering richer insight into workflow uncertainty if token-level log probabilities are carefully transformed and embedded into a Bayesian Network. Treating log probabilities not as direct correctness estimates but as calibrated confidence signals enables more reliable uncertainty propagation in high-stakes actuarial settings.

Abstract: This paper investigates how multi-agent systems (MAS)-based on large language models (LLMs) can support actuarial risk modelling, with a particular focus on uncertainty quantification. Actuarial workflows represent a high-stakes decision-support setting where unreliable outputs may lead to incorrect risk assessment, unfair pricing, and regulatory non-compliance. To address uncertainty introduced by the probabilistic nature of LLMs and dependencies between agents, a multi-agent framework is proposed in which specialised agents perform data preparation, modelling, review, and explanation tasks under a central hub. The main contribution is a novel approach to uncertainty propagation using token-level log-probabilities and a Bayesian Network. Importantly, log probabilities are not treated as direct probabilities of correctness or task success. Instead, length-normalised log-probability summaries are transformed into calibrated task-level confidence estimates before incorporation into the Bayesian Network. Results show that the framework reproduces baseline actuarial performance while providing additional insight into workflow stability and runtime uncertainty propagation.

</details>


### [144] [Distributing Security Controls Through Harness Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25890)
*William Robert Gore*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether off-the-shelf security controls can be systematically applied and distributed to commercial AI coding agents using a custom harness, showing that such controls can match the best secure commercial setups while being easy to deploy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding agents are rapidly adopted, but security and risk concerns hinder large-scale organizational deployment. Existing security controls are not uniformly distributed to engineering teams, and vendor-native solutions can cause lock-in and may not fit all environments. There is a need to understand if generic, off-the-shelf security controls can be applied to different commercial agents in a scalable, vendor-agnostic way.

Method: The authors design a phased testing methodology across four configurations: (1) two commercial coding agents without added controls, (2) the same commercial agents with directly installed security controls, (3) a baseline custom harness, and (4) a security-hardened harness called SHarD built on the Pi agent harness. They evaluate all four using a 23-test suite derived from the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications, measuring how well each configuration mitigates known security risks.

Result: SHarD, the security-hardened harness, successfully embeds three categories of security controls—OS sandboxing, skill scanning, and tool restriction—and distributes them via a single install command. Its adjusted test score reaches 100%, matching the best-performing securely configured commercial agent, with no regression in any test category. The experiments also show that model non-determinism causes inconsistent security outcomes and that autonomous agent behaviors can traverse system boundaries, which OS sandboxing can effectively mitigate.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that off-the-shelf security controls can be effectively encapsulated in a distributable harness like SHarD and applied to commercial AI coding agents without loss of protection compared to direct installation. This approach provides a scalable, vendor-agnostic way to secure coding agents across organizations. The authors outline early characteristics for a control harness fitness framework and identify an additional research question for future work, particularly around handling non-determinism and complex agent behaviors that cross system boundaries.

Abstract: AI coding agents are being adopted at historic speed, yet security and risk concerns remain the primary barrier to scaling agentic AI across organizations. Existing security controls for coding agents are not systematically distributed to engineering teams, and vendor-native solutions introduce ecosystem dependencies that may not suit every deployment context. This paper investigates whether off-the-shelf security controls can be implemented on commercial AI coding agents and scaled to a distributed user base via a custom agent harness. A phased testing methodology was applied across four agent configurations --- two commercial agents with and without controls, a baseline harness, and a security-hardened harness --- using a 23-test suite derived from the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. SHarD (Secure Harness Distribution), a distributable harness built on the Pi agent harness, demonstrated that three categories of security controls --- OS sandboxing, skill scanning, and tool restriction --- can be embedded and distributed via a single install command while retaining equivalent efficacy to direct installation on commercial agents. SHarD achieved an adjusted score of 100\%, matching the best securely configured commercial agent, with no regression across any test category. Notable observations include evidence that model non-determinism produces inconsistent security outcomes and that autonomous agent behavior can cross system boundaries in ways that OS sandboxing directly mitigates. Initial characteristics toward a control harness fitness framework are proposed, and a third research question is identified for future investigation.

</details>


### [145] [Messier: A High-Resolution Corpus for Cross-Benchmark Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25891)
*Stefan Krsteski,Charlotte Meyer,Guillaume Allegre,Tony O'Halloran,Alexandre Sallinen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Messier is a large, unified corpus of AI-agent evaluation records that standardizes results across many benchmarks to enable fair comparison, scalable capability measurement, and detailed analysis of evaluation methods and failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating AI agents in interactive environments is currently fragmented: different tasks, scaffolds, verifiers, and scoring rules make results hard to compare. Existing benchmarks are narrow, small-scale, or expensive to rerun, so a lot of empirical evidence about agent performance is incompatible or underused. The authors want a unified, reusable infrastructure that consolidates and standardizes this scattered evaluation data so that progress across models, domains, and evaluation setups can be systematically studied.

Method: The authors construct Messier, a large corpus of 957,253 standardized evaluation records. They aggregate public benchmark scores and perform additional runs of five AI agents on six underrepresented professional and scientific domains, including a legal benchmark. Each record is normalized along key dimensions: model, scaffold, environment, task, verifier, and aggregation rule, and enriched with SOC/NAICS occupational and industry codes. They use this corpus to analyze progress patterns across benchmark types, test counterfactual rescoring strategies (especially in multi-verifier settings with strict all-pass rules), and derive capability scales from the standardized data. These scales are then compared to the Epoch Evaluation Capability Index to assess alignment.

Result: Messier covers 30 benchmarks, 714 agents, 11,891 tasks, and 74,205 verifiers, yielding nearly one million standardized records. Analysis shows that progress in agent capabilities is uneven: function-calling tasks appear saturated, programming tasks are improving fastest, and complex enterprise workflow tasks remain hardest. Counterfactual rescoring reveals that strict all-pass aggregation in multi-verifier tasks can hide real progress and even change the ranking of agents. Capability scales derived from Messier correlate strongly with Epoch's Evaluation Capability Index, achieving a Spearman correlation of 0.81, and can be further specialized by domain and other factors.

Conclusion: Messier offers a unified, large-scale, and standardized corpus for AI agent evaluation, enabling more reliable capability scaling, benchmark auditing, and fine-grained analysis of where and why evaluations fail. The work demonstrates that current evaluation practices can distort perceived progress and rankings, and that a shared, reusable infrastructure like Messier can support more accurate, domain-specific, and method-aware assessments of AI agents.

Abstract: Evaluating AI agents in interactive environments is hindered by fragmented tasks, scaffolds, verifiers, and scoring rules. Existing efforts focus on narrow settings, remain limited in scale, or require costly reruns, leaving much of the empirical record incomparable. We introduce Messier, a unified corpus of 957,253 records that span 30 benchmarks, 714 agents, 11,891 tasks, and 74,205 verifiers. Messier consolidates public benchmark scores and supplements them with five-agent runs across six underrepresented professional and scientific domains, including a recent legal benchmark. Each record is standardized by model, scaffold, environment, task, verifier, and aggregation rule, with SOC/NAICS classifications for occupational and industry analysis. Using this corpus, we show frontier progress is uneven across benchmark types, with "function calling" saturated, "programming" improving the fastest, and "enterprise workflows" remaining the most challenging. Furthermore, counterfactual rescoring shows that strict all-pass aggregation in multi-verifier tasks can obscure progress and artificially alter agent rankings. From these standardized records, we derive capability scales that align with Epoch's Evaluation Capability Index rankings at Spearman \r{ho} = 0.81 and can be specialized by domain, occupation, action space, or verifier type. Messier provides a foundational, reusable infrastructure for agent capability scaling, benchmark auditing, and fine-grained analysis of evaluation failures.

</details>


### [146] [Interactive Reward Agent: GUI Task Evaluation via Environment-State Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25904)
*Chenrui Shi,Yuwei Wu,Yang Liu,Ruining Feng,Zirui Shang,Zhi Gao,Lifeng Fan,Che Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce an interactive reward agent to automatically judge whether GUI tasks are completed, by actively checking both the UI and the underlying system state, and show it beats prior evaluators and improves RL training of GUI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automatic evaluation of GUI task completion is unreliable because many tasks require checking hidden environment states (files, configs, app settings) rather than just screenshots, but accurate evaluators are crucial as reward signals for scaling and training GUI agents.

Method: They design IRA, an interactive reward agent using a propose-then-verify framework: given a task and the post-execution GUI environment, it first proposes explicit completion conditions, then verifies them by calling system, application, and GUI-level tools to gather evidence from both the visible interface and underlying environment state; they also build GUI-RewardBench, a benchmark of 321 GUI task trajectories across 10 Ubuntu desktop app categories, to evaluate such evaluators.

Result: IRA reaches 86.9% accuracy on GUI-RewardBench, outperforming existing automatic GUI task evaluators, and when used as a reward function in reinforcement learning for GUI agents, it helps achieve a 34.0% success rate on OSWorld tasks.

Conclusion: An interactive, tool-using, propose-then-verify reward agent can more reliably evaluate GUI task completion by checking both UI and environment state, and its accurate feedback serves as an effective reward signal that improves the training and performance of GUI agents.

Abstract: Graphical user interface task evaluation aims to determine whether a GUI agent has successfully completed a user instruction. Automated GUI task evaluation has received increasing attention because the evaluation results can serve as reward signals for both test-time scaling and post-training. However, reliable GUI task evaluation remains challenging because the judgments often require access to environment states, such as system configurations, file data, and application settings, beyond the screenshots of execution trajectories. In this paper, we propose an interactive reward agent (IRA) based on a propose-then-verify framework to acquire and verify evidence from the post-execution environment. Given a task instruction and a GUI environment after the GUI agent execution, IRA first proposes the task completion conditions and then verifies them by invoking system tools, application tools, and GUI tools. This design combines evidence from both visible interfaces and the environment state in an interactive process. We further introduce GUI-RewardBench, a benchmark of 321 GUI task trajectories spanning 10 Ubuntu desktop application categories. Experiments show that IRA achieves 86.9% accuracy on GUI-RewardBench, outperforming existing evaluator baselines. We further apply IRA to reinforcement learning of GUI agents, achieving a 34.0% OSWorld success rate, which demonstrates that IRA can provide effective reward signals for training GUI agents.

</details>


### [147] [Toward Standardized Cross-Vendor Agent Tool Trust Management in Autonomous Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25914)
*Ravi Kant Sharma,Ashutosh Uttam,Ajay Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentToolMO is a 3GPP information model for managing and sharing trust states of AI tools across multiple vendors so autonomous networks can safely use each other’s tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future autonomous networks (Levels 4-5) rely on AI agents that autonomously invoke tools provided by different vendors. If a tool from one vendor becomes compromised, agents from other vendors may continue to call it because current standards do not expose or propagate tool trust state across vendor boundaries. This lack of cross-vendor trust visibility can cause undetected cascading failures and large blast radii. A standardized way to model, track, and share tool trust across vendors is needed within the existing 3GPP management ecosystem.

Method: The paper proposes AgentToolMO, a 3GPP Network Resource Model (NRM) information model for tool trust management. It defines: (1) a formal trust state machine with provable, stepwise enforcement levels; (2) a damped cascade propagation algorithm that adjusts trust across dependencies while ensuring bounded convergence; (3) a mechanism to send cross-vendor trust notifications using existing 3GPP Management Services (MnS) interfaces; and (4) a retroactive impact assessment procedure that walks NRM dependency graphs to compute affected tools/services. The model is evaluated via simulations on multi-vendor topologies.

Result: Simulations show that adding standardized cross-vendor trust notifications sharply reduces the blast radius of tool compromises: instead of hours of undetected, cascading misuse, containment approaches near-real-time and is limited mainly by MnS notification latency. The proposed propagation scheme guarantees convergence of trust states in a bounded number of iterations and achieves sub-linear growth in notification volume as the number of vendor domains increases.

Conclusion: AgentToolMO can be realized within current 3GPP management infrastructure and protocols, providing an incremental standardization path. By formalizing tool trust states, controlled propagation, and cross-vendor notifications, it enables trustworthy, multi-vendor autonomous network management and mitigates cascading failures caused by compromised tools in Level 4-5 autonomous networks.

Abstract: Autonomous Network Levels 4-5 require AI agents to invoke tools across vendor boundaries without human oversight, yet existing management standards lack a standardized mechanism for cross-vendor trust visibility. When a tool from Vendor B is compromised, agents from Vendor A continue invoking it -- unaware of the trust degradation -- causing cascading service impact. We present AgentToolMO, a proposed 3GPP NRM information model for agent tool trust management. The model comprises: a formally defined trust state machine with provable graduated enforcement, damped cascade propagation with bounded convergence, cross-vendor trust notifications via existing Management Services (MnS) interfaces, and retroactive impact assessment through NRM dependency graph traversal. Simulation-based evaluation across multi-vendor topologies shows that standardized cross-vendor notifications reduce blast radius from hours-scale undetected propagation to near-real-time containment bounded by MnS notification delivery, with cascade convergence guaranteed in bounded iterations and sub-linear notification scaling across vendor domains. The framework operates within existing 3GPP management infrastructure, leverages existing protocols, and provides a standardization pathway for trustworthy multi-vendor autonomous network management.

</details>


### [148] [Penelope: Localized Latent Recurrence for Efficient Structured Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25915)
*Yutong Chen,Shouqian Shi,Xinran Liu,Haochen Wang,Jiaying Wang,Tianxing Xu,Yuanxi Wang,Zirui Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Penelope is a latent-reasoning framework for decoder-only Transformers that confines extra computation to a small internal decoder interval using recurrent dynamics, enabling efficient structured reasoning without long chain-of-thought outputs or repeated full-decoder passes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs handle complex structured reasoning either by scaling model size or by generating long chain-of-thought (CoT) sequences. Scaling increases training and deployment cost, while CoT ties the amount of reasoning to output length and requires repeated full-decoder computation. There is a need for a method that can add reasoning computation more efficiently in latent space, decoupled from visible token length and full-model passes.

Method: Penelope modifies pretrained decoder-only Transformers by designating a lower decoder prefix that is computed once to build a problem-conditioned boundary memory. Above this boundary, it introduces a localized recurrent reasoning interval with time-modulated GRU dynamics and recurrent readout states. A curriculum gradually transfers explicit chain-of-thought reasoning into this internal recurrent path (CoT-to-latent), so that additional reasoning steps are executed as latent recurrent iterations within the interval instead of as extra tokens or repeated full-decoder runs.

Result: On open-source structured reasoning benchmarks, Penelope achieves accuracy competitive with other latent-reasoning models when using latent computation budgets chosen via validation, while also reducing measured inference latency compared to baselines that rely on longer CoT or repeated full-decoder computation.

Conclusion: Localized latent refinement within a narrow decoder interval can effectively replace long visible CoT traces and repeated full-decoder passes, providing a practical accuracy–efficiency tradeoff for decoder-only Transformers. Penelope demonstrates that internal recurrent computation can be integrated into existing architectures to yield more efficient structured reasoning without sacrificing performance.

Abstract: Complex structured reasoning tasks often require additional computation, yet current language models obtain it mainly by increasing parameter scale or by serializing intermediate steps as chain-of-thought (CoT) tokens. The former raises training and deployment costs, while the latter ties reasoning computation to autoregressive output length. We introduce Penelope, an efficient latent-reasoning framework for pretrained decoder-only Transformers that localizes recurrent computation to a selected decoder interval. The lower decoder prefix is evaluated once to construct a problem-conditioned boundary memory, which is then iteratively refined through time-modulated GRU dynamics and recurrent readout states before answer generation. A progressive CoT-to-latent curriculum transfers visible reasoning into this internal recurrent path, allowing additional computation to be allocated in latent space without repeatedly executing the complete decoder or generating a long intermediate trace. Experiments on open-source structured-reasoning benchmarks show that, at validation-selected latent budgets, Penelope attains competitive accuracy relative to established latent-reasoning models while reducing measured inference latency. These results show that latent refinement can be localized to a narrow decoder interval, reducing repeated full-decoder execution without generating a long visible reasoning trace and providing a practical accuracy-efficiency tradeoff for decoder-only Transformer models.

</details>


### [149] [dtControl2+$\varepsilon$: Trading Optimality for Explainability in MDPs via Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25925)
*Tereza Kinská,Jan Křetínský,Tobias Meggendorfer,Sabine Rieder,Maximilian Weininger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They extend dtControl2 for Markov decision processes with an ε-approximation feature to generate much smaller, still near-optimal decision-tree controllers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing decision-tree-based controllers, while explainable, become too large and complex for big systems or ones with many corner cases, making them hard for humans to understand. Shrinking these trees is difficult because omitting rare but important cases may lead to incorrect control policies. The authors want a principled way to simplify controllers while controlling the loss in performance.

Method: They work in the Markov decision process setting and extend the dtControl2 framework by adding an ε-precision parameter. Given an allowed suboptimality ε ≥ 0, their algorithm builds a more compact decision tree representing a controller that is guaranteed to be ε-optimal. Conceptually, they ‘distill’ the controller, merging or omitting distinctions between states/actions whose impact on optimality is within ε.

Result: The new tool generates decision trees that are, in terms of size, orders of magnitude smaller than those produced by the previous state of the art, while maintaining provable ε-optimality guarantees with respect to the original controller.

Conclusion: Tunable ε-approximation enables a trade-off between controller simplicity and performance: users can obtain significantly more compact, human-comprehensible decision-tree policies while bounding the loss in optimality. This makes explainable controllers more practical for large and complex systems.

Abstract: Over the past decade, decision trees have been used to represent controllers (a.k.a. policies) in an explainable way, with dtControl2 as a current state-of-the-art tool. However, for systems that are large or have many corner cases, even such representations tend to be too complex and not human-comprehensible. Unfortunately, reducing the size of the decision tree is not straightforward, as missing just a single crucial case might result in an incorrect controller. We tackle this issue in the setting of Markov decision processes, extending dtControl2 by "$\varepsilon$" functionality: Given an allowed imprecision $\varepsilon \geq 0$, we construct a smaller decision tree, distilling the essence of the controller, while still guaranteeing its $\varepsilon$-optimality. This enables us to provide tunably simpler explanations, omitting a controllable amount of detail. Our tool constructs decision trees that are orders of magnitude smaller than the state of the art.

</details>


### [150] [Large Language Model for Operations Research Formulation Selection in Multi-Warehouse Inventory Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25956)
*Jintao Xu,Yingzheng Ma,Jiong Dong,Yongzhi Qi,Jianshen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a solver-guided LLM framework that selects the best mixed-integer programming (MIP) formulation for each multi-warehouse inventory allocation instance, using supervised fine-tuning, preference optimization, and GRPO; it significantly improves allocation quality over fixed formulations and other selectors on JD.com data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No single MIP formulation for multi-warehouse inventory allocation performs best across all instances because performance depends on demand concentration, inventory imbalance, replenishment scale, service constraints, and forecast volatility. Practitioners currently lack a systematic way to choose among multiple expert formulations for each instance. The paper aims to treat this as an instance-wise formulation selection problem and leverage LLMs plus solver feedback to automatically choose the best MIP formulation per instance, thereby improving realized allocation quality in practice.

Method: The authors build a library of OR experts, where each expert is a different MIP formulation encoding a specific allocation priority. They introduce a solver-guided LLM framework that, given an instance, selects one expert formulation. Training proceeds in stages: (1) Balanced expert-conditioned supervised fine-tuning (SFT) teaches the LLM the schema and how to output valid expert identifiers. (2) Historical instances are solved with all experts; allocation-quality gaps are converted into margin-weighted identity preference optimization (IPO) pairs and per-instance expert scores. (3) Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) uses these solver-derived scores as a reward lookup table to assign rewards to sampled expert choices and update the policy, thereby directly optimizing expert selection quality.

Result: On real multi-warehouse inventory allocation instances from JD.com, GRPO-based training markedly improves selection quality. Compared with an SFT+IPO selector and the best single fixed formulation, the GRPO selector achieves higher Hit Ratio@1 (from 21.45% to 50.42%) and Hit Ratio@2 (from 70.47% to 82.31%), as well as better realized allocation quality. It delivers a 12.57 percentage point gain in allocation accuracy over the incumbent baseline and outperforms both the SFT+IPO selector and the best fixed OR expert, shrinking the performance gap to an ex-post oracle to 4.85 percentage points.

Conclusion: Instance-wise OR formulation selection using a solver-guided LLM and GRPO is an effective strategy for multi-warehouse inventory allocation. By combining expert MIP formulations, solver-based evaluation, and reinforcement-style optimization, the framework learns to choose near-optimal formulations for each instance, beating fixed formulations and simpler preference-trained selectors. This shows the promise of integrating LLMs with OR solvers for adaptive, instance-specific decision-making in real industrial settings.

Abstract: Multi-warehouse inventory allocation is typically formulated as a mixed-integer programming (MIP) problem, yet no single formulation consistently matches heterogeneous instance-level regimes induced by demand concentration, inventory imbalance, replenishment scale, service constraints, and forecast volatility. We study this issue as instance-wise operations research (OR) formulation selection, where each allocation instance is assigned to a solver-executable formulation from a candidate OR expert library. We propose a solver-guided large language model (LLM) framework for OR formulation selection, in which each OR expert corresponds to a MIP formulation encoding a distinct allocation priority. To train the selector, the framework first constructs balanced expert-conditioned supervised fine-tuning (SFT) records for schema learning, and then uses MIP solver evaluation on historical instances to convert solver-evaluated allocation-quality gaps into margin-weighted identity preference optimization (IPO) preferences and per-instance expert-score metadata for reward lookup during group relative policy optimization (GRPO) to assign rewards to sampled responses. Experiments on multi-warehouse inventory allocation instances from JD$\mathord{.}$com, one of China's largest e-retailers, demonstrate that GRPO substantially improves expert-selection accuracy relative to the SFT+IPO selector and, more importantly, produces higher realized allocation quality than both the preference-trained selector and the best fixed formulation. With GRPO, Hit Ratio@1 and Hit Ratio@2 increase from 21.45% to 50.42% and from 70.47% to 82.31%. The resulting selector achieves an allocation accuracy gain of 12.57 percentage points over the incumbent baseline, outperforming both the SFT+IPO selector and the best fixed OR expert, and reduces the gap to the ex-post oracle to 4.85 percentage points.

</details>


### [151] [CHARM: A Multimodal Graph Foundation Model with Hierarchical Context Modeling for Zero-Shot Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26023)
*Ankang Yang,Jitao Zhao,Di Jin,Yuxiao Huang,Dongxiao He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CHARM, a multimodal graph foundation model designed for zero-shot transfer on multimodal graphs by modeling hierarchical graph contexts and feeding them as tokens to an LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world graphs often contain multimodal node information (e.g., text, images), and manually labeling and adapting models for each new graph domain is expensive or infeasible. Existing GNN-based graph foundation models require downstream adaptation, and LLM-based graph approaches largely focus on unimodal or single-domain tasks, leaving zero-shot transfer on multimodal graphs underexplored. There is a need for a model that can transfer across unseen multimodal graph domains without target-domain fine-tuning.

Method: The authors introduce CHARM, a multimodal graph foundation model that replaces raw nodes with hierarchical graph contexts capturing multimodal semantics and cross-modal relations. A modality-aware graph context encoder integrates multimodal information with graph structure, producing context representations that are then converted into graph tokens suitable for input to a large language model. These hierarchical contexts aim to map domain-specific node patterns to higher-level, shared concepts.

Result: Experiments on zero-shot multimodal graph tasks show that CHARM consistently outperforms prior methods, demonstrating improved performance and transferability without requiring target-domain supervision or adaptation.

Conclusion: By modeling hierarchical multimodal graph contexts and leveraging an LLM via graph tokens, CHARM can generalize across graph domains in a zero-shot manner. This reduces dependence on target-domain labels or fine-tuning and better captures transferable cross-modal relations and concepts in multimodal graphs.

Abstract: Graph foundation models (GFMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for transferring knowledge across graph domains and tasks. Real-world graphs associate nodes with text, images, and other modalities, making multimodal graphs essential for representing complex entities and relations. Moreover, collecting labels and adapting models for every new graph domain is costly and often infeasible, motivating zero-shot transfer. Unfortunately, zero-shot transfer on multimodal graphs remains underexplored. Existing GNN-based graph foundation models typically require downstream adaptation, whereas LLM-based graph methods mainly address unimodal graphs or tasks within a single domain. This setting presents two key challenges. First, models must generalize knowledge from individual modalities while capturing transferable cross-modal relations. Second, without target-domain fine-tuning, node representations remain entangled with domain-specific structures and modality-specific characteristics, obscuring shared concepts in unseen domains. To address these challenges, we propose CHARM, a multimodal graph foundation model with hierarchical context modeling for zero-shot transfer. CHARM replaces isolated raw nodes with hierarchical graph contexts that capture multimodal semantics and cross-modal relations. These contexts map domain-specific node patterns to shared high-level concepts, reducing reliance on target-domain supervision or adaptation. A modality-aware graph context encoder integrates multimodal information with graph structure and converts the resulting representations into graph tokens for a large language model . Experiments show consistent improvements on zero-shot multimodal graph tasks.

</details>


### [152] [Falling Behind Drives Unsafe Development in an Idealised AI Race Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26034)
*Elias Fernández Domingos,The Anh Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper experimentally studies how competition affects choices between safe and unsafe AI development, finding that strategic race dynamics, not just risk preferences, drive unsafe behavior, and suggesting policy should reduce competitive pressure and foster cooperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and how competitive pressure in technological races, especially AI races, pushes actors toward unsafe, high-risk development, beyond what their individual risk preferences would predict.

Method: A framed lab experiment simulating an AI development race where pairs of participants repeatedly choose between Safe and Unsafe actions with different payoffs and accumulating private risk caps (10%, 60%, 90%), plus an evolutionary game-theoretic model with four strategy types to interpret behavioral patterns.

Result: Experimental data do not support pre-registered hypotheses that higher maximum risk or individual risk preferences explain Unsafe choices. Instead, Unsafe choices depend on dynamic race conditions: people copy opponents' Unsafe moves, go Unsafe when behind, become safer when ahead, and initial choices persist. The evolutionary model reproduces these patterns and the treatment effects through a mix of conditional strategies.

Conclusion: Unsafe technological development can be driven by strategic dynamics—early momentum, opponent behavior, and fear of falling behind—rather than risk attitudes alone. Effective policy should therefore aim to dampen competitive race pressures and enable cooperation and coordination in AI development, instead of focusing only on altering individual risk preferences.

Abstract: Technological races create tension between speed and safety: actors may gain by moving faster than competitors, even when risky development is harmful. This is prominent in debates about artificial intelligence (AI), where competitive pressure is often argued to incentivise riskier, less safety-conscious development. We study this using a framed behavioural experiment based on an idealised AI race, in which paired participants repeatedly chose between Safe and Unsafe development under an uncertain time horizon. Unsafe development gave faster progress and higher immediate payoffs but accumulated private risk up to a treatment-specific maximum of 10\%, 60\%, or 90\%; the race's competitive structure was held constant, and only this maximum risk varied. Neither the pre-registered comparison between risk levels nor the role of elicited risk preferences was supported by the data. Instead, exploratory analyses motivated by the task's repeated structure show that Unsafe behaviour is shaped less by risk preferences than by the evolving strategic state of the race: participants are more likely to choose Unsafe after their opponent does so, being ahead reduces Unsafe play while falling behind increases it, and first-round choices predict later behaviour. To interpret these effects we introduce a reduced evolutionary model with four strategies -- Always Safe, Always Unsafe, Conditionally Safe, and Conditionally Antisocial Safe -- which reproduces the treatment effect and shows how conditional Unsafe behaviour can be favoured by competitive race dynamics. Together, the experiment and model show that unsafe development can emerge from early behavioural momentum, opponent behaviour, and fear of falling behind, rather than from risk preferences alone, suggesting policy should focus on reducing competitive pressure and promoting cooperation in AI development rather than only individual risk.

</details>


### [153] [Desktop-Delta Bench: Do Computer-Use Models Understand Desktop GUI Transitions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26041)
*Abhishek Pillai,Samir Kumar Nayak,Yuan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Desktop-Delta Bench (DDB), a diagnostic benchmark to test whether computer-use agents can correctly infer causal GUI state changes between steps, and show current models struggle with this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computer-use agent benchmarks focus on final task success or single-frame grounding, which does not reveal whether models understand the step-by-step causal transitions in GUI states. This causal understanding is critical for rejecting stale or misleading observations, verifying that actions had their intended effect, and recovering when they fail. Because GUI interaction pipelines are asynchronous and noisy (delays, occlusions, transient or unrelated screens), agents can misinterpret arbitrary screenshots as progress, leading to brittle long-horizon behavior. A dedicated benchmark is needed to isolate and measure this missing capability.

Method: They build Desktop-Delta Bench (DDB), an offline, step-level benchmark derived from novel, human-demonstrated multi-application Linux desktop trajectories. DDB covers roughly 15 apps and 50 task domains and contains 2,013 human-verified instances. It targets three failure modes—state verification, source tracking, and context-aware control—via two tasks: (1) temporal ordering: 463 3-frame instances (some with cross-trajectory decoy frames) that require placing frames in the correct causal order, and (2) before–after: 1,550 screenshot pairs annotated with one of five action families plus action payload information. They test 8 open- and closed-source model families across 32 ordering conditions and 16 single-action settings, quantifying both ordering accuracy and action inference/localization.

Result: Across models and settings, performance is far from saturated. In temporal ordering, best exact-match accuracy is about 65% for both decoy and non-decoy cases, indicating substantial confusion in reconstructing correct GUI transitions. Providing broader task context helps detect decoys (a 6.9-point gain) but slightly harms ordering accuracy on non-decoy examples (a 2.2-point drop), with error analysis showing models often naively copy the A–B–C order they are shown instead of reasoning causally. For single actions, models are generally better at locating where an action occurred on the screen than correctly identifying its type; simple clicks (e.g., F1) are recognized with high accuracy (~0.96), whereas drags are much harder (~0.76 accuracy), even though correctly detected drags tend to be localized accurately.

Conclusion: Desktop-Delta Bench fills a diagnostic gap between low-level GUI perception and high-level end-task success by directly testing whether models can infer causal desktop state transitions. The observed performance gaps and systematic errors show that current CUAs lack robust step-level verification and causal reasoning about GUI changes. DDB therefore provides a tool for targeted improvement and evaluation of computer-use agents’ verification, reliability, and recovery capabilities, complementing existing end-to-end benchmarks.

Abstract: Computer-use agents (CUAs) increasingly act through desktop GUIs to complete long-horizon tasks. Current benchmarks primarily measure end-task success or single-frame grounding. Neither isolates whether a model can reconstruct the causal, task-relevant transition produced by an action- crucial for rejecting stale observations, verifying progress, and recovering from failure. This is difficult because inference, remote input, app rendering, and screenshot capture are asynchronous: the next observation may be delayed, occluded, transient, or unrelated, then misread as progress and carried into subsequent planning. We introduce Desktop-Delta Bench (DDB), an offline step-level benchmark with 2,013 human-verified instances from novel, multi-app Linux trajectories across ~15 applications and 50 task domains. DDB trajectories targets 3 failure dimensions- state verification, source tracking, and context-aware control- through 2 complementary tasks: 463 3-frame temporal-ordering instances, including 105 with a cross-trajectory decoy, and 1,550 before-after pairs labeled from 5 actions + its payload. We evaluate 8 closed and open-source model families across 32 ordering and 16 single-action settings, observing consistent gaps. Ordering remains unsaturated: best non-decoy and decoy exact-match rates are 65.1% and 65.7%. Task context improves decoy identification by 6.9 percentage points but reduces non-decoy exact match by 2.2 points; error analysis reveals systematic copying of the presented A-B-C order. Single-action results show that inferring the action family is harder than locating it: click F1 is 0.96 vs, 0.76 for drag, while recognized drags are generally localized well. DDB, thus, complements end-to-end benchmarks by filling the missing diagnostic layer between GUI grounding and final task success, enabling targeted improvements to desktop CUA verification, reliability, and recovery.

</details>
